SIESTA'S COGNITIVE FEATS
By Reverie 💜 a.k.a Horny Senpai
  This document serves as a comprehensive compilation and analysis of Siesta's cognitive feats throughout The Detective Is Already Dead. Its purpose is to catalog and examine demonstrations of reasoning, deduction, observation, inference, prediction, problem-solving, strategic thinking, and information processing displayed by Siesta across the series.

Rather than focusing solely on conclusions, this document emphasizes the thought processes that allowed Siesta to reach them. Each feat is analyzed within its narrative context, highlighting the information available to her, the reasoning employed, and the significance of the outcome. Together, these feats provide a clearer understanding of Siesta's intellectual capabilities and her standing as one of the most notable detective figures within the SCD and outsmarting community.

VOLUME 1

EVENT 1: The Plane Hijacking – Four Years Ago

Context: Kimihiko Kimizuka, an eighth-grade boy with a "troublesome nature" of getting involved in incidents, is on a flight ten thousand meters above ground. A hijacker named Bat has taken over the cockpit, knocked out the co-pilot, and is threatening the pilot. Bat calls for a detective. Siesta, a mysterious girl of similar age seated next to Kimizuka, reveals herself as a detective and drags Kimizuka along as her "assistant."

Feat 1: Recognizing the Hijacker's True Motive from a Single Phrase

Siesta enters the cockpit with Kimizuka. Bat, a blond man with emerald eyes (later revealed to be an Android with modified ears), sits cross-legged on the unconscious co-pilot. He proposes a "game": if Siesta can deduce why he hijacked the plane, he will spare the passengers; if wrong, he will kill the captain.

Bat says: "I'm bored. Bored enough to come to a distant country and hijack a plane."

Siesta immediately declares: "Yes, that is the answer. You hijacked the plane because you were bored to tears."

Premises (explicit + implicit):

1. Premise 1 (Linguistic/Psychological): Bat's statement about being bored was not a joke or a rhetorical flourish—he said it directly and without contradiction.
2. Premise 2 (Behavioral Pattern): A person who prefaces a challenge with a statement of personal motivation is often revealing the true motive, especially if they phrase it casually and without apparent deception cues.
3. Premise 3 (Risk Assessment): Bat has everything to lose (his own life, the mission) if the game fails. If he were lying about boredom, he would have constructed a more plausible false motive.
4. Premise 4 (Strategic Observation): Bat's challenge is unusual—he wants her to guess, not to negotiate. This suggests the act of guessing itself serves his real goal.

Chain of Reasoning:

* Bat initiates a "game" instead of making demands.
* He volunteers a personal feeling ("bored") before stating the game's condition.
* Siesta recognizes that a truly motivated hijacker (e.g., for money, ideology, revenge) would not need to play games.
* Therefore, the stated reason (boredom) is the actual reason.

Conclusion announced immediately, without hesitation or additional questioning.

Key Dialogue:

Bat: "I'm bored. Bored enough to come to a distant country and hijack a plane."
Siesta: "Yes, that is the answer. You hijacked the plane because you were bored to tears."
Kimizuka: "...Wait a sec, Siesta. Are you serious?"
Siesta: "I am being serious, of course. Did he not say that he was bored, too bored, and wanted to hijack a plane?"

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Deductive Reasoning: A (Instantaneous deduction from a single verbal cue)
Social/Psychological Insight: A (Recognizes boredom as genuine motive, not bravado)
Risk Tolerance: S (Stakes: 600 lives. She answers without hesitation)

Feat 2: Identifying Bat's Fear of Death as the True Contradiction

After Bat admits defeat and attempts to leave, Siesta stops him. She reveals that his "boredom" answer was truthful, but another statement was a lie: his claim that he was willing to die for the hijacking.

Bat had said earlier: "I don't feel like I'm alive if I don't do this, probably." – implying suicidal commitment.

Siesta counters: "You said you will give up your life just to hijack this plane, but that is a lie. You are scared of death, aren't you?"

Premises:

1. Premise 1 (Behavioral Contradiction): Bat retreated too quickly and too easily for someone genuinely willing to die.
2. Premise 2 (Comparative Analysis): A man who hijacks a plane alone in an era of tight security would not simply give up because a teenage girl acted bravely—unless he wanted to lose.
3. Premise 3 (Incentive Inference): Bat's sigh of relief (implied) when saying he'd "probably be arrested" signals he sees prison as safety, not failure.
4. Premise 4 (Causal Logic): If he were ordered to die, but now fears death, he needs a justifiable reason to abort the mission. The detective's "correct guess" provides that justification.
5. Premise 5 (Operational Awareness): Bat's organization would kill him for failure. Japanese police protection is his only escape.

Chain of Reasoning:

* Observed: Bat gives up after one correct guess—no fight, no backup plan.
* Observed: He expresses relief at the prospect of arrest.
* Inferred: He does not want to complete the hijacking or die.
* Deduced: Someone ordered him to die in the crash (suicide mission).
* Deduced: He used the detective game as a face-saving exit strategy.
* Conclusion: His claim of willingness to die was a lie; he fears death.

Key Dialogue:

Siesta: "You said you will give up your life just to hijack this plane, but that is a lie. You are scared of death, aren't you?"
Bat: "What are you saying?"
Siesta: "You retreated way too early. A man hijacked a plane alone... and just retreated in the face of a girl?"
Siesta: "It's likely that you were ordered to hijack this plane. That party ordered you to die in the plane crash... But you're scared of dying, and you don't want to die. So you used us to give yourself a reason not to die, no?"

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Deductive Reasoning: S (Multi-layer inference from behavioral observation to hidden organizational dynamics)
Psychological Analysis: S (Identifies fear, self-preservation, and face-saving mechanism)
Strategic Reading: A (Understands Bat's use of the detective as a tool for his own survival)

Feat 3: Revealing Pre-existing Knowledge of the Entire Situation

After dismantling Bat's motive and fear, Bat asks a final question: "What clues did you have? If it's just because I retreated too quickly—"

Siesta cuts him off: "Haa, that is one point. But I knew right from the beginning."

Bat: "What do you mean?"

Siesta: "I know everything, from the fact that you boarded this plane, planned to hijack it, and even about the people who ordered you."

She then adds: "A first-rate detective will solve an incident before it happens. Well, I was a little late because I accidentally took a nap though."

Premises:

1. Premise 1 (Information Superiority): Siesta boarded this specific flight with prior knowledge of the threat.
2. Premise 2 (Causal Chain): The suitcase she had Kimizuka carry was pre-planted with a modified musket and "red bullets" made from her blood.
3. Premise 3 (Operational Definition): A "first-rate detective" does not merely react to crimes—they prevent them through advance intelligence and preparation.
4. Premise 4 (Self-Assessment): Her only error was napping, implying she had already done all necessary deduction before boarding.

Chain of Reasoning (implicit, revealed through action):

* She knew Bat was on the flight.
* She knew SPES ordered the hijacking.
* She knew Bat had betrayed the organization.
* She knew she would need a weapon inside the plane.
* She orchestrated Kimizuka's presence (he unknowingly carried her musket).
* She pre-modified ammunition with her blood to create an obedience-compulsion effect.

Conclusion: Every event—the hijacking, the game, the fight, the outcome—was anticipated and planned for.

Key Dialogue:

Bat: "What clues did you have in deducing this?"
Siesta: "Haa, that is one point. But I knew right from the beginning. I know everything, from the fact that you boarded this plane, planned to hijack it, and even about the people who ordered you."
Siesta: "A first-rate detective will solve an incident before it happens."

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Preventive Reasoning: S (Solved the incident before it occurred)
Strategic Planning: S (Planted assets, modified weapons, arranged Kimizuka's presence)
Information Gathering: S (Knew SPES's plans and Bat's role in advance)

Feat 4: The "Red Bullet" – Pre-emptive Neutralization of an Android's Attack Capability

After the fight, when Bat's feeler (modified ear appendage) is disabled, Siesta reveals the mechanism: "I just shot you with the 'red bullet' that was made from my 'blood', you know? Anyone who touched this blood cannot disobey the master—your feeler can never attack me again."

This is later confirmed when, in the present timeline, Bat's feeler cannot attack Natsunagi (who has Siesta's heart) because the same blood-based compulsion persists across the transplanted organ.

Premises (implied by Siesta's reasoning):

1. Premise 1 (Biological Modification): Siesta's blood contains a unique property or modification that can affect Android technology.
2. Premise 2 (Permanent Effect): Once an Android's component touches this blood, a permanent compulsion is established.
3. Premise 3 (Transferability): The compulsion is tied to Siesta's biological signature, not her physical presence—it transfers with her blood, organs, or genetic material.
4. Premise 4 (Pre-planning): She prepared these bullets before boarding, knowing she would face an Android.

Chain of Reasoning:

* Siesta knows SPES uses Android technology.
* She knows she will eventually fight them.
* She develops a countermeasure based on her own biology.
* She manufactures ammunition containing her modified blood.
* She ensures Kimizuka carries the weapon onto the flight.
* She uses it on Bat's feeler during the fight.

Result: Bat's attack capability against her is permanently neutralized.

Key Dialogue:

Siesta: "I just shot you with the 'red bullet' that was made from my 'blood', you know? Anyone who touched this blood cannot disobey the master—your feeler can never attack me again."
Bat: "...Goodness, what logic is that?"
Siesta: "That's a corporate secret."

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Strategic Pre-planning: S (Developed biological countermeasure to Android tech)
Combat Preparation: S (Modified ammunition, planted on plane)
Long-term Reasoning: S (Effect persists across time and even organ transplantation, as seen in Volume 2)

EVENT 2: The Medusa Case – Two Years Ago

Context: During their three-year journey together, Siesta and Kimizuka investigate rumors of a monster called "Medusa" living in a Western-style house. The owner, an old man, claims the rumors refer to his comatose daughter Mary. He welcomes them warmly, serves tea, and offers to show them Mary's room.

Feat 5: Detecting Poison Through Behavioral Anomalies and Pre-emptive Countermeasures

In the living room, the owner serves cold drinks. As Kimizuka reaches for his glass, Siesta stomps on his foot twice—first to stop him from drinking, second to cause him to trip and spill the drinks, shattering the glasses. She then "helps" the owner by wiping his clothes, secretly stealing the antidote syringe from his pocket.

Later, when poison fills the room, Siesta is unaffected because she already used the antidote. The owner collapses in confusion, having not anticipated his own antidote being stolen.

Premises (Siesta's reasoning chain):

1. Premise 1 (Environmental Observation): The room has no open windows despite the heat. The owner apologized for malfunctioning air-conditioning but made no effort to ventilate.
2. Premise 2 (Behavioral Pattern – Mary): Mary is in a post-coma unresponsiveness state but blinks in a "rhythmic, robotic manner" that shows "definite will" – specifically, trying to expose her stepfather.
3. Premise 3 (Logical Deduction from Mary's Blinking): A comatose girl has no reason to blink rhythmically unless she is signaling. Her condition was caused by the stepfather (as later confirmed). She is trying to warn visitors.
4. Premise 4 (Poison Delivery Inference): The drinks are the most likely delivery method for poison. The owner repeatedly offered them and left to "prepare" them alone.
5. Premise 5 (Antidote Presence): The owner would not poison himself; he has an antidote. Siesta identifies it during the drink-spilling incident and steals it.

Chain of Reasoning:

* Observed: Closed windows + heat + poisoned drinks planned.
* Observed: Mary's rhythmic blinking (non-random, purposeful).
* Inferred: Mary is trying to communicate danger.
* Inferred: The stepfather is the cause of her condition.
* Inferred: The room will be poisoned after drinks are offered.

Action: Spill drinks (prevent consumption), steal antidote (secure immunity), pretend to help owner (cover for theft).

Result: Siesta is immune; owner collapses; Mary's captivity is exposed.

Key Dialogue:

Kimizuka: "You already knew that guy's been targeting us?"
Siesta: "Sort of. I was really shocked when you actually planned to drink what was clearly a spiked beverage."
Kimizuka: "So back then... when you wiped his clothes with your handkerchief, you already stole that antidote."
Siesta: "Of course. I was not affected as I had used that."
Kimizuka: "But you are rather observant."
Siesta: "Are you a fool?... Did you forget what I said on the way to that western house? The importance of expanding our horizons, training our sight and hearing. For example, you should be more sensitive to human stares."

On Mary's blinking:

Siesta: "I was convinced when I saw Mary's eyes. She was blinking away furiously... It was a rhythmic, robotic manner... but there was definitely some will in there. I supposed she wanted to expose the mistake of her stepfather."

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Observational Deduction: S (Noticed rhythmic blinking pattern under extreme conditions)
Strategic Counter-planning: A (Stole antidote before poison was released)
Behavioral Analysis: A (Interpreted Mary's blinking as intentional communication)
Environmental Reasoning: A (Connected closed windows to poison delivery method)

EVENT 3: The Cruise Ship Battle – Present Day

Context: On a luxury cruise, Natsunagi (who has Siesta's heart) is abducted by Chameleon, an Android with invisibility and a regenerating tongue. Kimizuka and Saikawa search for her. At the critical moment, Siesta's consciousness temporarily takes over Natsunagi's body through memory transfer. She appears in the burning casino to fight alongside Kimizuka one last time.

Feat 6: Immediate Tactical Assessment of Chameleon's Weaknesses (Red Bullet Continuity)

Upon taking control of Natsunagi's body, Siesta immediately recognizes that Chameleon's tongue cannot attack her. Why? Because four years ago, she shot Bat's feeler with a red bullet made from her blood. That blood now flows in Natsunagi's body (via the heart transplant). Any Android that touched that blood cannot attack her – or anyone with her blood.

Premises:

1. Siesta's blood-based compulsion is tied to her genetic/biological signature.
2. Natsunagi's body contains Siesta's heart, therefore Siesta's blood properties are present.
3. Any Android previously affected by the red bullet cannot attack anyone with Siesta's blood.
4. Chameleon is an Android; if he ever touched her blood (or if the compulsion is universal to SPES Androids after exposure), he is vulnerable.

Chain:

* Siesta's blood property: "Anyone who touched this blood cannot disobey the master."
* That blood is now in Natsunagi's body.
* Siesta is currently using Natsunagi's body.
* Therefore, any Android that previously touched her blood cannot attack this body.
* She engages Chameleon directly with reduced risk.

Key Dialogue:

Kimizuka: "The 'heart' has your existence?"

[Siesta explains memory transfer]

During combat: Siesta reminds Kimizuka – "His 'tongue' can't attack me anymore."

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Tactical Memory Recall: S
Combat Deduction: A
Biological Reasoning: S

Feat 7: Coordinated Flanking Combat with Kimizuka

Siesta throws Kimizuka a gun, says "You—be my assistant," and they immediately flank Chameleon from opposite sides. Despite one year apart and Siesta inhabiting an untrained body, they synchronize attacks, dodge, and relay intel seamlessly. Siesta uses her immunity to directly engage the tongue while Kimizuka handles the tail. Their verbal banter distracts and enrages the enemy, making him predictable.

Premises:

1. Three years of shared combat history created intuitive synchronization.
2. Role specialization: Kimizuka = close-range distraction; Siesta = ranged precision.
3. Information sharing: Kimizuka calls out Chameleon's abilities (length/hardness variation) as he learns them.
4. Borrowed body limitations: Siesta compensates for lack of physical strength through strategy and precision.

Chain:

* Siesta enters from ceiling hole (high ground).
* She provides Kimizuka with a weapon (force multiplication).
* They take opposite positions (crossfire, no blind spots).
* Siesta uses red-bullet immunity to engage tongue directly.
* Kimizuka engages tail, drawing aggro.
* They alternate firing to suppress regeneration.
* Verbal banter maintains morale and enrages Chameleon (making him more predictable).

Key Dialogue:

Siesta: "You—be my assistant."
Kimizuka: "As you wish—famous detective."
Kimizuka: "Be careful! That thing can change its length and hardness!"
Siesta: "Huh, you're fine with that side?"
Kimizuka: "Hm, is there a problem?"
Siesta: "Your habit of spacing out somehow hasn't changed."

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Tactical Coordination: S
Combat Awareness: A
Leadership: A

Feat 8: Executing the Ringtone Strategy (Multi-party Coordination)

Natsunagi, before being taken over, slipped her phone into Chameleon's clothing. Saikawa, watching from a lifeboat with her all-seeing left eye, tracks Chameleon's invisibility. At the critical moment, Saikawa calls the phone. The ringtone reveals Chameleon's exact location. Siesta, who was informed of this plan by Natsunagi in their shared mental space, times her final shot with Kimizuka to coincide with the ring.

Premises:

1. Natsunagi communicated the phone-planting plan to Siesta in their mental space.
2. Saikawa's left eye can see through Chameleon's invisibility.
3. The phone call will come at the most vulnerable moment.
4. Siesta must coordinate her attack with Kimizuka to fire simultaneously at the ringtone source.

Chain:

* Natsunagi, while conscious, realized the value of a tracking device.
* She planted her phone on Chameleon during capture.
* She told Siesta the plan.
* Saikawa waits for the right moment and calls.
* Ringtone reveals position.
* Siesta yells "Assistant!" and Kimizuka responds "I know!"
* Both fire simultaneously.

Chameleon is defeated.

Key Dialogue:

Siesta: "Assistant!"
Kimizuka: "I know!"
Narration: "We looked towards that place, and squeezed the trigger... A blunt sound, a short cry, signalled the end of it all."

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Multi-party Coordination: S (4 people across different locations)
Delegated Reasoning: A
Timing/Execution: S

Feat 9: Strategic Succession – Identifying the Four as Her Legacy

After the battle, Siesta (still in Natsunagi's body) reveals her final wish: "Nagisa Natsunagi, Yui Saikawa, Charlotte Arisaka Andersen, Kimihiko Kimizuka – I wish for the four of you to defeat SPES. The four of you are my remaining legacy – my final hope."

Premises:

1. She is dead and cannot return (this is the last time).
2. Each of the four has a unique, irreplaceable capability:
Natsunagi: her heart (literal and figurative), passion, ability to move others.
Saikawa: left eye (sees through objects/invisibility), wealth, resources.
Charl: combat training, military connections, determination.
Kimizuka: trouble magnetism, experience, detective assistant skills.
3. Together, they cover weaknesses she alone could not.
4. Each has a personal reason to fight SPES (revenge, protection, loyalty, borrowed life).
5. A detective's legacy is not a weapon or information – it is people who continue the mission.

Chain:

* Siesta knows she cannot continue fighting.
* She assesses the four individuals now connected to her.
* She recognizes that each alone is insufficient.
* She also recognizes that together they form a complete unit.
* She articulates this as her "final wish" (which, given her nature, is also a strategic command).

Conclusion: The four of them are her legacy – the means by which SPES will eventually be defeated.

Key Dialogue:

Natsunagi: "I was asked after all."
Kimizuka: "By who? Wait, Siesta?"
Natsunagi: "Yes, that's the condition to get her to move once."
[Siesta's message]: "Nagisa Natsunagi, Yui Saikawa, Charlotte Arisaka Andersen, Kimihiko Kimizuka – I wish for the four of you to defeat SPES."
Narration: "The four of you are my remaining legacy – my final hope."

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Strategic Succession Planning: S
Personnel Assessment: S
Long-term Reasoning: S

EVENT 4: The "Girl's Dialogue" – Internal Reasoning About Interference

Context: In a shared mental space (the "heart"), Natsunagi and Siesta's consciousness argue. Natsunagi demands Siesta take over her body to save Kimizuka. Siesta initially refuses, saying she has no right to interfere. Natsunagi berates her, calling her childish. Siesta eventually agrees but only once.

Feat 10: Meta-cognitive Reasoning About Emotional Attachment and Professional Detachment

Siesta states: "If I had formed intimate relationships with humans, I would have lingering regrets. This fetter would definitely affect my work." This is her core professional principle. However, in the mental dialogue, she admits (through her actions, if not explicitly) that she has already violated this principle – she does care about Kimizuka.

Premises:

1. A detective must remain detached to make rational decisions.
2. Emotional attachments create biases that compromise judgment.
3. She has spent three years with Kimizuka, inevitably forming attachment.
4. She recognizes this as a "mistake" but cannot undo it.
5. Natsunagi's accusation ("You don't want to help him anymore? Eh, are you that childish?") forces her to confront this contradiction.

Chain:

* Siesta states her professional ideal: no intimate relationships.
* Yet she admits (to Kimizuka on Page 199) that she paid more attention to him than she imagined.
* In the mental space, she initially refuses to help, citing lack of right to interfere.
* Natsunagi calls her childish.
* Siesta re-evaluates: her principle of detachment has already been broken.
* She agrees to help, but only once – a compromise between attachment and professionalism.

Conclusion: Siesta's reasoning about emotional detachment is self-aware but imperfect; she allows one final exception.

Key Dialogue:

Siesta (to Kimizuka, earlier): "If I had formed intimate relationships with humans, I would have lingering regrets. This fetter would definitely affect my work."
In mental space: "That's a misunderstanding. I'm already dead. I don't have the right to interact with him."
Natsunagi: "You don't want to help him anymore!? Eh, are you that childish!?"
Siesta: "…You are the first human to berate me to this extent."
She eventually agrees: "But just once."

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Meta-cognitive Reasoning: A
Emotional Self-assessment: B (she recognizes her attachment but doesn't fully resolve the contradiction)
Ethical/Professional Reasoning: A

VOLUME 2

EVENT 1: The "Toilet Bound Hanako-san" Case – Middle School Cultural Festival (Flashback)
Context: Siesta intrudes into Kimizuka's apartment to convince him to become her assistant. He refuses, wanting a peaceful daily life. She learns about a problem at his middle school: students are disappearing, and rumors of "Toilet Bound Hanako-san" multiplying. Siesta attends the cultural festival in a school uniform to investigate the disappearances, which she suspects are linked to drugs and SPES.

Feat 1: Identifying the Drug Connection from a Plastic Bag Scrap in a Haunted House Toilet

While Kimizuka is suffering from a stomachache in the haunted house toilet (third floor, third cubicle – the rumored location of Hanako-san), Siesta climbs over the door and picks up a small plastic bag scrap behind the toilet bowl. She immediately deduces that it contains residue of a stimulant drug. She connects this to the missing students and the "multiplying Hanako-san" rumor.

Premises (explicit + implicit):

1. Spatial-Location Premise: The bag was found in the third-floor, third girls' toilet – the exact location associated with the Hanako-san urban legend. This is not a coincidence; the legend serves as a coded meeting point.
2. Chemical-Residue Premise: The bag contains trace amounts of a substance that, based on its physical appearance and smell, is consistent with a stimulant drug known to cause euphoria, increased concentration, and memory loss as side effects.
3. Behavioral-Correlation Premise: The students who have gone missing are predominantly from the track team – a group that would have motive to use performance-enhancing substances. Their sudden absences from school coincide with the rise in Hanako-san sightings.
4. Linguistic-Euphemism Premise: The name "Hanako-san" is a pun or coded reference. The drug is derived from plant "pollen," and "Hana" means flower in Japanese. The rumor of Hanako-san "multiplying" actually refers to more students becoming drug sellers to fund their own addictions – each new seller becomes another "Hanako-san."
5. Temporal-Operational Premise: The rumor states that Hanako-san appears at 3 AM. This is a deliberately inconvenient time for normal students but a perfect time for covert drug transactions, as the school is empty and security is minimal.

Chain of Reasoning:

* Step 1: Observe that the bag's location matches the rumored Hanako-san meeting spot → not random litter.
* Step 2: Analyze bag contents → identifies as a stimulant drug with memory-loss side effects.
* Step 3: Recall that missing students are from track team → likely users of performance enhancers.
* Step 4: Connect "multiplying Hanako-san" to new sellers → each missing student becomes a dealer.
* Step 5: Recognize "Hanako" as a euphemism for the plant-derived drug ("flower").
* Step 6: Conclude that the Hanako-san rumor disguises a drug distribution network operating from the school.

Conclusion: The "Toilet Bound Hanako-san" is not a ghost but a coded reference to a drug trade. The third-floor, third cubicle is the exchange point. The multiplying sightings indicate the drug network expanding as more students become addicted and become sellers. The drug is derived from a plant – later revealed to be connected to SPES.

Key Dialogue (Paraphrased from Volume 2):

Siesta: "Just wanted to investigate something... hm, found it." (picks up plastic bag)
Siesta: "What do you think this is?"
Kimizuka: "I guess... a bag containing flu medicine?"
Siesta: "It is a certain stimulant of sorts. After consuming it, the user will be in a state of euphoria, with increased concentration. I suppose in this school, it started spreading amongst the students of the track team."
Siesta: "This illegal drug was made from the 'pollen' of a certain plant. That's why it's called 'Hanako-san'? That's a stupid joke."

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Observational Deduction: A (identifies drug from microscopic residue)
Pattern Recognition: A (links rumor to criminal activity)
Linguistic Deduction: B (understands euphemism)
Environmental Reasoning: A (maps legend to operational reality)

Feat 2: Identifying the Bunny Suit as the Drug Seller from Running Shoes

After the drug deal trap is set, a person in a pink bunny suit (who was earlier distributing festival pamphlets) appears at the toilet. When Siesta draws her gun, the bunny flees. During the chase, Siesta notices that the bunny is wearing running shoes under the costume – a detail that seems trivial but becomes the key clue. She connects this to the fact that the missing students (the drug sellers) were from the track team, who would naturally wear running shoes.

Premises:

1. Incongruous-Detail Premise: A bunny costume is meant for mascot duty, not for running. The presence of running shoes indicates the wearer anticipates needing to flee quickly.
2. Demographic-Profile Premise: The drug sellers are from the track team. Track team members own and regularly wear running shoes.
3. Behavioral-Readiness Premise: The bunny appeared at the exact 3 AM time slot that Siesta coded as the deal time – confirming she was not a random staff member but the intended contact.
4. Concealment-Strategy Premise: The costume serves as a disguise to hide the seller's identity while allowing them to move freely through the crowded festival.

Chain of Reasoning:

* Step 1: Observe running shoes under bunny costume → wearer expects to run.
* Step 2: Recall that drug sellers are from track team → they wear running shoes habitually.
* Step 3: Note that the bunny appeared at the coded 3 AM meeting time → not a coincidence.
* Step 4: Infer that the costume is a disguise, not a genuine mascot outfit.
* Step 5: Conclude that the bunny is one of the drug sellers, using the festival as cover.

Conclusion: The bunny suit is a deliberate disguise worn by a track team member who sells drugs. The running shoes – practical for escape – became the giveaway because they are incongruous with the costume but consistent with the seller's athletic identity. Siesta's observation of this small detail cracked the case.

Key Dialogue (Paraphrased):

Kimizuka: "I remember you were distributing pamphlets..."
Siesta: "That bunny is the Toilet Bound Hanako-san."
Later narration: "The bunny had running shoes on, and basically showed her true identity. Many of the 'Hanako-san' were from the track team, so once Siesta noticed that 'bunny', she immediately noticed that was the one member who sold the drugs. The running shoes to be used for escaping became the anchor pulling her down."

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Observational Deduction: A (running shoes as key clue)
Behavioral Profiling: A (links track team to running shoes to drug sellers)
Disguise Penetration: B (sees through costume to identity)

Feat 3: Using the Pamphlet Schedule as a Coded Signal to Lure the Culprit

When Siesta receives the culture festival pamphlet, she appears to be checking the haunted house's operating hours, specifically the 15-minute breaks. Kimizuka assumes she is planning their visit. In reality, Siesta is noting that the haunted house has a 3 AM break slot – which is not for the haunted house itself but for the staff. She uses this information to code a message to the drug seller: she will be at the third-floor toilet at 3 AM as a "customer." The seller arrives, confirming the trap.

Premises:

1. Scheduled-Coincidence Premise: The drug deal occurs at 3 AM in the third-floor girls' toilet – an unusual time that would be known only to those in the network.
2. Pamphlet-as-Code Premise: The festival pamphlet lists the haunted house's full schedule, including the 3 AM "break" for staff. To an outsider, this is meaningless. To the seller, it signals that the person reading it knows the deal time.
3. Entrapment-Through-Routine Premise: By being at the toilet at 3 AM, Siesta presents herself as a customer. The seller, expecting a transaction, arrives without suspicion.
4. Reaction-as-Confirmation Premise: When Siesta draws her gun instead of exchanging money, the seller flees – confirming guilt. An innocent staff member would not run.

Chain of Reasoning:

* Step 1: Obtain pamphlet and scan for the 3 AM time slot.
* Step 2: Recognize that 3 AM matches the rumored Hanako-san appearance time.
* Step 3: Infer that the pamphlet's 3 AM break is the coded signal for the drug deal.
* Step 4: Go to the toilet at that time, posing as a customer.
* Step 5: When the bunny arrives, draw weapon → flight response = guilt.
* Step 6: Conclude that the pamphlet successfully functioned as a lure.

Conclusion: Siesta transformed an innocent cultural festival pamphlet into a sophisticated piece of counter-intelligence. She used the seller's own operational schedule against them, turning a routine time slot into a trap. This demonstrates her ability to weaponize existing systems and routines.

Key Dialogue (Paraphrased):

Kimizuka: "So, you used the code to contact the 'bunny', and acted as a customer?"
Narration: "In hindsight, when Siesta received the pamphlet, she was insistent on the time. She wasn't referring to the rest time, but at the drug deal at 3am in the morning. The 'bunny' obediently came to the scene, saw Siesta wield the gun, realized it was a trap, and ran off."

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Strategic Communication: A (uses innocent pamphlet as coded signal)
Trap Setting: A (lures culprit to location)
Operational Security: B (understands criminal communication methods)

Feat 4: Connecting the School Drug to SPES and Recognizing the Larger Conspiracy

After the case is resolved, Siesta notes that the "flower" used to produce the drug is related to SPES. This is not just a local school problem – it is part of a global conspiracy. The drug's effects (memory loss, euphoria) match the properties of SPES's experimental substances. Siesta's ability to see the big picture allows her to connect a petty drug ring to the secret organization she is fighting.

Premises:

1. Botanical-Source Premise: The drug is derived from a plant's pollen. SPES uses biological and plant-based technology extensively (Androids, SEED, biological weapons).
2. Effect-Profile Premise: The drug causes memory loss and altered consciousness. Similar effects have been observed in other SPES-related incidents (e.g., the pollen from Betelgeuse later in Volume 2).
3. Distribution-Network Premise: The drug is spreading through schools – a tactic that destabilizes society from within, consistent with SPES's methods of infiltration rather than direct attack.
4. Organizational-Footprint Premise: A simple school drug ring would not have the sophistication to produce a plant-derived stimulant with memory-altering properties. This points to an external supplier.

Chain of Reasoning:

* Step 1: Identify the drug's plant origin from the "pollen" reference.
* Step 2: Recall SPES's use of botanical components (SEED as a plant entity, Betelgeuse's pollen, etc.).
* Step 3: Note that the drug's memory-loss side effect matches SPES's other biological weapons.
* Step 4: Recognize that school infiltration is a long-term destabilization tactic, not a local crime.
* Step 5: Conclude that this case is a small part of SPES's broader operation, not an isolated incident.

Conclusion: Siesta's ability to zoom out from a local nuisance to a global conspiracy is a hallmark of her detective work. She never treats any case as truly isolated – everything connects to SPES. This allows her to anticipate future incidents and prepare countermeasures.

Key Dialogue (Paraphrased):

Siesta: "But the 'flower' used this time appears to be related to that organization."
Kimizuka: "That organization, as in?"
Siesta nodded silently, confirming so.

Narration: "The secret organization SPES – yes, if we're talking drugs, there should be a boss... a mastermind. Those guys spread their shadows around me without me knowing."

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Pattern Recognition: S (connects local drug case to global conspiracy)
Strategic Awareness: A
Threat Modeling: A

Feat 5: The "I'll Protect You" Recruitment – Strategic Psychological Persuasion

At the airport cafe (after the school festival case), Kimizuka asks Siesta the critical question: "If I become your assistant, what's in it for me?" He expects her to offer money, adventure, or excitement. Instead, she closes her eyes, then opens them and says: "I'll protect you. Even with that nature of yours, even when you get involved in any incident or trouble, I shall step up and protect you." This is not a sentimental gesture – it is a calculated psychological appeal that addresses Kimizuka's deepest fear: his "nature of getting into trouble" makes him vulnerable. By offering protection, she gives him the one thing he cannot get on his own.

Premises:

1. Psychological-Profile Premise: Kimizuka's core motivation is not money or excitement but security and a peaceful daily life. His "nature of getting into trouble" has caused him lifelong stress.
2. Asset-Value Premise: Kimizuka's trouble-magnetism is a valuable asset for Siesta – it will draw incidents and enemies to her, allowing her to pursue SPES. But he will only agree if the benefit outweighs the risk.
3. Risk-Compensation Premise: The main risk of being with Siesta is danger. The only way to counterbalance that risk is to offer superior protection.
4. Credibility Premise: Siesta has already demonstrated her combat abilities (defeating Bat on the plane). Her promise of protection is backed by observable capability.
5. Emotional-Appeal Premise: By speaking gently and looking him in the eyes, she signals sincerity and personal commitment – not just a transactional arrangement.

Chain of Reasoning:

* Step 1: Assess Kimizuka's personality – he wants peace, not adventure.
* Step 2: Recognize that his trouble-magnetism is both an asset (for Siesta) and a liability (for him).
* Step 3: Calculate that the only reward he would value is protection from the very dangers his nature attracts.
* Step 4: Formulate the promise as "I'll protect you" – directly addressing his fear.
* Step 5: Deliver the promise with emotional sincerity to overcome his skepticism.
* Step 6: Conclude that he will accept because the offer resolves his core conflict.

Conclusion: Siesta's recruitment of Kimizuka is a masterclass in strategic persuasion. She does not offer what she has (money, adventure) but what he needs (security). This turns a reluctant target into a willing partner. The "three years of adventure" that follow are built on this foundation of trust and protection.

Key Dialogue (Paraphrased):

Kimizuka: "If I become your assistant, what's in it for me? What benefit can you give me?"
Siesta closed her eyes tightly, "I'll protect you." And then she opened her eyes, saying with a gentle smile. "Even with that nature of yours, even when you get involved in any incident or trouble, I shall step up and protect you."
Siesta: "You—be my assistant."
Kimizuka: "...Well, since you said so, it's not like I can't accept it."

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Psychological Insight: S (identifies Kimizuka's core motivation)
Strategic Recruitment: S (secures essential asset for SPES pursuit)
Emotional Intelligence: A (uses sincerity to build trust)

EVENT 2: The Jack the Ripper / Cerberus Case – London
Context: Siesta and Kimizuka are in London investigating a series of heart-removal murders mimicking Jack the Ripper. The culprit is an Android named Cerberus (dog-like, sharp nose, transformation ability). Later, a more powerful enemy – Hel – appears, with red eyes that can control minds.

Feat 6: Diagnosing Cerberus's Ability from the Crime Scene and Mythology

At a church murder scene, Siesta kneels by the corpse of a clergyman whose left chest has been ripped open and heart removed. She immediately states: "This is the work of Cerberus." She explains that Cerberus is the "watchdog of hell" known for devouring human hearts. She also deduces that Cerberus has a highly sensitive nose, which is why the police have been unable to capture it – it can smell pursuers from a distance and flee.

Premises:

1. Modus-Operandi Premise: The heart is removed with surgical precision, not random violence. This matches the signature of a specific Android rather than an ordinary killer.
2. Codename-System Premise: SPES Androids are named after mythological creatures, and their modifications correspond to the creature's traits (Bat = ears, Chameleon = invisibility, Cerberus = ?).
3. Mythological-Knowledge Premise: Cerberus in Greek mythology is a three-headed dog that guards the gates of hell. Dogs have exceptional olfactory abilities. Therefore, Cerberus's Android modification is likely the nose.
4. Evasion-Pattern Premise: Despite multiple murders, police have no leads. This suggests the culprit can detect and avoid pursuers before they get close – consistent with enhanced smell.
5. Historical-Continuity Premise: The modern "Jack the Ripper" imitator is not a random copycat but SPES's Cerberus, using the legend as cover for ritualistic heart harvesting.

Chain of Reasoning:

* Step 1: Observe heart removal – consistent with SPES Android signatures.
* Step 2: Recall that SPES uses codenames tied to abilities (Bat = ears).
* Step 3: Identify mythological Cerberus = dog → nose.
* Step 4: Infer that Cerberus's nose is enhanced, allowing long-range detection.
* Step 5: Conclude that the police's inability to catch him is due to this olfactory advantage.

Conclusion: Siesta's diagnosis of Cerberus's ability is based on a combination of forensic observation, codename logic, and mythological knowledge. She correctly identifies both the culprit's identity and his primary sensory advantage, which then informs her counter-strategy.

Key Dialogue (Paraphrased):

Siesta: "This is the work of Cerberus."
Kimizuka: "Cerberus, you say?"
Siesta: "Codename Cerberus. Watchdog of Hell, known for devouring human hearts."
Later: "The enemy's 'nose' seems rather potent. It's been able to escape me no matter how I tried to pursue."

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Deductive Reasoning: A (from crime scene to codename to ability)
Mythological Knowledge: B (uses mythology as operational intelligence)
Pattern Recognition: A (recognizes SPES naming conventions)

Feat 7: The Bait Strategy – Using Kimizuka as Lure to Overcome Cerberus's Nose

Siesta proposes that instead of chasing Cerberus, they should lure it to them. She notes that Cerberus fears her and will flee if it smells her. However, Cerberus does not consider Kimizuka a threat. Therefore, if Kimizuka is isolated in a hotel room, Cerberus will attack him. Meanwhile, Siesta will hide nearby, using a specialized gas to mask her scent. This turns Cerberus's greatest strength (its nose) into its greatest weakness (overconfidence when no threat scent is present).

Premises:

1. Fear-Response Premise: Cerberus has encountered Siesta before and knows she is dangerous. It will flee at the first sign of her scent.
2. Threat-Perception Premise: Cerberus does not perceive Kimizuka as a threat. He is an ordinary human (from Cerberus's perspective).
3. Isolation-Vulnerability Premise: If Kimizuka is alone, Cerberus will attack without hesitation, believing there is no danger.
4. Scent-Masking Premise: Siesta can add a specialized gas to the hotel's sprinkler system that numbs olfactory receptors. Once activated, Cerberus's nose becomes useless.
5. Counter-Ambush Premise: With Cerberus's nose neutralized, Siesta can approach undetected and strike from close range.

Chain of Reasoning:

* Step 1: Recognize that Cerberus's nose is both its strength and its operational anchor.
* Step 2: Identify that Cerberus flees from Siesta but not from Kimizuka.
* Step 3: Isolate Kimizuka → Cerberus will come.
* Step 4: Hide Siesta's scent with gas → Cerberus will not detect the trap.
* Step 5: Wait for Cerberus to engage Kimizuka.
* Step 6: Siesta enters, shoots Cerberus.
* Step 7: Conclude that turning an enemy's strength into a vulnerability is the most efficient victory.

Conclusion: This strategy exemplifies Siesta's tactical genius. She does not try to overpower Cerberus's nose (impossible) or chase it (fruitless). Instead, she uses its own behavioral pattern (fleeing from her, attacking others) as the bait, and its sensory dependence as the point of failure. The plan requires precise timing, Kimizuka's cooperation, and pre-arranged infrastructure modification (gas in sprinklers).

Key Dialogue (Paraphrased):

Siesta: "We shall not give chase after Cerberus. We will lure Cerberus to us."
Siesta: "Cerberus fears only me. In other words, it will be more than happy to attack you alone."
Kimizuka: "So you're using me as bait!?"
Siesta: "You should have known this day would come ever since you started this job."

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Strategic Planning: S (bait-and-ambush with scent countermeasure)
Risk Assessment: A (calculates that Kimizuka can survive long enough)
Deception: A (uses enemy's fear response against it)
Counter-Intelligence: S (neutralizes primary sensory advantage)

Feat 8: The Sprinkler Gas Strategy – Environmental Pre-engineering

Before the operation, Siesta added a specialized gas to the hotel's sprinkler system. When Kimizuka lights a Zippo lighter to set the bed on fire, the sprinklers activate, releasing the gas. Cerberus's nose is numbed, and Siesta, who had been hiding outside the room (beyond the gas's range but waiting), can now enter through the window undetected. The gas is harmless to humans but specifically targets olfactory receptors.

Premises:

1. Fire-as-Trigger Premise: Kimizuka will need to create an emergency that activates the sprinklers. Siesta knows he carries a Zippo (given by Fuubi-san) and will use it in a crisis.
2. Gas-Efficacy Premise: The gas is a custom formulation that temporarily numbs the sense of smell without causing other harm. It disperses quickly but lingers long enough for the operation.
3. Olfactory-Neutralization Premise: Cerberus's primary detection method is smell. Without it, he is effectively blind to Siesta's approach.
4. Positioning Premise: Siesta hides outside the window, upwind and beyond the gas's dispersal range, so her scent does not trigger Cerberus prematurely.
5. Timing-Exploitation Premise: The moment the sprinklers activate, Cerberus will be confused by the sudden loss of smell, creating an opening for Siesta to enter and shoot.

Chain of Reasoning:

* Step 1: Pre-arrange gas in sprinkler system before operation.
* Step 2: Instruct Kimizuka to create a fire if needed (implicitly via Zippo).
* Step 3: Kimizuka lights bed on fire → sprinklers activate.
* Step 4: Gas disperses → Cerberus's nose numbed.
* Step 5: Siesta, who was waiting outside, enters through window.
* Step 6: She shoots Cerberus while his senses are compromised.
* Step 7: Conclude that pre-engineering the environment is the key to controlling the battlefield.

Conclusion: Siesta's use of the sprinkler system is a brilliant example of environmental warfare. She does not rely on luck or improvisation – she builds the trap into the physical space before the enemy even arrives. This level of pre-planning is characteristic of her "first-rate detective" philosophy: solve the incident before it happens.

Key Dialogue (Paraphrased):

After the battle, Siesta explains: "Seems like there is still some stench here. Before you opened the window, this room should be filled with a special gas."
Kimizuka realizes: "It probably was the plan Siesta had prepared. She knew I brought the Zippo, expected that the sprinklers would be activated in the battle, and added some gas in the water. Cerberus had a sharp nose, and might be numbed by the gas, so he didn't expect Siesta to be nearby."

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Environmental Engineering: S (modifies building infrastructure as trap)
Predictive Planning: S (anticipates Kimizuka's actions with Zippo)
Counter-Intelligence: S (neutralizes enemy's primary sense)
Chemical Knowledge: A (formulates olfactory-numbing gas)

Feat 9: Recognizing Hel's True Ability – Mind Control Through Red Eyes

When Hel first appears, she uses her red eyes to freeze Siesta in place, preventing her from moving or speaking. Siesta immediately recognizes that Hel's ability is visual-based mind control. Later, this understanding becomes critical to developing a counter-strategy.

Premises:

1. Causation-Observation Premise: The moment Hel's eyes glow red, Siesta's body stops responding. When eye contact is broken, movement returns. This is not a coincidence.
2. No-Other-Vector Premise: Hel does not use sound, touch, or any other obvious mechanism to control Siesta. The only variable is eye contact.
3. Android-Modification Premise: Each Android has a specific sensory or physical modification (ears, nose, tongue, etc.). Hel's modification must be her eyes.
4. Command-Response Premise: Hel speaks a command ("You shall not take a step further, and you shall not make a sound") and the command is executed. This indicates the ability is not just paralysis but targeted instruction-following.
5. Self-Application Vulnerability Premise: The ability does not discriminate between target and self. If Hel sees her own red eyes, she would be subject to her own commands.

Chain of Reasoning:

* Step 1: Observe paralysis coinciding with red-eye glow.
* Step 2: Test (inferentially) by breaking eye contact → movement returns.
* Step 3: Rule out other mechanisms (sound, gesture, environment).
* Step 4: Conclude that the eyes are the source of the ability.
* Step 5: Further infer that the ability works on visual contact, not just direct gaze (reflections count).
* Step 6: Note that Hel can issue specific commands, not just freeze targets.
* Step 7: Recognize the vulnerability: if Hel sees her own reflection, she will obey her own commands.

Conclusion: Siesta's rapid identification of Hel's ability – in a single encounter, under combat stress – demonstrates her analytical speed. She understands not only what the ability does but also its logical vulnerabilities, which she later exploits with a mirror.

Key Dialogue (Paraphrased):

Hel: "And furthermore, you shall not take a step further, and you shall not make a sound."
Hel's red eyes flickered. Siesta could not move or speak.
Kimizuka: "Is that the ability of an Android... to control other people's actions or something?"
Siesta (internally) recognizes the mechanism.

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Observational Deduction: S (identifies ability from single encounter)
Threat Assessment: A
Vulnerability Analysis: S (recognizes self-targeting weakness)

Feat 10: Borrowing the Sirius – Rapid Resource Acquisition and Diplomatic Negotiation

After Kimizuka is abducted by Hel, Siesta vanishes for several hours. She returns piloting "Sirius," a secret humanoid combat weapon developed by the British military. She negotiated its loan in a matter of hours by leveraging her reputation as a detective fighting SPES and the imminent threat of Hel's terrorist attack on Parliament.

Premises:

1. Urgency-Crisis Premise: Hel plans to release the biological weapon Betelgeuse to destroy Parliament. This is not just a personal rescue mission – it is a national security threat.
2. Reputation-Equity Premise: Siesta has worked with various governments and agencies over three years. Her reputation as a reliable, effective operative gives her negotiating leverage.
3. Mutual-Benefit Premise: The British government wants to stop Hel but may not have the intelligence or on-the-ground capability. Siesta offers to pilot Sirius in exchange for its use.
4. Time-Constraint Premise: Conventional military response would be too slow. A single operative with a specialized weapon is the fastest option.
5. Technical-Competence Premise: Siesta has previously demonstrated ability to operate advanced technology (she has experience from three years of fighting SPES).

Chain of Reasoning:

* Step 1: After Kimizuka's abduction, assess that rescue requires military-grade force.
* Step 2: Identify that the British military has been developing anti-Android weapons (Sirius).
* Step 3: Contact military/government contacts (built over three years).
* Step 4: Present the threat: Hel will attack Parliament imminently.
* Step 5: Offer her services as pilot – she has the intelligence, the skills, and the motivation.
* Step 6: Negotiate loan of Sirius.
* Step 7: Deploy to rescue Kimizuka and stop Hel.

Conclusion: Siesta's ability to rapidly acquire advanced military resources demonstrates her network of contacts, her reputation, and her persuasive skills. She treats resource acquisition as part of detective work – solving a problem sometimes means getting the right tool, even if that tool is a secret government mecha.

Key Dialogue (Paraphrased):

Kimizuka: "Siesta, how did you get such a thing...?"
Siesta: "...Ah well, picked it up on the way here?"
Kimizuka: "Stop lying!"
Siesta: "...I didn't think you would actually be abducted, and I was so shocked, I hurried off to negotiate with the English government, so I borrowed the humanoid combat weapon 'Sirius' that their military has been developing in secret."

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Diplomatic/Negotiation Skill: S
Resource Acquisition: S
Crisis Management: S
Network Utilization: A

Feat 11: The Mirror Counter – Turning Hel's Ability Against Herself

After the explosion at Big Ben, Hel attacks again, gravely wounded. She commands Siesta: "You shall remain still and be stabbed by me." Siesta does not move – not because she is controlled (she is, but she also chooses not to resist). She angles the small mirror she carries as one of her "seven tools" toward Hel. Hel sees her own red eyes in the reflection, and the command applies to herself. She remains still and stabs herself in the chest.

Premises:

1. Self-Targeting Vulnerability Premise: Hel's ability works on anyone who makes eye contact with her red eyes – including Hel herself. There is no self-exemption in the ability's logic.
2. Reflection-Equivalence Premise: A mirror reflection is optically equivalent to direct eye contact for the purposes of the ability. The brain processes the reflected eyes as "eyes looking at me."
3. Command-Execution Premise: The command "remain still and be stabbed" applies to whoever is under the ability's influence. Hel, seeing herself, becomes the target.
4. Mirror-as-Tool Premise: Siesta always carries a small mirror as one of her seven tools. She has prepared for situations where reflection might be useful (e.g., seeing around corners, signaling, or countering eye-based abilities).
5. Strategic-Passivity Premise: Siesta does not try to dodge or attack because that would break the illusion. She remains still, allowing Hel to see her own command being "obeyed" by Siesta, not realizing the mirror is redirecting the command.

Chain of Reasoning:

* Step 1: Recognize Hel's ability vulnerability (self-targeting).
* Step 2: Recall that she carries a mirror.
* Step 3: When Hel issues a command that involves Hel's own action ("stabbed by me"), note that the command can be reflected.
* Step 4: Position mirror so Hel sees her own eyes.
* Step 5: Remain still (as commanded by Hel, but also to avoid breaking the reflection).
* Step 6: Hel's own hand, obeying the command, stabs herself.
* Step 7: Conclude that the enemy's greatest weapon is also her greatest vulnerability.

Conclusion: This is one of Siesta's most elegant tactical victories. She does not overpower Hel – she out-thinks her. By exploiting the logical flaw in Hel's ability (no self-exemption), and using a simple tool (a mirror), she turns Hel's own power against her. This is pure deduction translated into combat.

Key Dialogue (Paraphrased):

Hel: "You shall remain still and be stabbed by me..."
Hel stabbed her own chest. "Why...?"
Siesta: "Check."

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Counter-Strategy: S (turns enemy's ability against herself)
Environmental Awareness: A (uses mirror as tool)
Psychological Exploitation: S (understands the ability's self-targeting vulnerability)
Combat Deduction: S (executes under extreme pressure - after intentionally ramming Sirius into Betelgeuse which was at the roof of the BIG BEN and falling from the roof as well after that - Broke her leg due to the landing impact. Also note that the BIG BEN is 96 Meters tall.)

EVENT 3: The Alicia / Hel Split Personality Case – London to Island

Context: After Hel is defeated (stabbed by her own sword), she disappears. Soon after, Kimizuka finds an amnesiac girl sleeping in a cardboard box in a London alley. She calls herself Alicia and claims to be 17 but looks 12-13. Siesta takes her in as a "substitute detective" while recovering from her injuries. Over two weeks, Siesta observes Alicia, tests her, and ultimately deduces that Alicia and Hel are the same person – two personalities in one body.

Feat 12: Recognizing Alicia as Hel's Split Personality through Behavioral Observation and Testing

Over two weeks, Siesta notices inconsistencies in Alicia's behavior, memories, and physical traits. Alicia has no memories but claims to be 17 (Hel's age). She wears an eyepatch over her left eye – the same eye that was Hel's red eye. She is strangely familiar with Kimizuka's injured left eye (which Hel caused). Siesta creates a test: she sends Alicia to find the "Sapphire Eye," a legendary artifact that does not exist. Alicia returns with an eyepatch for Kimizuka, not the artifact – showing she understood the real need was to protect Kimizuka's eye, not find a fake treasure. This confirms that Alicia has Hel's knowledge and body.

Premises:

1. Temporal-Coincidence Premise: Alicia appeared immediately after Hel's defeat and disappearance. This is not a coincidence; Hel's physical body became Alicia's body.
2. Age-Inconsistency Premise: Alicia claims to be 17 but looks 12-13. Hel is 17. This suggests the appearance is altered (via Cerberus's transformation ability, which Hel inherited), but the underlying age is correct.
3. Eyepatch-Anomaly Premise: Alicia wears an eyepatch over her left eye – the exact eye that was Hel's red eye (source of her mind control ability). This is likely to hide the distinguishing feature.
4. Injury-Knowledge Premise: Alicia knows that Kimizuka's left eye was injured (by Hel during their battle) without being told. This indicates she has Hel's memories.
5. Fake-Mission Test Premise: Siesta sends Alicia to find the non-existent "Sapphire Eye." If Alicia were a genuine amnesiac with no connection to Hel, she would either fail to find anything or bring back a random sapphire. Instead, she brings an eyepatch for Kimizuka – showing she understood the real objective was to help his injured eye, which Hel would know.
6. Memory-Loss Pattern Premise: Alicia's amnesia is selective – she remembers being "in a dark room" and "wanting to go to school." This matches the experience of a suppressed personality (Alicia) emerging while the dominant personality (Hel) is unconscious.

Chain of Reasoning:

* Step 1: Note temporal correlation: Hel disappears, Alicia appears.
* Step 2: Observe physical consistency: same age, same left eye covered.
* Step 3: Test memory retention: Alicia knows about Kimizuka's eye injury.
* Step 4: Design fake mission: "Find the Sapphire Eye."
* Step 5: Analyze Alicia's response: she returns with an eyepatch, not a gem.
* Step 6: Interpret response: she knew the mission was fake but wanted to help Kimizuka's real injury.
* Step 7: Conclude that Alicia shares Hel's body and some of Hel's knowledge, but with a different personality.

Conclusion: Siesta's diagnosis of dissociative identity disorder is not clinical guesswork – it is based on a systematic observation and testing protocol. She treats Alicia as a case to be solved, using behavioral experiments to confirm her hypothesis. This is detective work at the level of psychological profiling.

Key Dialogue (Paraphrased):

Alicia gives Kimizuka an eyepatch for his left eye. "You can't heal if you don't wear an eyepatch."
Siesta: "Is this correct?" (testing)
Alicia: "Th- that is correct."
Siesta: "You're really bad at lying."
Later, Alicia confesses: "I think there's another person inside me... I should be the one 'inside' – that's why I didn't have any memory."

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Clinical Psychological Deduction: S (diagnoses dissociative identity disorder)
Long-term Observation: S (two weeks of monitoring)
Testing Strategy: A (creates fake mission to confirm hypothesis)
Behavioral Analysis: S

Feat 13: Understanding Hel's True Motivation – Desire for Father's Love

During the final battle on the island, Siesta engages Hel in psychological combat. Hel insists she is following "destiny" and the "Bible" of SPES. Siesta counters that Hel's real motivation is not ideological but emotional: she wants the love and recognition of her "father" – SEED, the plant entity that created her. Hel's rage when confronted confirms the deduction.

Premises:

1. Emotional-Volatility Premise: Hel becomes disproportionately angry when questioned about her motives. A true ideologue would respond with calm conviction, not rage.
2. Repetitive-Phrasing Premise: Hel repeats the same phrases ("destiny," "mission," "the Bible") without elaboration. This suggests she is reciting a script, not expressing genuine belief.
3. Behavioral-Contradiction Premise: Hel switches to Alicia's personality to avoid being shot – showing she values her own life and wants to share pain, not just complete a mission. This is inconsistent with a fanatic.
4. Origin-Story Premise: Hel was born from Alicia's "pain" as a defensive personality. Her entire existence is a response to suffering, not an ideological choice.
5. Parent-Child Dynamic Premise: Hel refers to SEED as "father" and desperately seeks his approval. The way she speaks about him has a childlike quality, not soldier-like obedience.
6. Confirmation-by-Reaction Premise: When Siesta says "you wanted your father's love," Hel screams "No!" and attacks wildly – the classic reaction of a truth that has been exposed.

Chain of Reasoning:

* Step 1: Observe Hel's emotional instability when questioned.
* Step 2: Note that she parrots SPES doctrine without personal investment.
* Step 3: Recall that she was born from Alicia's pain – a defensive creation, not a recruited soldier.
* Step 4: Listen to how she speaks about SEED: "father," "recognition," "mission."
* Step 5: Infer that her need for SEED's approval is the real drive, not abstract "destiny."
* Step 6: State the deduction directly to provoke a reaction.
* Step 7: Observe the reaction (rage, loss of composure) – confirmation.

Conclusion: Siesta's psychological profiling of Hel reveals that the enemy is not a monster but a damaged child seeking parental love. This understanding is not just academic – it informs the final strategy of saving Alicia by suppressing Hel, rather than killing the body. Siesta chooses redemption over destruction because she understands Hel's humanity.

Key Dialogue (Paraphrased):

Siesta: "You never wanted to be part of SPES, no?"
Hel: "…I told you many times that I am just obeying destiny…"
Siesta: "You are a new existence born out of Alicia's defensive instincts – in that case, there is no way you can have any instincts born out of SPES. That's why you're so desperate to be a member of SPES – you are just a fake."
Siesta: "You wanted the love of your father, no? You just wanted your father's love."

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Psychological Analysis: S (identifies deep emotional motivation)
Empathic Deduction: A (recognizes need for love behind violent actions)
Behavioral Pattern Recognition: S
Interrogation Strategy: A (uses provocation to confirm hypothesis)

Feat 14: The Bond-Based Counter to Hel's Mind Control – Trust as Immunity

During the final battle, Hel tries to make Siesta kill herself using her red eyes. Siesta raises a gun to her own temple. But when Kimizuka says "Siesta, you won't die," she lowers the gun. She explains that she trusts Kimizuka more than she trusts herself. Hel's ability works by overriding the target's own will – but if the target's trust in an external person is stronger than their self-trust, that external voice can override the override. This mutual trust makes both of them immune to Hel's control as long as they can speak to each other.

Premises:

1. Mechanism-Premise: Hel's ability does not create new commands ex nihilo; it suppresses the target's own will and substitutes Hel's command. The target still has a will – it is just being overridden.
2. Trust-Hierarchy Premise: If a person has an external source of trust that they value more than their own judgment, that external voice can become the new "anchor" for their will.
3. Mutual-Reliance Premise: Siesta trusts Kimizuka more than herself. Kimizuka trusts Siesta more than himself. This is reciprocal.
4. Counter-Command Premise: When Kimizuka says "Siesta, you won't die," that statement – coming from the person she trusts most – carries more weight than her own internal voice or Hel's external command.
5. Linguistic-Override Premise: The command from the trusted person does not need to be a direct counter-command ("don't kill yourself") – it can be an affirmation ("you won't die") that contradicts the suicidal command.

Chain of Reasoning:

* Step 1: Analyze Hel's ability as will-suppression, not will-elimination.
* Step 2: Recognize that a sufficiently strong external anchor can resist suppression.
* Step 3: Identify that she and Kimizuka have developed mutual trust over three years.
* Step 4: Formalize that trust as "we trust each other more than we trust ourselves."
* Step 5: When Hel commands suicide, Kimizuka's voice provides the counter-anchor.
* Step 6: Lower the gun, demonstrating immunity.
* Step 7: Conclude that the bond itself is a weapon against mind control.

Conclusion: This is a metafictional level of reasoning – Siesta deduces that the narrative trope of "trust" is actually a tactical asset. She weaponizes the relationship she built with Kimizuka over three years. The bond is not just emotional; it is a counter-measure to psychological attack.

Key Dialogue (Paraphrased):

Hel: "You shall kill yourself now…!"
Siesta raised the gun to her temple.
Kimizuka: "Siesta, you won't die."
Siesta immediately put down the gun.
Siesta: "It's simple. I trust my Assistant, more than anyone else – even myself. Even if my consciousness is aware that I should die, if his words refute it, I will believe it without hesitation. That is all."

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Psychological Counter-Strategy: S (uses trust as weapon against mind control)
Relational Intelligence: S (understands the power of their bond)
Meta-Cognitive Reasoning: A (recognizes that self-trust can be outsourced)
Combat Deduction: S (executes under enemy fire)

EVENT 4: The Final Plan – Consciousness Transfer via Heart Transplant (Island, One Year Ago)
Context: In the climactic battle on the island, Hel takes Siesta's heart. Siesta had planned this from the beginning. Her heart is "specially made" – it can retain her consciousness. When Hel takes it, Siesta's consciousness transfers into Hel's body. From inside, she suppresses Hel's personality, allowing Alicia's personality to eventually emerge. She also gives the new personality a name: Nagisa Natsunagi.

Feat 15: The Ultimate Long-Term Strategy – Dying to Save Alicia and Create a New Detective

Siesta reveals that she knew the outcome of the battle before it began. She allowed Hel to take her heart because her heart has a unique property: it can store her consciousness. Once inside Hel's body, her consciousness can suppress Hel from within. Over time, Hel will fade, and Alicia (the original, suppressed personality) will become dominant. Siesta also ensures that a year later, Kimizuka will meet the new personality (Nagisa Natsunagi) and continue the fight against SPES. This is the ultimate "solve the incident before it happens" – she solved her own death a year in advance.

Premises:

1. Heart-Property Premise: Siesta's heart is "specially made" – it can retain her consciousness post-mortem and transfer it to a recipient via transplant.
2. Consciousness-Transfer Premise: When Hel takes Siesta's heart, Siesta's consciousness will travel with it into Hel's body.
3. Personality-Suppression Premise: Once inside Hel's body, Siesta's consciousness can suppress Hel's personality from within, much like Hel originally suppressed Alicia.
4. Alicia-Emergence Premise: With Hel suppressed, the original personality (Alicia) will eventually become dominant.
5. New-Identity Premise: Alicia needs a new name and life to escape her past. Siesta chooses "Nagisa Natsunagi" – a name that evokes summer, warmth, and a new beginning.
6. Succession-Premise: Kimizuka will need a new detective to work with. Nagisa Natsunagi will take over Siesta's role, continuing the fight against SPES.
7. Memory-Loss Contingency Premise: The pollen from Betelgeuse will cause Kimizuka to lose his memories of the final battle, but Siesta trusts that he will remember her and continue the mission.

Chain of Reasoning:

* Step 1: Foresee that the battle with Hel cannot be won by conventional means (killing her would kill Alicia).
* Step 2: Recognize that her heart has unique properties that can be used for consciousness transfer.
* Step 3: Plan to have Hel take her heart, allowing Siesta to enter Hel's body.
* Step 4: From inside, suppress Hel's personality.
* Step 5: Allow Alicia to emerge as the dominant personality.
* Step 6: Give Alicia a new identity (Nagisa Natsunagi) so she can live a normal life.
* Step 7: Ensure that Kimizuka will meet her a year later, forming a new detective-assistant pair.
* Step 8: Use the pollen's memory-loss effect to protect Kimizuka from the trauma of her death.
* Step 9: Conclude that this is the only way to save Alicia, defeat Hel, and continue the mission.

Conclusion: This is the single most impressive cognitive feat in the entire volume. Siesta not only foresees her own death but orchestrates it as a strategic move. She turns death into a victory condition. The plan spans a full year, involves multiple parties (Kimizuka, Hel, Alicia, Fuubi-san, Charl), and requires precise knowledge of biology (heart consciousness transfer), psychology (split personality suppression), and logistics (ensuring Kimizuka meets Natsunagi). She is the "first-rate detective" who solves the incident before it happens – even when the incident is her own death.

Key Dialogue (Paraphrased):

Siesta (in Hel's body): "Actually, my heart is specially made, you know? For example, I can place my consciousness in my heart, and can retain my sense of self even in other bodies."
Kimizuka: "Then, you!"
Siesta: "Yes, this is the only way. I invaded Hel to suppress her consciousness. This is the only way to fend her off."
Siesta: "Did I not say it before? A true famous detective will solve the incident before it happens – I knew long ago that the situation would end up this way."
Later (Siesta's final monologue): "Remember the name of the person who shall wake you from your sleep – Nagisa. Nagisa Natsunagi."

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Long-term Strategic Planning: SSS (plans her own death and resurrection a year in advance)
Biological/Medical Deduction: S (understands heart transplant consciousness transfer)
Succession Planning: SSS (creates a new detective to continue her mission)
Sacrificial Logic: S (willingly dies to save Alicia and defeat Hel)
Causal Foresight: SSS (predicts every step of the battle and its aftermath)

VOLUME 3

EVENT 1: Six Years Ago – The Orphanage on the Island (Flashback)

Context: Six years before the present timeline, Siesta (then around age 11-12) is living in an orphanage that secretly conducts human experiments for SPES. She meets Nagisa (then called "Number 602"), a sickly girl with heart disease who is used as a test subject. Along with another girl named Alicia (peach-haired inventor), Siesta begins investigating the facility's secrets. This flashback is revealed when Hel, summoned via the Infinity Mirror, narrates Nagisa's past.

Feat 1: Deduction of the Facility's Hidden Purpose from the Teddy Bear Listening Device

While visiting Nagisa's wardroom, Siesta picks up a teddy bear – a gift from a wealthy Japanese couple (later revealed to be Saikawa's parents). She unzips the back and finds a small button-battery-like device. She immediately identifies it as a listening device and deduces that the facility is monitoring the children's conversations. Moreover, she has already prepared a dummy recording to replace the conversation – indicating she anticipated surveillance.

Premises (explicit + implicit):

1. Physical-Anomaly Premise: The teddy bear has an unusual lump in its back that is not part of its normal construction. The stitching has been tampered with.
2. Operational-Security Premise: The facility has a documented history of erasing children's memories after failed experiments. To maintain control, the staff would need to know what children are discussing – especially if any are becoming suspicious.
3. Donor-Access Premise: The teddy bear was delivered by the doctor/headmaster, not directly by the donors. The staff had physical access to the bear before giving it to Nagisa, allowing them to plant the device.
4. Technical-Knowledge Premise: Siesta recognizes the small round device as a miniature listening device – a technology she has encountered before (likely from Alicia's inventions or her own background).
5. Counter-Surveillance Premise: The fact that she has already swapped the room's audio feed with a dummy recording proves she had already suspected surveillance before finding the device. The bear discovery confirms her hypothesis.

Chain of Reasoning:

* Step 1: Observe the unusual lump in the teddy bear's back.
* Step 2: Unzip and retrieve the small electronic device.
* Step 3: Recognize the device as a listening bug (based on prior technical knowledge).
* Step 4: Recall that the facility erases children's memories – surveillance is a logical tool to identify which children need erasure.
* Step 5: Infer that the adults are hiding something significant – they wouldn't surveil children without reason.
* Step 6: Note that she had already prepared a dummy recording – indicating she anticipated this possibility.
* Step 7: Conclude that the facility is engaged in secret activities that require monitoring children's conversations, and that the staff cannot be trusted.

Conclusion: The facility is systematically surveilling the children to detect any who are discovering the truth about the human experiments. Siesta's counter-surveillance measures show she had already deduced the presence of monitoring before finding the physical evidence.

Key Dialogue (paraphrased):

Siesta pulls the zipper behind the teddy bear. A small round device falls out.
Alicia: "A listening device. It seems this facility has been hiding some things from us children."
Nagisa: "You mean we're being watched? Did they just hear our conversation?"
Siesta: "It's fine. The conversation in this room has been swapped with a dummy recording."

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Observational Deduction: A (identifies hidden device from minor lump)
Counter-Surveillance: S (already prepared dummy recording)
Pattern Recognition: B (links surveillance to memory erasure)
Technical Knowledge: A (recognizes bug type)

Feat 2: Systematic Investigation Using Alicia's Inventions – Building the Resistance

Over several weeks, Siesta leads a covert investigation of the facility. She uses Alicia's inventions – spying devices, wiretaps, reconnaissance tools – to gather intelligence. She identifies the basement laboratory where SEED keeps tanks of clones (Androids), deduces that the facility is not conducting normal clinical trials but human experiments to implant "seeds" into children, and uncovers that the headmaster is actually SEED – a plant entity from space.

Premises:

1. Tool-Usage Premise: Alicia is a prodigy inventor who can create functional spying equipment (cameras, listening devices, even weapons). Siesta leverages this resource.
2. Temporal-Pattern Premise: Children disappear regularly, always after failed experiments, and survivors have no memory of the missing children – indicating both a high mortality rate and systematic memory erasure.
3. Financial-Anomaly Premise: The facility receives substantial donations from wealthy individuals (like Saikawa's parents) but still requires clinical trial funding. The money is not going to operating expenses – it is funding secret research.
4. Physical-Evidence Premise: The basement contains large tanks with green liquid and humanoid forms – clones of SEED. These are the "Androids."
5. Behavioral-Analysis Premise: The headmaster can transform his appearance and gender at will – impossible for a normal human. This suggests he is not human.

Chain of Reasoning:

* Step 1: Deploy surveillance equipment throughout the facility (via Alicia).
* Step 2: Monitor staff movements, especially at night and during "clinical trials."
* Step 3: Identify the basement elevator as a restricted area with unusual activity.
* Step 4: Infiltrate the basement (during the confrontation scene).
* Step 5: Observe the tanks containing humanoid forms connected by tubes.
* Step 6: Recognize these as clones being grown – "Androids" being manufactured.
* Step 7: Observe the headmaster's transformation ability (from old man to youth to woman).
* Step 8: Deduce that he is not human – a plant entity called SEED, the "Primordial Seed."
* Step 9: Conclude that the facility is a front for SPES to create Androids and find human vessels compatible with the seed.

Conclusion: The orphanage is a secret SPES laboratory where children are used as test subjects to create Androids and find a suitable human vessel for SEED. Siesta's systematic investigation using Alicia's inventions uncovered the entire operation.

Key Dialogue (paraphrased):

Siesta and Nagisa confront SEED in the basement.
Siesta: "Are those Androids?" (pointing to tanks)
SEED transforms: "The 'Primordial SEED' am I. These are all clones cultivated from parts cut off from me."
Nagisa: "For what? War? Money? Why did we have to sacrifice ourselves for you?"
SEED: "What drives me – is this insatiable survival instinct."

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Investigative Reasoning: A (systematic gathering and analysis of evidence)
Pattern Recognition: A (connects donations, disappearances, experiments, and transformation ability)
Resource Utilization: A (uses Alicia's inventions effectively)
Strategic Planning: A (organizes resistance and confrontation)

Feat 3: Recognizing Alicia's Fake Betrayal as Self-Sacrifice (Emotional Deduction)

At the climax of the confrontation, SEED is about to kill Siesta and Nagisa. Suddenly, Alicia (who had been acting as a double agent) reveals she has been working with SEED. She demands that SEED give her the seed instead, and that the other children be released or have their memories erased. Siesta immediately sees through the act: Alicia is not betraying them; she is sacrificing herself to save her friends. By taking the seed herself, she hopes to become the vessel, end the experiments, and protect Siesta and Nagisa.

Premises:

1. Character-Consistency Premise: Alicia has always been kind, protective of other children, and deeply loyal to Siesta and Nagisa. A sudden, complete betrayal is inconsistent with her established personality.
2. Behavioral-Cue Premise: When Alicia makes her demands, she does not look at Siesta or Nagisa. She keeps her back turned or avoids eye contact – classic signs of reluctance or deception.
3. Outcome-Analysis Premise: The demands she makes sound selfish ("Give me the seed," "The other kids are useless") but the actual result would be: Siesta and Nagisa would have their memories erased and be released (alive and safe), while Alicia would bear the risk of the seed experiment alone.
