4. Hundred Demons Warning Premise: Hakutengu explicitly stated that there are multiple such devices: the Holy Scripture, the Clock of the End, the Locked Box, and beings like himself. The holy relics are likely part of this system.
5. Puzzle Analogy Premise: The square indentations on the bottom of both relics suggest they are designed to interlock with other pieces. This implies a larger set exists.
6. Testable Hypothesis Premise: If the relics store lost memories, touching the second relic should restore memories related to the Vampire Rebellion (the next major event after the Magical Girl arc).

Chain of Reasoning:

* Step 1: Observe the second holy relic's shape and note its identity with the first.
* Step 2: Recall that Kimizuko's touch on the first relic restored memories of Reloaded.
* Step 3: Recall Hakutengu's statement about multiple devices recording past/future.
* Step 4: Connect the Doomsday Clock location to Hakutengu's "Clock of the End."
* Step 5: Hypothesize that the second relic contains memories of the Vampire Rebellion.
* Step 6: Direct Kimizuko to touch the relic to test the hypothesis.
* Step 7: Observe that Kimizuko indeed regains those memories.
* Step 8: Conclude that the relics are physical memory-storage devices and part of a larger puzzle set.
* Step 9: Infer that Bruno left the first relic intentionally, and Mia's prophetic dream led her to the second.

Conclusion: Siesta's deduction is a synthesis of physical observation (shape identity), empirical testing (prior memory restoration), and mythological knowledge (Hakutengu's warning). She correctly identifies the relics as fragments of lost world records and recognizes that they are puzzle pieces that must be assembled.

Key Dialogue (paraphrased):

Mia: "This is the Holy Relic I found." (placing reddish-brown triangular pyramid on desk)
Siesta: "It looks just like the one we received from Noël."
Nagisa: "In short, both what Mr. Kimizuka brought and what Lady Mia found there were Holy Relics, ceremonial objects possibly holding the lost memory of the world?"
Siesta: "Yes, considering what we experienced three days ago. That's definitely the case."
(Kimizuko touches the relic and recovers memories)
Nagisa: "What have you seen?"
Kimizuko: "It's just a continuation of last time. After the Magical Girl's case was resolved, it's the Vampire who took the stage."
Siesta: "I see. Hakutengu talked about this too. 'There are several devices scattered around the world recording the past and the future. The Holy Book, the Clock of the End, the Locked Box, even beings like myself. They are meant to forewarn the world.' — This Doomsday Clock that Mia found must be, after all, something that informs us of said dangers."

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Object Deduction: S (identifies the relic's function from shape and prior experience)
Pattern Recognition: A (connects two relics as part of a set)
Synthesis of Disparate Information: S (integrates Hakutengu's warning, Bruno's legacy, Mia's dream, and Kimizuko's memory restoration)
Predictive Testing: A (formulates and confirms hypothesis through empirical action)

Feat 2: Epistemological Analysis – Recognizing the Contradiction Between Kimizuko's Story and Her Own Memory as Evidence of External Tampering

After Kimizuko finishes narrating the recovered memories of the Vampire Rebellion, Siesta identifies a critical epistemological problem. The story contains terms and concepts – the Phantom Thief as a Tuner, Kimizuko's nature as the Singularity, the Akashic Records – that she has no memory of. Yet the story does not feel absurd or fabricated; it "makes sense" in a way that suggests internal consistency. Siesta recognizes that this combination (no memory + feels true) is impossible under normal circumstances. She concludes that someone has tampered with the world's records, not just individual human memories, and that the holy relics bypass this tampering.

Premises:

1. Story Consistency Premise: Kimizuko's story is detailed, internally coherent, and matches the recovered memories of both Reloaded and the Vampire Rebellion – unlikely to be fabricated or delusional.
2. Individual Memory Gap Premise: Siesta has no recollection of the Phantom Thief being one of the twelve Tuners, of the term "Singularity" applied to Kimizuko, or of the "Akashic Records" as a known concept.
3. Cross-Reference Premise: Nagisa and Mia also lack these memories. This is not an individual cognitive error but a shared gap.
4. Non-Absurdity Premise: Despite the memory gap, the story does not feel contradictory or impossible. It has a logical flow that makes it believable.
5. Suspicious Coherence Premise: The feeling of "making sense" despite no prior knowledge is unusual. Normally, a fabricated story would feel false; a true story would trigger recognition. The absence of recognition combined with acceptance suggests external influence.
6. External Tampering Premise: The most plausible explanation is that someone has altered the world's records (documents, databases, even collective memory) to erase certain concepts.
7. Holy Relic Bypass Premise: Kimizuko's ability to remember these concepts after touching the holy relics indicates that the relics are physical artifacts that bypass this tampering.

Chain of Reasoning:

* Step 1: Listen to Kimizuko's complete story of the Vampire Rebellion, noting specific terminology.
* Step 2: Identify three key terms: Phantom Thief (as a Tuner), Singularity (as Kimizuko's nature), Akashic Records (as a world secret).
* Step 3: Check her own memory – all three terms are absent or unrecognizable.
* Step 4: Verify with Nagisa and Mia – they also have no recollection.
* Step 5: Observe that the story does not feel false – it has internal logic and coherence.
* Step 6: Recognize that this combination (no memory + feels true) is impossible under normal conditions.
* Step 7: Infer that the story is true, but the memories have been actively removed or suppressed.
* Step 8: Conclude that someone has tampered with the world's records, not just individual minds.
* Step 9: Note that the holy relics restored Kimizuko's memories, proving they bypass the tampering.
* Step 10: Declare that the world is in a "strange state" and that they must investigate further.

Key Dialogue (paraphrased):

Siesta: "There were several words and lines in Assistant's story that caught my attention." (She lists the three quotes from Mia, Reloaded, and Ice Doll)
"Not just the Akashic Records, I have no recollection of the Phantom Thief, who was meant to be one of the Tuners, and your nature as the Singularity."
Nagisa: "I feel the same way. But the me you described in Kimizuka's story probably knew all of that as a matter of course."
Siesta: "It feels contradicting, yet it somehow makes sense, as if someone is forcing us to accept it."
Nagisa: "Right now, we can't rely on our memories. Our intellect and experience, the weapons we've used so far, are probably useless. And we don't even know who our enemy is."
Siesta: "If that's the case, our plan is decided. Let's embark on a journey to unravel the secrets of the world."

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Memory Discrepancy Detection: S (identifies specific, verifiable contradictions between story and current knowledge)
Metacognitive Analysis: S (recognizes that the feeling of "making sense" is itself a clue to external manipulation)
Epistemological Reasoning: S (questions the reliability of her own memory and perception at a fundamental level)
Consistency Verification: A (cross-references multiple witnesses to confirm the gap)
Tampering Inference: S (deduces that the world's records, not just individual memories, have been altered)

Feat 3: Strategic Framing – Declaring a Journey to Unravel the Secrets of the World

After synthesizing all the evidence – the two holy relics, the memory discrepancies, the Doomsday Clock, Hakutengu's warning, and Bruno's legacy – Siesta announces their course of action. She declares that they cannot rely on their memories, intellect, or experience because those may have been tampered with. The only reliable anchors are physical artifacts like the holy relics that can restore lost memories. She concludes that they must embark on a journey to find the remaining puzzle pieces and restore the lost records of the world. This declaration mirrors the beginning of her original adventure with Kimizuko seven years ago, bringing the story full circle.

Premises:

1. Unreliable Memory Premise: Their current memories cannot be trusted as accurate representations of reality, as demonstrated by the Phantom Thief, Singularity, and Akashic Records gaps.
2. Physical Evidence Premise: The holy relics are physical artifacts that demonstrably restore lost memories when touched. Physical evidence is more reliable than memory.
3. Puzzle Analogy Premise: The square indentations on the relics indicate that they are designed to interlock with other pieces. Similar objects must exist elsewhere.
4. Action Imperative Premise: Inaction will not resolve the underlying mystery. The world is in a "strange state" (as Siesta said in the prologue), and the crisis Bruno warned about is ongoing.
5. Detective's Duty Premise: As detectives, their job is to uncover the truth, no matter how unsettling or dangerous.
6. Team Capability Premise: The team (Siesta, Nagisa, Kimizuko, Mia, Noël, and potentially others) has the skills, resources, and connections to undertake such a journey.

Chain of Reasoning:

* Step 1: Recognize that they cannot trust their own memories, intellect, or experience – all may have been compromised.
* Step 2: Acknowledge that conventional investigative methods (interviews, document review, database searches) may be useless because the records themselves have been altered.
* Step 3: Identify the holy relics as reliable anchors – they restored Kimizuko's memories, proving that physical artifacts can bypass the tampering.
* Step 4: Note the square indentations on the relics – they are puzzle pieces designed to connect with others.
* Step 5: Infer that similar objects must exist elsewhere in the world, possibly held by other Tuners or hidden in significant locations.
* Step 6: Conclude that the only way forward is to actively search for these pieces and assemble them.
* Step 7: Announce the plan: a journey to unravel the secrets of the world.
* Step 8: Frame it as a detective's mission, not a burden – a continuation of their work.
* Step 9: Invite the team to join, referencing the original adventure seven years ago to create a sense of continuity.

Key Dialogue (paraphrased):

Nagisa: "Right now, we can't rely on our memories. Our intellect and experience, the weapons we've used so far, are probably useless. And we don't even know who our enemy is."
Siesta: "If that's the case, our plan is decided. Let's embark on a journey to unravel the secrets of the world."
(She walks to the window, turns back, and smiles)
Siesta: "Who will we face next? An android, an alien, a vampire, or perhaps an unknown enemy? The nature of the case didn't matter. As detectives, we had always taken on many mysteries mixed together like a hotpot. The ultimate mystery, however, must be a story that unravels the mysteries hidden in the world itself."

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Strategic Resolution: S (formulates a clear, actionable plan from ambiguous and incomplete data)
Long-term Planning: A (commits to a journey to find puzzle pieces across the world)
Team Leadership: A (unites the group around a common mission)
Narrative Framing: B (connects the new journey to the original adventure seven years ago)
Risk Assessment: A (acknowledges the danger but chooses action over inaction)

EVENT 2: The Epilogue – Post-Vampire Rebellion Analysis and Phantom Thief Deduction

Context: After Kimizuko finishes telling the story of the Vampire Rebellion (recovered via the second holy relic), Siesta, Nagisa, Mia, and Olivia discuss the implications. They identify the Phantom Thief Arsene (also known as Abel A. Schönberg) as the likely culprit behind the world's memory loss. Siesta also reveals that she has been unable to contact Charlotte Arisaka Anderson for two weeks, and that Charlotte left a pre-scheduled message directing them to find Kase Fuubi (the Assassin). This sets up the next arc.

Feat 4: Historical Retrospective – Acknowledging Siesta's Foreknowledge of Scarlet's Fate and Her Decision to Entrust the Resolution

When Mia mentions that Siesta must have sensed that Scarlet would someday become an "Enemy of the World," Siesta admits partial foreknowledge. She knew from Scarlet himself that he intended to make her his "Bride" to break the short-life curse on vampires. She also knew that his goal was to enable his clan to be reborn as humans. However, she did not know the full context – specifically, that his true bride was Marley (his childhood friend Jeanne) and that his original wish was to protect her as a human. Siesta acknowledges that she could not judge whether Scarlet's plan was good or evil – it was a moral gray area. Therefore, she entrusted the matter to Nagisa and Kimizuko, trusting them to find the truth and make the right choice. She thanks them for doing what she could not.

Premises:

1. Scarlet's Direct Disclosure Premise: Scarlet had told Siesta directly about his plan to make her his "Bride" (due to her special heart) to break the vampire curse. He did not, however, disclose Marley's existence.
2. Goal Recognition Premise: Siesta understood that Scarlet's ultimate goal was to enable vampires to be reborn as humans – to break the short-lifespan curse imposed on his race.
3. Moral Ambiguity Premise: Siesta could not determine whether this goal was just or evil. It was a gray area – a desire for species survival versus potential harm to humans.
4. Incomplete Information Premise: She did not know about Marley, the childhood friend he revived, or that she was the true object of his wish.
5. Entrustment Premise: Rather than intervening prematurely or making a unilateral judgment, Siesta left the matter to Nagisa and Kimizuko, trusting their judgment and abilities.
6. Mia's Observation Premise: Mia notes that Siesta must have sensed Scarlet would become an "Enemy of the World," even though it wasn't in the Holy Scripture – indicating Siesta's predictive intuition.
7. Gratitude Premise: After learning the full story, Siesta thanks Nagisa and Kimizuko for doing what she could not – uncovering the truth and granting Marley's wish.

Chain of Reasoning:

* Step 1: Recall that Scarlet had proposed making her his "Bride" and explained his plan to break the vampire curse.
* Step 2: Recognize that his goal – species survival – was not inherently evil, nor was it clearly just.
* Step 3: Acknowledge that she lacked complete information (she didn't know about Marley or his original wish).
* Step 4: Decide not to intervene directly, trusting that Nagisa and Kimizuko would find the truth.
* Step 5: Observe that they ultimately resolved the case – they uncovered Marley's identity, confronted Scarlet, and granted the client's wish.
* Step 6: Recognize that her trust was justified.
* Step 7: Thank them for doing what she could not do herself.

Key Dialogue (paraphrased):

Mia: "You must have sensed it too, Senpai. That Vampire would someday become an 'Enemy of the world'. Even though it wasn't even directly referenced in the Holy Scripture."
Siesta: "I didn't know everything either. … Like, I didn't even know about his true bride. I expected that Scarlet would someday bare his fangs at the world. But his goal was to break the short life curse and make his clan reborn as humans. I couldn't judge whether that was good or evil at that time. You guys did what I couldn't do. Let me thank you again."

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Self-Awareness of Limits: A (acknowledges incomplete knowledge)
Moral Judgment Abstention: B (recognizes gray areas and refrains from premature judgment)
Trust-based Delegation: A (entrusts resolution to successors)
Historical Reflection: B
Predictive Intuition: A (Mia's observation that Siesta sensed Scarlet's fate)

Feat 5: Synthetic Deduction – Identifying the Phantom Thief as the Culprit Behind the World's Memory Loss

In the epilogue discussion, Siesta and the team synthesize information from multiple sources to identify the Phantom Thief Arsene (also known as Abel A. Schönberg) as the likely mastermind behind the world's memory loss. Key pieces of evidence include: (1) Fuubi's hypothesis that Arsene may be the infamous criminal Abel; (2) Stephen's discovery of unknown elements in the Seven Deadly Sins demons (elements not from this world, possibly stolen concepts); (3) The known ability of Arsene to steal intangible things (hearts, wills, even memories); (4) The fact that the Phantom Thief, Singularity, and Akashic Records are precisely the concepts that have been erased from the world's records; (5) The holy relics bypass this theft because they are physical artifacts.

Premises:

1. Fuubi's Hypothesis Premise: Fuubi (the Assassin) has been investigating the Phantom Thief and believes that Arsene's true identity may be Abel A. Schönberg – the world's most infamous criminal, behind countless unsolved cases. This hypothesis was shared with Kimizuko and relayed to Siesta.
2. Stephen's Analysis Premise: Stephen Bluefield (the Inventor) analyzed the body of Greed (one of the Seven Deadly Sins) and detected a substance that cannot be considered to be from this world – unknown elements not in the periodic table. Similar unknown elements were found in Yggdrasil (where the Primordial Seed was sealed).
3. Concept Theft Premise: The Phantom Thief's known ability is to steal anything – including intangible things like human wills and hearts. He can steal concepts and common sense itself.
4. Memory Gap Correspondence Premise: The concepts that are missing from the world's records (Phantom Thief as a Tuner, Singularity, Akashic Records) are precisely the types of things the Phantom Thief would have an interest in stealing.
5. Holy Relic Bypass Premise: The holy relics are physical objects that restored Kimizuko's memories. If the Phantom Thief steals concepts, physical artifacts may survive the theft.
6. Charlotte's Message Premise: Charlotte left a pre-scheduled message directing Siesta to find Fuubi if she couldn't be contacted. Fuubi has been chasing Abel, so this connects.

Chain of Reasoning:

* Step 1: Recall Fuubi's hypothesis that Arsene = Abel A. Schönberg.
* Step 2: Recall Stephen's detection of unknown elements in the Seven Deadly Sins demons.
* Step 3: Recognize that the Seven Deadly Sins were created or manipulated by Arsene (as previously hypothesized).
* Step 4: Recall that Arsene can steal intangible things – hearts, wills, memories.
* Step 5: Note that the missing concepts (Phantom Thief, Singularity, Akashic Records) are exactly the kind of high-level knowledge he would want to erase.
* Step 6: Infer that Arsene is the one who tampered with the world's records.
* Step 7: Conclude that the holy relics survived because they are physical artifacts, not just conceptual knowledge.
* Step 8: Decide that they need to find Fuubi to learn more – she has been chasing Abel and may have critical information.

Key Dialogue (paraphrased):

Mia: "There was a word in this story that I'm not very familiar with either. Phantom Thief, is it?"
Kimizuko: "Yeah, that's probably what interested us most right now. Phantom Thief Arsene. Also known as Abel A. Schönberg. He is the twelfth Tuner that I have forgotten."
Nagisa: "Why did I forget him? Who made me forget him?"
Kimizuko: "You were talking to Ms. Fuubi about it. Phantom Thief can steal not only tangible objects but anything they want. That's right. Phantom Thief can steal even people's hearts and abstract concepts from where they belong, overturn common sense. If we assume that… Is the anomaly happening in this world now created by Phantom Thief? Did he steal memories from humans and the world alike?"
Nagisa: "But how did Phantom Thief do such a thing? And for what purpose?"
Kimizuko: "That is… I don't know."

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Synthetic Deduction: S (connects Fuubi's intel, Stephen's analysis, known abilities, and memory gaps to identify the culprit)
Conceptual Theft Recognition: S (understands that intangible concepts can be stolen)
Conspiracy Mapping: A (links multiple disparate incidents – memory loss, missing records, unknown elements – to a single mastermind)
Evidence Integration: S (synthesizes testimony, scientific analysis, and known abilities)

Feat 6: Strategic Division of Labor – Planning the Search for Fuubi and Charlotte

After receiving Charlotte's pre-scheduled message (directing them to find Fuubi if she can't be contacted), Siesta agrees to split the team. She proposes that she, Nagisa, and Mia will search for Fuubi (the Assassin), while Kimizuko investigates Charlotte's disappearance alone. This division leverages each person's strengths: the female detectives have the authority and connections to track a Tuner; Kimizuko has a personal connection to Charlotte. The ultimate goal is to find Fuubi, who holds the key to both the Phantom Thief case and Charlotte's location.

Premises:

1. Charlotte's Message Premise: Charlotte pre-scheduled a message to Siesta before the Ceremony of Holy Return, stating: "If you can't contact me when this message arrives, please look for Kase Fuubi."
2. Charlotte's Silence Premise: Neither Siesta nor Kimizuko has been able to contact Charlotte for two weeks. This is unusual, as Charlotte typically maintains some communication.
3. Fuubi's Knowledge Premise: Fuubi has been investigating Abel A. Schönberg (the Phantom Thief) and may know where Charlotte is or what happened to her.
4. Resource Allocation Premise: The team has two separate but related tasks: find Fuubi and find Charlotte. They cannot be in two places at once.
5. Individual Strengths Premise: Siesta, Nagisa, and Mia have the authority as Tuners (or former Tuners) and connections to track another Tuner (Fuubi). Kimizuko has a personal history with Charlotte and may have insights into her movements.
6. Priority Premise: Finding Fuubi is the higher priority because she is the key to both the Phantom Thief case and Charlotte's location.

Chain of Reasoning:

* Step 1: Receive Charlotte's pre-scheduled message (read by Siesta).
* Step 2: Note that Charlotte cannot be contacted – unusual and concerning.
* Step 3: Recall that Fuubi has been chasing Abel A. Schönberg (the Phantom Thief).
* Step 4: Infer that Fuubi may have information about Charlotte's whereabouts.
* Step 5: Recognize that the team cannot cover both objectives simultaneously.
* Step 6: Propose splitting the team into two groups.
* Step 7: Assign roles based on strengths: the detectives (Siesta, Nagisa, Mia) to track Fuubi; Kimizuko alone to investigate Charlotte.
* Step 8: Note that Mia's suggestion of a "girl team" and a "boy team" is a practical (and slightly humorous) division.
* Step 9: Conclude that finding Fuubi is the priority – she holds the answers.

Key Dialogue (paraphrased):

Siesta: "Actually, I haven't been able to contact Charl lately. It's been about two weeks now. I don't even know where she is right now."
Kimizuko: "Siesta too? I tried sending her a message once after the Ceremony of Holy Return, but she didn't even read it."
(Later)
Siesta: "I said I haven't been able to contact Charl earlier, but actually I got a message from her that seems to be scheduled in advance. She might have prepared it just before the Ceremony of Holy Return. 'If you can't contact me when this message arrives, please look for Kase Fuubi.'"
Nagisa: "That means Ms. Fuubi might know something about Charl's whereabouts."
Mia: "There are two problems here. Shall we split up? A team to look for Charl and a team to chase Fuubi?"
Kimizuko: "But then you guys will fight over me, won't you?"
Nagisa: "Okay, let's split up into a girl team with me and Siesta and Mia, and a boy team with Kimihiko alone."
Kimizuko: "Let's go arrest the cop. I'll think of the charges when I find her."

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Strategic Planning: A (efficient division of labor)
Information Integration: B (uses Charlotte's message as a key clue)
Team Coordination: A (assigns roles based on strengths and relationships)
Priority Assessment: A (correctly identifies Fuubi as the priority)
Humorous Reframing: C (not a cognitive feat, but notable for character)

Tier Justification:

* S feats: Feats 1, 2, 3, 5 – all demonstrate high-level deductive, epistemological, synthetic, and strategic reasoning. These are the core cognitive feats of the volume.
* A feats: Feats 4 and 6 – solid self-awareness, delegation, and strategic planning, but less exceptional than the S-tier feats.

The Most Significant Feat: Feat 2 (Recognizing the Memory Discrepancy)

This is the cognitive highlight of the volume. Siesta does not simply accept Kimizuko's story or dismiss it – she analyzes the relationship between the story and her own memory at a meta-level. She identifies specific terms that are missing from her recollection yet notes that the story "makes sense." She recognizes that this combination (no memory + feels true) is impossible under normal circumstances, leading her to conclude that someone has tampered with the world's records. This is a second-order epistemological deduction – she is not just solving a mystery; she is diagnosing the unreliability of her own cognitive apparatus. This feat sets the stage for the entire "lost records" arc and establishes the Phantom Thief as the primary antagonist.

This volume serves as a transitional bridge between the "Vampire Rebellion" arc (concluded in this volume's flashback) and the forthcoming "Phantom Thief" arc. Siesta's cognitive feats are less about in-the-moment case-solving and more about metacognitive diagnosis and strategic direction-setting. She identifies the true enemy (the Phantom Thief), recognizes the mechanism of the world's memory loss (concept theft), and sets the team on the path to confront him. The holy relics become the key to restoring lost knowledge, and the hunt for Fuubi becomes the next priority. Siesta's role has evolved from active detective to strategic coordinator – she is now the leader of a team investigating the very fabric of reality. Her acknowledgment that she entrusted the Vampire Rebellion resolution to Nagisa and Kimizuko demonstrates her growth from a solitary genius to a mentor who trusts her successors.

VOLUME 10

Siesta is physically unconscious for most of the volume, lying in a hospital bed awaiting heart surgery. However, she appears in three critical sections: (1) the prologue (active, searching ruins with Kimizuka), (2) a dream sequence where her consciousness interacts with Kimizuka, and (3) the final epilogue (active, discussing recovered memories). Her cognitive feats in this volume are therefore concentrated but exceptionally sharp.

EVENT 1: The Ruins Search – Finding Kase Fuubi and the Third Holy Relic (From the Future: A Prologue)

Context: Siesta and Kimizuka are searching an abandoned, plant‑covered mansion in rural America for Kase Fuubi, a former assassin who escaped prison. They need her help recovering lost memories related to “Phantom Thief.” After a brief knife fight that ends in a draw, Siesta reveals a hypothesis about the Holy Relics – mysterious objects that restore lost memories – and uses a geometric pattern in their locations to predict where the third relic lies. She then uses auditory perception to locate the hidden relic beneath the floor. She also negotiates with the distrustful Fuubi without demanding trust.

Feat 1.1: Spatio‑Geometric Pattern Recognition – The 10,000 km Hypothesis

Siesta presents a hypothesis based on three data points about Holy Relic locations. She demonstrates that the first two relics were each exactly 10,000 km from a certain point in Japan, and argues that their current location in America – also 10,000 km from that same point – should contain the third relic. This requires calculating or verifying large distances, recognizing a circular pattern, and inferring intentional design.

Premises:

1. First Relic Location Premise: The first Holy Relic was found at Bruno Belmondo’s mansion. (Exact coordinates unknown to the reader, but Siesta knows them from previous investigation.)
2. Second Relic Location Premise: The second Holy Relic was found next to the Doomsday Clock. (Mia Whitlock recovered it; Siesta learned its location from her.)
3. Reference Point Premise: There exists a specific, unidentified point in Japan (the Hokuriku region, as later revealed) that serves as the center for this pattern.
4. Distance Measurement Premise 1: The distance from the Japanese reference point to Bruno’s mansion is exactly 10,000 km.
5. Distance Measurement Premise 2: The distance from the same Japanese reference point to the Doomsday Clock is also exactly 10,000 km.
6. Distance Measurement Premise 3: The distance from the Japanese reference point to their current American location is also exactly 10,000 km.
7. Statistical Improbability Premise: Three independent points all lying exactly 10,000 km from the same center is highly improbable by chance. The probability of even two such coincidences is low; three is effectively zero without intentional placement.
8. Geometric Constraint Premise: Points at a fixed distance from a common center lie on a circle of that radius. Therefore, all three locations are on the same circumference.
9. Intentional Design Premise: The most parsimonious explanation for such a precise geometric pattern is that someone (or something) deliberately placed the relics at these coordinates.
10. Guidance Premise: The phrase “He guided us here” (uttered by Kimizuka and affirmed by Siesta) indicates that Bruno Belmondo – or his posthumous will – is the orchestrator.

Chain of Reasoning:

* Step 1: Retrieve from memory the exact locations of the first two Holy Relics (Bruno’s mansion, Doomsday Clock) and the Japanese reference point.
* Step 2: Calculate or verify the great‑circle distances between the Japanese point and each relic location. Both yield 10,000 km.
* Step 3: Note that these are not approximate but exact – a significant detail suggesting measurement rather than estimation.
* Step 4: Extend the calculation to their current American location. Determine that it also lies exactly 10,000 km from the Japanese point.
* Step 5: Recognize that three distinct points sharing the same distance from a common center form a circle of radius 10,000 km.
* Step 6: Calculate the probability of three randomly chosen locations all satisfying this condition. Conclude it is astronomically low.
* Step 7: Infer that the pattern is not random but intentional – someone designed the relic placements.
* Step 8: Consider who could have such knowledge and motive. Bruno Belmondo, the Information Broker who orchestrated the Tuner system, is the most likely candidate.
* Step 9: Conclude that the third Holy Relic should be located somewhere on this circle. Since they are already at a point on that circle, the relic is likely nearby – specifically, in the ruins they are searching.
* Step 10: State the hypothesis to Fuubi and Kimizuka, noting that the pattern “has meaning.”
* Step 11: When Fuubi asks about the Japanese point, Siesta replies “Well, you’ll understand if you look at a map later” – implying that the reference point is identifiable but she is keeping it confidential (or wants Fuubi to discover it herself).

Conclusion: The third Holy Relic is present in the American ruins. Siesta uses spatial pattern recognition to justify searching this location, and indeed they find it. The pattern later helps them understand that Bruno guided their journey.

Key Dialogue (paraphrased):

Siesta (raising her index finger): “We’ve found two Holy Relics so far. I predict the third one is here. Starting from a certain point in Japan, the first Holy Relic was found at Bruno’s mansion. Exactly 10,000 kilometers away. Mia found the second one next to the Doomsday Clock. 10,000 kilometers too. And now, we’re here. It’s not much of a hypothesis, but I think it has meaning.”

Fuubi: “So you guess my location based on that?”

Siesta (looking pleased): “Well, you’ll understand if you look at a map later.”

Kimizuka: “He guided us here.”

Siesta (nodding): “Yes.”

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Spatial Intelligence: S - Identifies a 10,000 km radius pattern without modern mapping tools; works with great‑circle distances mentally.
Pattern Recognition: S - Detects a geometric relationship from only three data points.
Quantitative Reasoning: A - Handles large numbers and circle geometry; understands exact vs. approximate distances.
Abductive Reasoning: S - Infers intentional design (Bruno’s guidance) from the pattern.
Statistical Reasoning: B - Implicitly calculates improbability of random coincidence.

Feat 1.2: Auditory Detection of a Hidden Compartment

While searching the abandoned mansion, Siesta stomps on the floor multiple times and listens for acoustic differences. She identifies a spot where the sound is distinct – hollow, more resonant – and correctly deduces a cavity beneath. This leads directly to the discovery of the third Holy Relic.

Premises:

1. Acoustic Principle Premise: Different materials and structural configurations produce different sound resonances when struck. Solid ground produces a dull, short thud; a hollow cavity produces a longer, lower‑pitched echo.
2. Floor Construction Premise: The mansion’s floor is made of consistent material (likely wood or stone) across most of the room, so any acoustic variation is unlikely to be due to surface differences.
3. Hollow Space Premise: A void beneath a floor (e.g., a basement, crawlspace, or hidden compartment) creates a distinct acoustic signature – more reverberant, less dampened.
4. Building Age Premise: The mansion is old, abandoned, and overgrown with plants. Unexpected cavities are more likely to be original hidden compartments (e.g., for storing valuables or secrets) than modern modifications or structural damage.
5. Search Context Premise: They are explicitly searching for a Holy Relic, which is a small, valuable object. It is reasonable to assume it would be hidden rather than left in plain sight.
6. Comparative Method Premise: By stomping across the floor and comparing sounds, she can identify anomalous sections.
7. Safety Premise: If a cavity exists, it could be unstable or trapped. She instructs Fuubi to “get back” before any explosive opening.

Chain of Reasoning:

* Step 1: Begin a systematic search of the mansion, not relying on visual clues alone.
* Step 2: Use stomping as a method to generate controlled acoustic input.
* Step 3: Walk across the floor, stomping at regular intervals, listening for changes in pitch, duration, and resonance.
* Step 4: Compare each stomp’s acoustic response to the baseline (sounds from areas known to be solid).
* Step 5: Identify a specific spot where the sound is noticeably different – “The sound is different here.”
* Step 6: Interpret the difference as indicating a hollow space beneath, not a surface anomaly.
* Step 7: Infer that this hollow space is likely a hidden compartment rather than a natural void, given the building’s original purpose and the search context.
* Step 8: Conclude that the Holy Relic is likely inside or accessible through this cavity.
* Step 9: Signal Fuubi to create a safe distance (five meters), then have her throw an explosive to open the floor.
* Step 10: After the explosion, observe the broken floor revealing a hollow empty space and a small underground passage.
* Step 11: Personally descend into the passage (without hesitation) to retrieve the relic.
* Step 12: Return with a yellowish pyramid‑shaped object – the third Holy Relic.

Conclusion: The Holy Relic was hidden in a concealed underground compartment. Siesta’s auditory perception and rapid interpretation of acoustic cues directly enabled its discovery.

Key Dialogue (paraphrased):

Siesta (stomping on floor several times): “The sound is different here.”

Fuubi (to Kimizuka and herself): “Get back.” (Fuubi throws an explosive; dust rises)

Siesta: “Let’s go get the Holy Relic.”

Kimizuka (resigned): “What are you going to do if it’s broken?”

(Siesta jumps down into the passage, returns with the relic)

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Sensory Perception (Auditory): A - Distinguishes subtle acoustic differences in a noisy, decaying environment.
Deductive Reasoning: A - Connects sound difference → hollow space → hidden compartment → relic location.
Practical Reasoning: B - Takes immediate, decisive action (descending into unknown passage).
Risk Assessment: B - Orders Fuubi to stand back before explosive opening.

Feat 1.3: Strategic Negotiation with Fuubi – Bypassing the “No Trust” Creed

Siesta needs Fuubi’s cooperation to recover lost memories and pursue Abel. However, Fuubi has explicitly stated that she trusts no one and acts only on her own standards. Siesta does not ask for trust; instead, she appeals to shared goals, uses information as currency, and asks specific, non‑threatening questions.

Premises:

1. Fuubi’s Character Premise: Fuubi has been a Tuner for years, operating as an assassin for the Federation Government. Her survival has depended on trusting no one. She explicitly says “Believing no one is my creed.”
2. Shared Goal Premise: Both Siesta and Fuubi want to recover lost memories – especially those related to “Phantom Thief” and the “Akashic Records” – and stop Abel A. Schönberg.
3. Information Asymmetry Premise: Fuubi, having worked with Bruno Belmondo longer, is likely closer to the truth than Siesta. Siesta acknowledges this: “Fuubi, who had been acquainted with Bruno for a long time, was likely nearer to the truth than us.”
4. Bruno’s Will Premise: Bruno entrusted his will to remaining former Tuners, including Fuubi, to continue the fight against the government’s cover‑ups.
5. Charlotte’s Disappearance Premise: Charlotte Arisaka Anderson is missing, and Fuubi may have information about her or be connected to her disappearance.
6. Previous Hostility Premise: Siesta and Fuubi have clashed before (implied by their knife fight ending in a draw). Direct confrontation or emotional appeals will fail.
7. Practical Cooperation Premise: They are currently in the same location, searching for the same relic. Cooperation on this immediate task is mutually beneficial.

Chain of Reasoning:

* Step 1: Observe Fuubi’s defensive posture and her refusal to answer Kimizuka’s question about trust. (“Fuubi didn’t answer my question.”)
* Step 2: Recognize that asking for trust directly is counterproductive – it would trigger Fuubi’s ingrained suspicion.
* Step 3: Instead, frame the interaction not as a partnership based on trust, but as a temporary convergence of interests.
* Step 4: Begin with a low‑stakes, factual question: “Don’t you know about Charlotte? She seemed to be following you.” This is a legitimate inquiry about a missing person, not a demand for allegiance.
* Step 5: When Fuubi denies knowledge (“No idea. I would definitely notice if anyone was tailing me”), accept it without accusation. Do not press or show disappointment.
* Step 6: Then pivot to the shared goal. State: “Really? I thought you and I had the same goal. To recover the lost memories of the past. Make us remember. If you’re working with Bruno, that should be your purpose.”
* Step 7: This statement does several things: (a) it acknowledges that Fuubi may have her own reasons, (b) it links those reasons to Bruno (whom Fuubi respects), (c) it presents Siesta as an ally in that goal, not an adversary.
* Step 8: Ask directly: “Hey, Fuubi. How much do you remember? How much have you recalled?” This question appeals to Fuubi’s own desire for answers, not to any obligation toward Siesta. It is also a factual question about memory, not an emotional appeal.
* Step 9: Fuubi deflects (“You’re looking for a tool to get your memory back, right?”) but does not refuse cooperation. She mentions “Holy Relics,” confirming she has relevant information.
* Step 10: Siesta accepts the deflection and moves on to the practical task of finding the relic. The negotiation is not about winning verbal concessions but about establishing a functional working relationship.

Conclusion: Siesta never forces trust. She creates a working relationship based on mutual utility and shared curiosity. Fuubi does not attack them, shares some information (about Holy Relics), and eventually they work together to find the relic. The interaction is a masterclass in negotiating with a distrustful counterpart.

Key Dialogue (paraphrased):

Siesta: “Don’t you know about Charlotte? She seemed to be following you.”

Fuubi: “No idea. First of all, I would definitely notice if anyone was tailing me.”

(Later, after Siesta explains the Holy Relic pattern)

Siesta: “Really? I thought you and I had the same goal. To recover the lost memories of the past. Make us remember. If you’re working with Bruno, that should be your purpose.”

Siesta: “Hey, Fuubi. How much do you remember? How much have you recalled?”

Fuubi (not answering directly): “You’re looking for a tool to get your memory back, right? We’ve found two Holy Relics so far. I predict the third one is here.”

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Persuasion & Negotiation: A - Reframes from trust‑based to interest‑based cooperation.
Social Intelligence: A - Understands Fuubi’s psychological constraints (distrust as survival mechanism).
Strategic Framing: S - Uses shared goal as a bridge without demanding emotional commitment.
Questioning Technique: A - Asks factual, non‑threatening questions that appeal to self‑interest.
Emotional Regulation: B - Remains calm and non‑reactive to Fuubi’s deflections.

Feat 1.4: Meta‑Cognitive Understanding of the Holy Relic Touch

When Kimizuka hesitates to touch the third Holy Relic, fearing he might learn things he shouldn’t or interfere with others’ stories, Siesta does not argue. She simply overlaps her palm with his and touches the relic together. This non‑verbal action communicates partnership, shares the burden, and implicitly says “I’ll bear this with you.”

Premises:

1. Kimizuka’s Hesitation Premise: Kimizuka explicitly states his reluctance: “I know. I have to do it. Touch it and get my memory back. But I may end up knowing things I shouldn’t know again. … I don’t think I have the right to interfere with other people’s stories.”
2. Previous Experience Premise: The last time he touched a Holy Relic, he saw “the end of the vampire king that I shouldn’t have known” – a memory that belonged to a “world with just the king and the bride.”
3. Moral Guilt Premise: His hesitation is not fear of pain or death, but moral guilt about trespassing on others’ private histories.
4. Necessity Premise: Despite his reluctance, he knows the story will not start unless someone touches the relic. (“I know, the story won’t start unless I touch it.”)
5. Siesta’s Role Premise: Siesta is his partner, the detective to his assistant. They have shared everything for three years.
6. Non‑Verbal Communication Premise: Words have already been exhausted. (“We’ve exchanged enough words since she woke up, no, even before she woke up.”)

Chain of Reasoning:

* Step 1: Observe Kimizuka’s hesitation and his expressed moral concern.
* Step 2: Recognize that verbal reassurance (“It’s okay, you have the right”) would be insufficient – he already knows logically that he must touch it, but his emotional barrier remains.
* Step 3: Decide on a non‑verbal approach. Physical presence can communicate what words cannot.
* Step 4: Smile softly to signal understanding and lack of judgment.
* Step 5: Overlap her palm with his – a gesture that says “we do this together.”
* Step 6: Touch the relic simultaneously, sharing the experience and the burden.
* Step 7: After the memory transfer, when Kimizuka experiences nausea, rub his back to provide comfort.
* Step 8: When he recovers, ask “Assistant, will you tell me?” – now that the memory is shared, she invites him to narrate, turning his feared trespass into a shared story.

Conclusion: Siesta’s non‑verbal response is a sophisticated emotional and strategic choice. She bypasses Kimizuka’s moral anxiety through physical partnership, then transforms the experience into a collaborative narrative.

Key Dialogue (paraphrased):

Kimizuka: “I know. I have to do it. Touch it and get my memory back. But I may end up knowing things I shouldn’t know again. … I don’t think I have the right to interfere with other people’s stories.”

(Pause)

Siesta (smiling softly): “Or something like that.”

(She overlaps her palm with his. No words. They touch the relic together.)

(After the memory transfer, Kimizuka feels nauseous; Siesta rubs his back.)

Siesta: “Assistant, will you tell me?”

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Emotional Intelligence: S - Recognizes that verbal reassurance is insufficient for moral guilt.
Non‑Verbal Communication: S - Uses physical touch to convey partnership and shared burden.
Strategic Silence: A - Knows when words are unnecessary.
Empathy: A - Responds to his nausea with physical comfort.

EVENT 2: The Daydream Garden – Siesta’s Mental Landscape (A Certain Interlude in Dream)

Context: After Abel attacks Kimizuka with the “Code of Evanescence,” leaving him a living corpse without sensory perception, Kimizuka’s consciousness appears in a garden – Siesta’s mental landscape, where she has previously communicated with Natsunagi Nagisa and Hel. Siesta is lucid, drinking tea, and fully aware of the situation. Her body is undergoing heart surgery in the real world. She explains why Kimizuka is there, how he can return, and offers guidance.

Feat 2.1: Meta‑Cognitive Analysis of Her Own Mental Space

Siesta calmly dissects the nature of the daydream realm, listing possible causes for Kimizuka’s presence and distinguishing between enabling conditions and trigger events. She demonstrates self‑awareness about her own cognitive state while in a dream.

Premises:

1. Prior Experience Premise: She has met Nagisa and Hel in this same mental space before. This is not a new phenomenon; it is a recurring feature of her consciousness.
2. Not Necessarily Real Premise: The space is not physically real. It could be a dream, a mental construct, or a “daydream” (the novel’s term for her heart‑based consciousness).
3. Surgery Premise: Her body is currently undergoing heart transplant surgery. The artificial heart made by Stephen is being implanted. This operation could affect the stability, permeability, or expansion of this mental space.
4. Blood Link Premise: During the Vampire Rebellion (Volume 9’s flashback), Siesta was half‑vampirized and bit Kimizuka’s neck, drinking his blood. His genes entered her body, creating a biological connection.
5. Consciousness Fading Premise: In the real world, Kimizuka’s consciousness is in danger of being erased by Abel’s “Code of Evanescence.” His five senses have shut down; he is “dead while alive.”
6. Causal Hierarchy Premise: Different causes can operate at different levels – some are enabling conditions (necessary but not sufficient), others are triggers (the immediate cause).
7. Uncertainty Acknowledgment Premise: She does not claim certainty about the first explanation (“I can’t say exactly how”).

Chain of Reasoning:

* Step 1: Begin by clarifying her own ontological status: “I am not necessarily real.” This shows metacognitive awareness – she knows she might be a dream construct.
* Step 2: Recall that Nagisa and Hel have accessed this space before. This establishes that the space is not unique to Kimizuka, but rather a general feature of her consciousness that certain people can enter.
* Step 3: List three candidate explanations for why Kimizuka is here now.
* Step 4: Explanation A (Surgery): “I’m having heart surgery right now. It might have some effect on me. Like, how this space is expanding.” She qualifies this with “I can’t say exactly how” – low confidence. This is a contextual factor.
* Step 5: Explanation B (Blood Link): “The second is that you came inside me.” (Clarifies: “You know, the other day, Scarlet’s castle – I drank your blood.”) This is a past event that created a permanent biological channel. Medium confidence – the link exists, but why now?
* Step 6: Explanation C (Consciousness Fading): “And the biggest reason why you came to this place – that is, your consciousness in the real world was in danger of fading away.” High confidence – directly addresses the timing and urgency.
* Step 7: Rank the explanations by causal relevance: Explanation C is the trigger (immediate cause), Explanation B is the enabling condition (the channel through which the trigger operates), Explanation A is a contextual factor (the surgery may amplify the effect but is not necessary).
* Step 8: Conclude that his presence is a consequence of his near‑death state, and that he must find his way back.

Conclusion: Siesta demonstrates exceptional metacognitive awareness, analyzing her own mental space from a third‑person perspective and distinguishing between enabling factors, triggers, and contextual conditions. She also accurately expresses different confidence levels for each hypothesis.

Key Dialogue (paraphrased):

Kimizuka: “What is this?”

Siesta: “I am not necessarily real. … Maybe. But I’ve met Nagisa and Hel here before.”

Kimizuka: “But why did I come to this place too?”

Siesta: “I wonder. There are several possible reasons, but… One is that I’m having heart surgery right now. I can’t say exactly how, but it might have some effect on me. Like, how this space is expanding. The second is that you came inside me. You know, the other day, Scarlet’s castle – I drank your blood. And the biggest reason why you came to this place – that is, your consciousness in the real world was in danger of fading away.”

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Meta‑Cognition: S - Analyzes her own mental state and its mechanisms from a third‑person perspective.
Causal Reasoning: S - Distinguishes enabling conditions (blood link) from triggers (consciousness fading) from contextual factors (surgery).
Introspective Reasoning: S - Maintains lucidity and self‑awareness while in a dream state.
Uncertainty Calibration: S - Accurately expresses different confidence levels for each hypothesis.
Hypothesis Generation: A - Produces multiple plausible explanations without committing prematurely.

Feat 2.2: Strategic Advice for Consciousness Restoration – The “Will” Framework

When Kimizuka asks how to return to his body, Siesta provides a structured, multi‑step protocol. She emphasizes both external help (someone calling for him) and internal determination (realizing his own “Will”). This is not vague encouragement but a practical cognitive framework.

Premises:

1. Disconnection Premise: Abel’s “Code of Evanescence” has severed his sensory connection to the real world. He cannot see, hear, feel, smell, or taste. He is a “living corpse” – conscious but disconnected.
2. External Call Necessity Premise: Someone in the real world must reach out to him. This call cannot be transmitted through physical senses (which are disabled) but through a “chain” or bond that links that person to him.
3. Internal Will Necessity Premise: Before he can recognize the external call, he must clarify his own desire – what he wants to do, where he wants to go, how he wants to live. This “Will” is the engine that drives his return.
4. Directional Metaphor Premise: The process can be visualized as walking toward a ray of light in darkness or rain. Even when perception is impaired, the direction matters.
5. Temporal Order Premise: The internal Will must be clarified before he can follow the external call, but the external call must exist for him to have something to follow. They are interdependent.
6. Prior Success Premise: She has seen this work before (implied by her reference to Nagisa and Hel accessing her daydream). The framework is not theoretical but empirically grounded.

Chain of Reasoning:

* Step 1: Acknowledge that returning is possible – she does not say “if” but “how.”
* Step 2: Identify the first necessary condition: someone must call for him. This is not a passive hope but an active requirement. (“It’s important that someone calls for you.”)
* Step 3: Describe the mechanism of following that call: “follow the chain linking you with that person.” This implies that the bond (friendship, love, shared history) functions as a guide even when senses are offline.
* Step 4: Add a qualifier about environmental difficulty: “Even if it rains, even if it’s dark” – meaning that the path may be obscured, but he must still walk toward the light.
* Step 5: Identify the second necessary condition: he must realize his own “Will” first. (“Of course, you need to realize your own ‘Will’ first.”)
* Step 6: Define “Will” concretely: “You will think about how you want to live, where you want to go.”
* Step 7: Ask the triggering question: “Hey, assistant. What do you want to do for tomorrow?” This forces him to articulate his Will, not just feel it vaguely.
* Step 8: After he answers (implicitly, in his own mind), the process can begin.

Conclusion: The framework is a practical cognitive protocol for restoring consciousness from a comatose or sensory‑deprived state. Kimizuka later follows this advice, and with Nagisa’s constant calls, he returns.

Key Dialogue (paraphrased):

Kimizuka: “How can I return, Siesta?”

Siesta (sipping tea): “It’s important that someone calls for you. And then you follow the chain linking you with that person. Even if it rains, even if it’s dark, you will walk towards that ray of light. Of course, you need to realize your own ‘Will’ first. You will think about how you want to live, where you want to go — Hey, assistant. What do you want to do for tomorrow?”

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Strategic Planning: S - Provides a clear, actionable multi‑step recovery protocol.
Psychological Insight: A - Understands the need for both external anchor (someone calling) and internal anchor (Will).
Problem Decomposition: S - Breaks down an abstract problem (consciousness restoration) into concrete steps.
Metaphorical Communication: A - Uses “light,” “chain,” “rain,” “darkness” imagery effectively.
Socratic Questioning: A - Asks “What do you want to do for tomorrow?” to force articulation of Will.

Feat 2.3: Quantitative Self‑Assessment – The 50% → 30% → 12.5% Progression

When discussing how much of her story’s burden Kimizuka has carried, Siesta offers a numerical estimate that she deliberately adjusts downward in a teasing manner. This demonstrates comfort with quantitative reasoning and the use of numbers for relational dynamics.

Premises:

1. Burden Sharing Premise: Kimizuka has been her assistant for three years, sharing the difficulties of detective work – solving cases, fighting enemies, traveling the world.
2. Protagonist Role Premise: Siesta is the central figure, the “Ace Detective” and “the protagonist who carried her story.” The primary burden is hers.
3. Conversational Context Premise: The discussion occurs in a lighthearted moment of their dream conversation, not a serious performance evaluation or argument.
4. Teasing Relationship Premise: Their relationship includes playful banter. Siesta often teases Kimizuka by calling him “stupid” or questioning his competence.
5. Fractional Fluency Premise: The numbers she chooses (50%, 30%, 12.5%) are all simple fractions (1/2, ~1/3, 1/8), indicating intentional selection.

Chain of Reasoning:

* Step 1: Start with a generous estimate that acknowledges Kimizuka’s contribution: “someone who carried half of it with me” (50%).
* Step 2: Immediately revise downward for humorous effect: “Half, no, thirty percent…” (30%).
* Step 3: Revise further, seemingly as an afterthought: “…no, maybe 12.5 percent?” (1/8).
* Step 4: The descending numbers are not random – each is a clear fraction that the listener can easily recognize.
* Step 5: The humor comes from the contrast between the initial generosity (half) and the final, comically low figure (one‑eighth). It is a form of understatement.
* Step 6: When Kimizuka reacts with mock outrage (“I’m on the verge of being fired, am I?”), she smiles as if she was waiting for that reaction.
* Step 7: She then asks “Why did you make me your assistant with that score?” – a rhetorical, playful question that has no answer.
* Step 8: After a pause, she smiles nostalgically and repeats “Really, I wonder why,” maintaining the teasing tone while also showing genuine fondness.

Conclusion: Siesta uses quantitative language to make an abstract point about interdependence, then subverts it for rapport. The rapid recalculation shows numerical fluency, and the timing of the joke demonstrates social awareness.

Key Dialogue (paraphrased):

Siesta: “Well, in my case, I had someone who carried half of it with me. Half, no, thirty percent… no, maybe 12.5 percent?”

Kimizuka (retorting): “I’m on the verge of being fired, am I?”

Siesta (as if waiting for it): “Fufu, I wonder why. Really, I wonder why.”

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Quantitative Reasoning: B - Comfortable with fractions and percentages; can mentally calculate 1/8 = 12.5%.
Humor & Rapport: A - Uses numbers to tease without cruelty; times the joke for maximum effect.
Social Communication: A - Adjusts estimate to fit the playful tone of the conversation.
Narrative Framing: B - Uses quantitative language to make an abstract point (burden sharing) more concrete.

Feat 2.4: Causal Inference – Ranking Explanations for Kimizuka’s Presence

Although Siesta lists three possible reasons for Kimizuka being in her daydream, the order, qualifiers, and phrasing reveal a ranking by likelihood and causal relevance. This is an implicit Bayesian inference.

Premises (same as Feat 2.1):

1. Reason 1: Surgery (uncertain effect, contextual)
2. Reason 2: Blood link (established physical connection, enabling condition)
3. Reason 3: Consciousness fading (immediate crisis, trigger)

Chain of Reasoning:

* Step 1: Present the three reasons sequentially, but with different qualifiers.
* Step 2: For Reason 1, say “I can’t say exactly how” – this signals low confidence. Also use “might” (modal verb indicating possibility, not certainty).
* Step 3: For Reason 2, state as a matter of fact: “You came inside me” (then clarifies the blood drinking). No qualifier like “maybe” or “perhaps.” This indicates medium‑to‑high confidence that the connection exists.
* Step 4: For Reason 3, introduce with “And the biggest reason” – this is an explicit comparative statement. “Biggest” implies primacy in importance, likelihood, or causal relevance.
* Step 5: Explain Reason 3: “your consciousness in the real world was in danger of fading away” – stated as a fact, not a possibility.
* Step 6: The pattern of qualifiers (“I can’t say exactly” → no qualifier → “biggest”) signals her ranking.
* Step 7: Infer that Reason 3 is the proximate cause (the trigger that made him appear now), Reason 2 is an enabling condition (the channel that makes access possible), and Reason 1 is a contextual factor (the surgery may have expanded the space but is not necessary).

Conclusion: Siesta distinguishes between necessary conditions, sufficient conditions, and triggers. Her causal reasoning is sophisticated and implicitly probabilistic.

Key Dialogue (paraphrased):

 (Same as Feat 2.1)

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Causal Reasoning: S - Distinguishes trigger from enabling conditions from contextual factors.
Uncertainty Calibration: S - Accurately expresses different confidence levels (low for surgery, medium for blood link, high for consciousness fading).
Bayesian Inference (implicit): A - Ranks hypotheses by likelihood without explicit calculation.
Metalinguistic Awareness: A - Uses qualifiers (“I can’t say exactly,” “might,” “biggest”) to signal confidence.

EVENT 3: The Epilogue – Recovered Memories and New Inferences (From the Future, Another Epilogue)

Context: After recovering memories via the three Holy Relics, Siesta, Kimizuka, and Fuubi sit outside ruins at night, discussing what they have learned. They have remembered Abel A. Schönberg as Phantom Thief, the nature of the Akashic Records as a world‑controlling program (SYSTEM), and the battle at the control tower. However, the final showdown details are still missing. They also learn that Charlotte is missing and possibly involved with a terrorist organization. The conversation turns to Abel’s fate and their next steps.

Feat 3.1: Inference That Abel Survived – The Memory Erasure as Evidence

After Kimizuka finishes narrating his recovered memories, Siesta points out that Abel prophesied his return at the end of their battle. The fact that the final battle details are erased, combined with the scale of memory loss, leads her to conclude that Abel is still alive.

Premises:

1. Prophecy Premise: In the recovered memory, Abel explicitly stated: “My plan is not over yet. There are still many secrets left uncovered.” This is a direct statement of continuation.
2. Memory Gap Premise: The exact details of the final showdown with Abel are missing from everyone’s memory. (“Only the words ‘Phantom Thief’, ‘Akashic Records’, ‘Singularity’ have disappeared from my mind. However the records are missing. I don’t remember anything about the final showdown with Abel.”)
3. Erasure Scale Premise: Key terms have been erased from the world’s records, not just individual recollections. This is a global phenomenon.
4. Intentionality Premise: Large‑scale, precise erasure of specific memories requires an actor with the power to rewrite world records. The only known entity with such power is Abel himself, via his “Code of Oblivion” (or similar Code).
5. Counterfactual Premise: If Abel had been permanently neutralized (killed, sealed, or rendered powerless), there would be no need to erase memories of the final battle. Leaving those memories intact would pose no threat – in fact, it would help his enemies confirm his defeat.
6. Motivation Premise: The purpose of erasure is to hide his survival and prevent his enemies from knowing how they won (or lost) or what his weaknesses might be.

Chain of Reasoning:

* Step 1: Listen to Kimizuka’s recovered memory, noting Abel’s final words before vanishing.
* Step 2: Observe that the memory cuts off before the conclusion – the final showdown is absent.
* Step 3: Compare her own memory – she also lacks those details. (“We had gradually remembered his existence with the two Holy Relics before, but it became clear this time.”)
* Step 4: Recognize that such a specific, targeted memory gap is unlikely to be natural forgetting. Natural forgetting is gradual and imprecise; this is a clean erasure of a discrete event.
* Step 5: Infer that an actor with memory‑erasure abilities caused this gap.
* Step 6: Identify the most likely candidate: Abel, who has demonstrated the “Code of Oblivion” (as seen with Professor Moriya’s erasure later in the volume).
* Step 7: Consider Abel’s motivation. If he were dead, erasure would be pointless – no one to hide. If he were alive, erasure would be essential to conceal his survival and his plans.
* Step 8: Apply the counterfactual: Suppose Abel were dead. Then the memory of his defeat would be harmless, even beneficial. Why erase it? There is no good reason.
* Step 9: Therefore, the most parsimonious explanation is that Abel is alive and actively hiding.
* Step 10: State the conclusion as a question (“But assistant. Doesn’t that mean Abel is still alive?”) to invite confirmation, but the reasoning is clear.

Conclusion: The memory loss itself is evidence of Abel’s continued existence. Siesta uses absence of memory as positive evidence – a sophisticated epistemological move.

Key Dialogue (paraphrased):

Kimizuka: “…Yeah, we failed to kill him. In the memories I regained, Abel had prophesied his return at the tail end of our battle. What happened after that? … We fought, we definitely fought. I remember that. Only the words ‘Phantom Thief’, ‘Akashic Records’, ‘Singularity’ have disappeared from my mind. However the records are missing. I don’t remember anything about the final showdown with Abel.”

Siesta (muttering to herself): “Phantom Thief Abel A. Schönberg. That was my assistant’s enemy back then, huh… So did we all have our memories stolen by him? But assistant. Doesn’t that mean Abel is still alive?”

Kimizuka: “…Yeah, we failed to kill him.”

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Inference from Absence: S - Treats missing memory as positive evidence (argument from silence used correctly).
Counterfactual Reasoning: A - Considers what would be true if Abel were dead, and shows contradiction.
Abductive Reasoning: S - Selects the most parsimonious explanation (Abel alive) among alternatives.
Memory Analysis: A - Distinguishes natural forgetting from targeted erasure.
Epistemological Reasoning: A - Recognizes that the absence of evidence can be evidence of absence under certain conditions.

Feat 3.2: Philosophical Synthesis – Living Inside the Map, Not Redrawing It

As Siesta gazes at the starry night sky with Fuubi, she offers a reflective statement that synthesizes the volume’s central theme: rejecting the idea of remaking the world from above (Ryan White’s plan to redraw borders, Abel’s plan to invert reality), and instead affirming the value of living within the given world.

Premises (implicit in her dialogue and the volume’s themes):

1. Human Situatedness Premise: Humans are always located somewhere – on muddy ground, on rough seas, on passenger planes at 10,000 meters. There is no transcendental “view from nowhere.”
2. Map Metaphor Premise: The world can be thought of as a map. To be “inside the map” means to be subject to its constraints, boundaries, and given features.
3. Ryan’s Temptation Premise: Ryan White wanted to “redraw the map” – to reshape national borders and the world order through controlled war, standing above as a cartographer.
4. Abel’s Temptation Premise: Abel wanted to “invert the world” – to rewrite the fundamental program of reality, becoming the administrator of SYSTEM.
5. Failure of Hubris Premise: Both approaches lead to destruction, loss, and tyranny (as seen in Ryan’s transformation and Abel’s crimes).
6. Alternative Premise: True justice, true living, is not about escaping the map or becoming its dictator. It is about how one moves within it – stepping on muddy ground, sailing rough seas, flying close to stars.
7. Fuubi’s Observation Premise: Fuubi says “It’s not bad, this corner of the world” – a statement of contentment with a small, bounded place under the stars.

Chain of Reasoning:

* Step 1: Observe the night sky and the sense of being in a specific “corner of the world” (Fuubi’s phrase).
* Step 2: Reflect on the volume’s events – Ryan’s attempted coup, Abel’s plan to steal Akashic Records, the battle at SYSTEM.
* Step 3: Recognize a common thread: both antagonists tried to position themselves above the world (as cartographer or administrator).
* Step 4: Contrast this with the lived experience of humans: stepping on muddy ground, driving boats, flying in planes. These are activities within the world, not above it.
* Step 5: Note that even the highest human point (10,000 meters in a plane) is still “inside the map” – still within the physical and social world.
* Step 6: Conclude that the ambition to escape or redraw the map is misguided.
* Step 7: Articulate the positive alternative: “We don’t redraw maps. We live our lives inside the map.”
* Step 8: Affirm that this is what they (Siesta, Kimizuka, Fuubi) are doing – continuing their adventures within the given world.

Conclusion: Siesta’s statement is a philosophical resolution to the volume’s central conflict. It rejects both Abel’s and Ryan’s hubris and affirms an immanent, bounded, yet meaningful existence.

Key Dialogue (paraphrased):

Fuubi (looking up at the night sky): “It’s not bad, this corner of the world.”

Siesta (gazing up the same way): “We step on the muddy ground, we drive a boat over the rough sea, we fly on a passenger plane at ten thousand meters above where we are closest to the stars. But in the end, we are always inside the map. We are living here.”

Kimizuka: “Yeah, that’s right. We don’t redraw maps. We live our lives inside the map.”

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Philosophical Reasoning: S - Articulates a coherent ethical position from scattered thematic elements.
Synthesis of Abstract Themes: S - Integrates the volume’s motifs of maps, justice, hubris, and human limitation.
Metaphorical Thinking: S - Uses “map” as a rich, multi‑layered symbol (physical geography, political borders, SYSTEM program).
Contrastive Reasoning: A - Contrasts “redrawing maps” with “living inside the map” to highlight the difference.
Poetic Expression: A - Delivers the insight in a memorable, image‑rich statement.

Feat 3.3: Social Boundary Enforcement – Correcting Kimizuka’s Jealousy

When Kimizuka, on the phone with Nagisa and Ookami, tells Ookami to “cut off contact with Nagisa from now on” out of jealousy or protectiveness, Siesta calmly reprimands him without anger or escalation.

Premises:

1. Autonomy Premise: Natsunagi Nagisa is an adult (college student) capable of making her own decisions about her relationships and friendships.
2. No Authority Premise: Kimizuka has no official or informal right to control who Nagisa interacts with. They are not in a romantic relationship that would grant such a say (and even then, controlling friendships would be inappropriate).
3. Proportionality Premise: Even if Kimizuka has feelings for Nagisa, that does not grant him veto power over her friendships with other men (including Ookami).
4. Social Norm Premise: In normal social interactions, telling someone to cut off contact with a mutual acquaintance is considered overbearing unless there is a legitimate safety concern.
5. Context Premise: The phone call is about a serious matter (Charlotte’s whereabouts and a terrorist organization). Kimizuka’s jealous comment is off‑topic and unprofessional.

Chain of Reasoning:

* Step 1: Hear Kimizuka’s command to Ookami: “Please cut off contact with Nagisa from now on.”
* Step 2: Recognize the underlying emotion (jealousy, protectiveness, or possessiveness).
* Step 3: Assess whether the comment is appropriate. It is not – Nagisa is not his property, and Ookami has done nothing wrong.
* Step 4: Formulate a response that is clear, calm, and principled, not emotional.
* Step 5: State the normative principle directly: “Assistant, you don’t have the right to restrict Nagisa’s relationships with other men.”
* Step 6: Do not escalate or lecture. One sentence suffices.
* Step 7: Kimizuka clears his throat and changes the subject (“Sorry, Ookami‑san. Let me switch again.”) – implicitly accepting her correction.
* Step 8: No further discussion needed. The boundary has been established.

Conclusion: Siesta enforces a basic social boundary without anger, condescension, or lengthy argument. She demonstrates emotional regulation and a clear understanding of autonomy rights.

Key Dialogue (paraphrased):

Kimizuka (on phone): “Please cut off contact with Nagisa from now on.”

Siesta (calmly, in the background): “Assistant, you don’t have the right to restrict Nagisa’s relationships with other men.”

Kimizuka (clears throat): “Sorry, Ookami‑san. Let me switch again.”

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Social Reasoning (Deontic): A - Understands rights and boundaries; applies general principle to specific case.
Emotional Regulation (in others): A - Corrects Kimizuka without anger or conflict, avoiding escalation.
Normative Reasoning: B - States the principle clearly and concisely.
Situational Awareness: B - Recognizes that the comment was off‑topic and inappropriate.

Feat 3.4: Coordinated Inference with Kimizuka – The Final Holy Relic Location

Mia describes a prophetic dream containing images: three sacred objects, a large stone tablet, a locked door, and a triangular entity floating in the air. Kimizuka recognizes the triangular monument as the SYSTEM control tower from his recovered memory. Without explicit verbal exchange, he and Siesta reach the same conclusion about where the last forgotten memory lies.

Premises:

1. Dream Imagery Premise: Mia’s dream contains a “triangular entity floating in the air.”
2. Shape Match Premise: That triangular entity matches the shape of the Holy Relics collected so far (triangular pyramids).
3. SYSTEM Identity Premise: In Kimizuka’s recovered memory, the control tower that houses the Akashic Records is an inverted triangular pyramid – exactly matching the dream imagery.
4. Memory Location Premise: If SYSTEM is the master program that controls the world and stores world records, then the last lost memory (the “Great Calamity”) is likely located there.
5. Holy Relic Analogy Premise: The three Holy Relics they collected are physical fragments of lost memory. The “three sacred objects” in Mia’s dream likely correspond to these three relics.
6. Oracle Reliability Premise: Mia’s prophetic dreams are not always complete or literal, but they reliably point to real phenomena (as seen throughout the series).

Chain of Reasoning (shared, non‑verbal):

* Step 1 (Kimizuka): Recall the inverted triangular monument from the recovered memory. Recognize it in Mia’s description.
* Step 2 (Kimizuka): State aloud: “A triangular monument hovering mid‑air. Its shape was the same as the Holy Relics we collected so far. SYSTEM, the master program that controls the world – the last forgotten memory is in that control tower.”
* Step 3 (Siesta): Hear Kimizuka’s conclusion. Instantly recognize its consistency with her own understanding of the relics and SYSTEM.
* Step 4 (Siesta): Connect the “three sacred objects” to the three Holy Relics they have touched.
* Step 5 (Siesta): Infer that the “locked door” and “stone tablet” are other elements within SYSTEM that they will need to navigate.
* Step 6 (Both): Exchange a nod – silent confirmation that they have reached the same conclusion.
* Step 7 (Siesta): State their next course of action: “We’ll definitely bring Charl back.”
* Step 8 (Siesta, to Nagisa on the phone): “Yeah, leave it to us.”

Conclusion: The final piece of lost memory (the “Great Calamity”) is in the SYSTEM control tower. Siesta and Kimizuka’s coordinated inference, achieved without explicit debate, demonstrates their deep partnership.

Key Dialogue (paraphrased):

Mia (on phone): “A fragmented dream. Three sacred objects, a large stone tablet, a locked door, and a triangular entity floating in the air. Does it ring a bell? Kimihiko, in the memory you recovered this time, is there anything related to those?”

Kimizuka: “…Yeah, there is. I see, so that’s what it is. A triangular monument hovering mid‑air. Its shape was the same as the Holy Relics we collected so far. SYSTEM, the master program that controls the world – the last forgotten memory is in that control tower.”

Siesta (nodding with Kimizuka): “We’ll definitely bring Charl back.”

Nagisa (on phone): “So, Kimihiko and Siesta…!”

Siesta: “Yeah, leave it to us.”

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Collaborative Reasoning: A - Reaches shared conclusion without explicit debate or lengthy explanation.
Rapid Pattern Matching: S - Connects dream imagery (triangular entity) to SYSTEM to Holy Relics in seconds.
Inference by Analogy: A - The triangular entity is analogous to the Holy Relics in shape, implying similar function.
Non‑Verbal Communication: B - Nodding indicates shared understanding without words.
Oracle Interpretation: A - Recognizes that Mia’s dream imagery is symbolic but points to real locations.

Despite being physically incapacitated for much of the volume, Siesta demonstrates superior reasoning across multiple domains: spatial pattern recognition (10,000 km circle), auditory perception, negotiation with a distrustful ally, meta‑cognitive analysis of her own dream space, causal inference distinguishing triggers from enabling conditions, strategic planning for consciousness restoration, inference from absence (Abel’s survival), philosophical synthesis (“living inside the map”), and coordinated reasoning with Kimizuka. Her dream sequence appearance is particularly notable for its lucid self‑awareness and structured advice. The epilogue shows her ability to draw high‑level inferences from memory gaps and articulate a coherent ethical position.

VOLUME 11

Note on Volume 11’s structure: The volume opens with a “From the Future: A Prologue” set after the memories of the Great Cataclysm have been restored. Then it flashes back to the Great Cataclysm events (the final battle with Abel, the confrontation with the Federation Government, the awakening of Siesta, the fight with Ice Doll, the resolution with Yggdrasil). Finally, it returns to the present with a cherry blossom viewing epilogue. Siesta is physically present in the prologue, in the flashback (after her awakening), and in the epilogue.

EVENT 1: The Jungle Approach – Survival and Environmental Analysis (From the Future: A Prologue)

Context: Siesta, Kimizuka, and Fuubi trek through a dense jungle on a remote island to reach an ancient control tower where the Akashic Records (SYSTEM) is located. The jungle is overgrown due to Yggdrasil seeds spreading worldwide. Siesta walks ahead, seemingly unaffected by the difficult terrain, and demonstrates practical survival skills.

Feat 1.1: Fire Creation from a Hemp Wick – Practical Survival Reasoning Under Threat

When a snake suddenly lunges at Siesta, she instantly creates a small flame from a hemp wick and uses it to startle the snake away. She does not rely on modern tools (lighter, matches) but uses a primitive fire‑starting method, demonstrating knowledge of both survival techniques and animal behavior.

Premises:

1. Environmental Premise: They are trekking through a humid jungle; snakes are common in such environments.
2. Threat Premise: A snake bares its sharp fangs and lunges directly at Siesta. The snake is fast and close – drawing a gun or knife would be too slow.
3. Tool Premise: Siesta carries a hemp wick – a dry, flammable plant fiber. She has it prepared (likely pre‑charred or kept dry).
4. Ignition Knowledge Premise: Hemp wick can be ignited by friction (rubbing against a rough surface) or by a spark from a flint. Siesta likely has a hidden fire‑starting tool (or the wick was already smoldering).
5. Animal Behavior Premise: Most wild animals, including snakes, are startled by sudden bright light or heat. Fire is an ancient animal deterrent.
6. Safety Premise: A small, controlled flame is safer than a gunshot (which could ricochet or cause a larger fire) or a knife (which requires close contact).

Chain of Reasoning:

* Step 1: Perceive the snake’s attack trajectory – it is aiming for her leg or torso.
* Step 2: Assess available responses: gun (too slow, risk of missing), knife (requires close combat, risk of bite), shouting (ineffective), or fire (fast deterrent).
* Step 3: Recall that snakes are ectothermic and sensitive to infrared heat; a sudden flame mimics a predator’s body heat or creates an aversive stimulus.
* Step 4: Retrieve the hemp wick from her pocket or belt – she keeps it accessible for precisely such situations.
* Step 5: Ignite the wick – using a hidden flint, a pre‑lit ember from earlier, or friction against her metal belt buckle (not shown but implied by her speed).
* Step 6: Present the flame toward the snake – the sudden burst of light and heat startles the animal.
* Step 7: The snake twists its body and flees – confirming the tactic worked.
