* Step 8: Immediately control the flame to prevent spreading fire in the dry jungle.
* Step 9: Casually remark to Kimizuka that it was “just a snake,” minimizing the danger and maintaining calm.

Conclusion: Siesta’s choice of a non‑lethal, primitive fire deterrent is optimal for the situation – it is fast, safe (no risk of friendly fire), effective against the specific animal, and requires minimal energy. This demonstrates practical survival knowledge honed over years of field work.

Key Dialogue (paraphrased):

Kimizuka: “Siesta!” (warning of the snake)

Siesta (calmly): “Sorry, can’t really give you my blood.”

(A small flame rises from her hand; the snake flees)

Siesta (looking exasperated at Kimizuka): “You don’t need to shout so loud. It’s just a snake. In her hand was the burnt end of a hemp wick.”

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Practical Survival: A - Knows how to create fire from natural materials under pressure.
Animal Behavior Knowledge: B - Understands that sudden flame deters snakes.
Reaction Speed (Problem‑solving): A - Chooses the optimal deterrent in under a second.
Risk Assessment: B - Evaluates that fire is safer than weapons in this context.
Tool Preparedness: B - Carries a hemp wick for fire‑starting – shows forethought.

Feat 1.2: Rhetorical Question About What to Take to a Deserted Island – Strategic Self‑Positioning and Relationship Management

When Kimizuka asks what she would take to a deserted island, Siesta first answers “I don’t really need anything in particular” – demonstrating self‑sufficiency. Then, after Kimizuka lists practical tools (knife, rope, fishing rod), she rephrases: “That’s the case, so I would take you with me. Boredom kills, after all.” This is not mere flirtation but a strategic acknowledgment of their partnership, delivered with humor to avoid sentimentality.

Premises:

1. Self‑Sufficiency Premise: Siesta can survive alone in the wilderness – she can create fire, catch fish, build shelter, find water. She needs no external tools.
2. Kimizuka’s Answer Premise: He says he would take her (“Knowledge and experience, this girl has it all”), which is a compliment but also practical.
3. Social Dynamic Premise: Their relationship is built on teasing and banter, not overt emotional declarations.
4. Psychological Need Premise: Even if she can survive alone, isolation is mentally taxing. A companion alleviates boredom and provides backup.
5. Humor as Defense Premise: She uses a joke (“Boredom kills”) to avoid saying something overly sentimental, while still affirming that she values his company.

Chain of Reasoning:

* Step 1: Hear Kimizuka’s question – it is hypothetical but reveals his priorities.
* Step 2: Evaluate her own survival capabilities – she truly needs no tools.
* Step 3: Answer honestly but plainly: “I don’t really need anything in particular.”
* Step 4: Listen to his follow‑up: “Well, Siesta, I would take you” – he has turned the question back on her, implying he values her.
* Step 5: Recognize that a straightforward “I would take you too” would be too cloying for their dynamic.
* Step 6: Instead, rephrase her answer: “That’s the case, so I would take you with me” – accepting his compliment while reframing it as a practical choice.
* Step 7: Add the humorous justification: “Boredom kills, after all” – which both teases him (implying he is entertainment, not a survival asset) and affirms that she wants his company.
* Step 8: When he retorts “So I’m just a clown?”, she smiles and takes his hand, completing the gesture.

Conclusion: The answer is a masterclass in balancing honesty, humor, and affection. She acknowledges his value while maintaining her self‑sufficient persona.

Key Dialogue (paraphrased):

Kimizuka: “Well, Siesta, I would take you. Knowledge and experience, this girl has it all. Far more useful than taking any tool, she and I are capable of overcoming any difficulty together.”

Siesta: “Are you stupid, Kimi?”

Kimizuka: “You’re so unfair. … Siesta, what about you? If you could take only one thing with you, what would it be?”

Siesta: “Eh, I don’t really need anything in particular.”

Kimizuka: “Figures. … That’s the case, so I would take you with me.”

Siesta: “Boredom kills, after all.”

Kimizuka: “So I’m just a clown?”

(She smiles and accepts his hand.)

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Social Intelligence: A - Uses humor to express partnership without sentimentality.
Self‑Assessment: B - Accurately knows her own survival capabilities.
Emotional Communication: A - Conveys affection indirectly through banter.
Strategic Framing: B - Reframes his compliment as a practical choice.

Feat 1.3: Recognition of the World’s Memory Manipulation – Meta‑Cognition About Thought Control

As they approach the colossal ruin (the control tower), Siesta remarks that they “didn’t even question it” – referring to the world’s anomalies (Yggdrasil seeds spreading unnaturally, encroaching on human territory). She connects this to Abel stealing the Akashic Records and controlling human thoughts. This demonstrates her ability to step back and question her own perception – a form of meta‑cognition.

Premises:

1. Anomaly Premise: Yggdrasil seeds have spread worldwide, causing plants to grow at an unnatural pace, slowly encroaching on human territories.
2. Human Passivity Premise: Despite this obvious crisis, humanity has not reacted with urgency. The problem has been “accepted” as normal.
3. Memory Loss Premise: The world’s memories of the final battle with Abel are missing. Key terms (“Phantom Thief,” “Akashic Records,” “Singularity”) have been erased.
4. Controlled Perception Premise: If memories can be erased, other cognitive functions – such as pattern recognition, suspicion, and questioning – might also be suppressed.
5. Enemy Capability Premise: Abel (Phantom Thief) has the power to inject “Code” that alters reality, including human perception and memory.
6. Historical Precedent Premise: Hakutengu (from earlier volumes) mentioned that there are devices scattered around the world that record the past and future, and that these devices can be manipulated.

Chain of Reasoning:

* Step 1: Observe the rapid, unnatural growth of Yggdrasil vegetation – it is not normal ecology; it is accelerating and covering previously barren lands.
* Step 2: Note that this growth is actively reducing human living space, yet there has been no coordinated global response.
* Step 3: Recall that the world’s memory was forcefully “rebooted” after the Great Cataclysm, erasing knowledge of Abel and the Akashic Records.
* Step 4: Extend this reasoning: if memory can be erased, then other cognitive filters could also be active – perhaps the very tendency to question anomalies has been suppressed.
* Step 5: Recognize that she herself did not question Yggdrasil’s spread until now – which is itself evidence of manipulation.
* Step 6: Infer that the perpetrator is the same one who stole the Akashic Records – Abel A. Schönberg.
* Step 7: Conclude that the world’s passivity is not ignorance but engineered perception control – “Perhaps even our very thoughts were being controlled.”

Conclusion: The world’s failure to react to Yggdrasil is not due to stupidity or apathy but to active manipulation of human cognition by Abel, using his “Code” abilities. Siesta’s ability to recognize this manipulation in her own thinking is a high‑order meta‑cognitive skill.

Key Dialogue (paraphrased):

Kimizuka: “They seemed to spread to this land as well. … I believe this place was originally a desert. Yet the reason the area is now thick with trees – Yggdrasil. The special seeds carried by the wind from that great tree have rejuvenated and brought life to the wastelands. But now those seeds have been sown across the world, and the plants that continue to grow at an unnatural pace are slowly, but surely, encroaching on human territories.”

Siesta (softly): “We didn’t even question it. Perhaps even our very thoughts were being controlled. By the thief – who stole Akashic records.”

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Meta‑Cognition: S - Recognizes that her own thoughts may have been manipulated – questioning her own perception.
Causal Inference: A - Links Yggdrasil spread to Abel’s memory theft.
Pattern Recognition: A - Connects Hakutengu’s warning about recording devices to current anomalies.
Epistemological Reasoning: S - Questions the reliability of her own memory and perception at a meta‑level.

Feat 1.4: Using the “Key That Opens Any Lock” – Understanding of Narrative Mechanics and the Singularity’s Role

At the door in the control tower, after Kimizuka fails to pick the lock and Fuubi threatens to break it down, Siesta produces a key from her pocket – one of the “seven tools of the Ace Detective.” She claims it can open any lock. When Kimizuka questions this (suggesting it’s just a cover for her lock‑picking skills), she admits the trick but adds a crucial twist: “However, this one key can always open the doors of the stories you’re involved in.” The door opens with a click. This demonstrates her understanding that their reality follows narrative logic and that Kimizuka, as the Singularity, is the protagonist whose story must progress.

Premises:

1. Lock Premise: The door has a small, complex keyhole. Conventional lock‑picking (wire) fails.
2. Fuubi’s Solution Premise: Fuubi suggests breaking the door down – a violent, brute‑force approach.
3. Tool Premise: Siesta carries a special key that she has used before. It is part of her “seven tools” as the Ace Detective.
4. Previous Instances Premise: In their past adventures, certain “plot‑important” doors have opened for them at exactly the right moment, often with minimal effort.
5. Narrative Premise: Their world is governed by SYSTEM, which operates like a story program. Events follow a logic of causality that rewards protagonist action.
6. Singularity Premise: Kimizuka is the “Singularity” – an existence that unconsciously corrects contradictions and allows the story to reach its intended conclusion.
7. Key Symbolism Premise: The key is not a physical master key but a symbolic object that represents “access to the next story beat.”

Chain of Reasoning:

* Step 1: Observe the failed lock‑picking attempt and Fuubi’s impatient suggestion.
* Step 2: Recall that in previous critical moments, a solution has always appeared when they were truly stuck (e.g., doors opening, enemies appearing at the right time).
* Step 3: Recognize that brute force is inelegant and may damage the mechanism – but more importantly, it violates the “rules” of their narrative world.
* Step 4: Retrieve the special key – not because it is a universal physical tool, but because it is a narrative device that works for the protagonist.
* Step 5: When Kimizuka accuses her of just being a good lock‑pick, admit the trick playfully (“Well, revealing the trick does take away some of the magic”) – this acknowledges the surface‑level explanation.
* Step 6: Then reveal the deeper truth: “However, this one key can always open the doors of the stories you’re involved in.”
* Step 7: Insert the key; the door opens instantly – confirming the narrative logic.
* Step 8: Smile cheerfully, knowing that her understanding of the world’s mechanics is correct.

Conclusion: Siesta operates on a meta‑level – she knows that the world runs on story logic and that key moments are pre‑destined to open for the protagonist (Kimizuka). The key is a physical manifestation of this narrative guarantee.

Key Dialogue (paraphrased):

Fuubi (frustrated): “No choice, we shall break it down?”

Kimizuka: “That sounds so wrong on so many levels.”

Siesta (murmuring, pulling out a key from her pocket): “Maybe, with this.”

Kimizuka: “I always thought it was just a cover for your lock‑picking skills?”

Siesta (cheerful smile): “Well, revealing the trick does take away some of the magic. However, this one key can always open the doors of the stories you’re involved in.”

(Click – the door swings open.)

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Narrative Intelligence: S - Understands that their reality follows story conventions (meta‑cognition about the novel’s structure).
Practical Tool Use: B - Possesses a functional key that works in this context.
Social Deception (Playful): B - Misled Kimizuka about the key’s nature for humor.
Problem‑Solving: A - Chooses the elegant solution (key) over brute force (breaking down).

Feat 1.5: Touching SYSTEM to Recover Memories – Calculated Risk and Trust in the Singularity

At the climax of the prologue, they reach the cracked, weakly glowing triangular pyramid SYSTEM. Kimizuka reaches out and touches it, recovering the memories of the Great Cataclysm’s conclusion. Siesta does not hesitate or object – she trusts that this is the correct course. She also touches it together with him, sharing the experience.

Premises:

1. Memory Storage Premise: SYSTEM / Akashic Records holds the world’s erased memories. The three holy relics were fragments; SYSTEM is the master database.
2. Previous Experience Premise: Kimizuka has touched holy relics before and recovered lost memories. Each touch caused nausea but was ultimately beneficial.
3. Holy Relics Premise: They have already used three holy relics to recover partial memories. The relics fit into a stone slab, which opened the door to SYSTEM.
4. Singularity Premise: Kimizuka’s nature as the Singularity allows him to interface with SYSTEM without being destroyed.
5. Necessity Premise: To understand the ongoing Great Cataclysm (Yggdrasil spread, world anomalies, memory gaps), they need the full memory of the final battle with Abel.
6. Risk Premise: Touching SYSTEM could have side effects – pain, memory overload, or physical harm. But the alternative (remaining ignorant) is worse.
7. Partnership Premise: They have always faced dangers together. She will not let him face this alone.

Chain of Reasoning:

* Step 1: Recognize that this is the same SYSTEM they fought over a year ago, now cracked and malfunctioning.
* Step 2: Recall that Kimizuka’s touch on the holy relics restored memories; this larger SYSTEM should have a similar function.
* Step 3: Understand that the missing memories are the key to understanding why the world is still in crisis.
* Step 4: Assess the risk – the SYSTEM is damaged, but still glowing weakly. It is functional enough to use.
* Step 5: Accept that the risk is necessary. Ignorance is not an option when the Great Cataclysm is ongoing.
* Step 6: Let Kimizuka take the lead – he is the Singularity, so the interface is safer for him.
* Step 7: When he hesitates (“I may end up knowing things I shouldn’t know again”), she does not argue – she simply overlaps her palm with his and touches the relic together.
* Step 8: This shared touch communicates “I’m with you” without words, reducing his psychological burden.
* Step 9: After the memory transfer, when he experiences nausea, she rubs his back – physical comfort.

Conclusion: Siesta makes a calculated decision to recover the lost memories, understanding that this is the only way to move forward. Her non‑verbal support demonstrates emotional intelligence and partnership.

Key Dialogue (paraphrased):

Kimizuka (reaching toward SYSTEM): “I have come to take back our story.”

(He touches SYSTEM; it lights up brightly. Then: “And then I remembered. The continuation of the fight against Abel—the story conclusion.”)

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Risk Assessment: A (Accepts potential danger for necessary information.)
Trust in Partner: A (Defers to Kimizuka as the Singularity for the interface.)
Strategic Memory Recovery: A (Knows the correct sequence - holy relics then SYSTEM).
Emotional Support: A (Provides non‑verbal comfort without patronizing.)

EVENT 2: The Monastery – Kimizuka Awakens Siesta (Flashback)

Context: During the Great Cataclysm, after Kimizuka retrieved Siesta’s “will” from Abel and made the choice to make Nagisa the Ace Detective (leaving the Vampire position vacant), he goes to the sleeping Siesta in the monastery. He bites his lip and kisses her, transferring blood that contains her will. She wakes up.

Note: This is not a cognitive feat of Siesta herself (she is unconscious until the kiss), but upon waking she demonstrates immediate memory retrieval and emotional recognition.

Feat 2.1: Immediate Memory Retrieval Upon Awakening – No Confusion, Full Recall

Upon waking, Siesta immediately recognizes Kimizuka, knows her own codename, and recalls their entire history. She does not suffer amnesia despite the trauma of having her “will” organ stolen, being in a coma for over a year, and undergoing heart transplant surgery.

Premises (implied by her responses):

1. Identity Premise: She knows she is “Siesta” – her codename as the Ace Detective (now Vampire).
2. Partner Recognition Premise: She knows Kimizuka as “Boy K with a knack for getting into trouble, Kimizuka Kimihiko” – using their first meeting nickname.
3. Relationship Definition Premise: She defines him as “my most important – my dearest partner” – indicating full memory of their three years together.
4. Emotional State Premise: She cries and says “I missed you” – showing that her emotional memory is intact.
5. Biological Memory Theory Premise: As Scarlet (the Vampire King) suggested, a person’s will and memories circulate through all their cells via DNA. Even if the “will” organ was stolen, other cells retained the information.

Chain of Reasoning (unconscious but demonstrated by her responses):

· Step 1: Upon regaining consciousness, immediately access long‑term memory storage.
· Step 2: Retrieve her own identity – “Codename, Siesta.”
· Step 3: Identify the person in front of her – cross‑reference facial recognition, voice, and tactile sensation.
· Step 4: Recall their first meeting in Japan (Ice Doll’s mission), not just the plane incident – “Boy K.”
· Step 5: Retrieve the emotional significance of their relationship – “dearest partner.”
· Step 6: Allow emotional expression – tears and verbal affection – without repression.

Conclusion: Siesta’s memory was not stored solely in the stolen organ. Her consciousness and memories were distributed throughout her body (as Scarlet had hypothesized about vampires). The blood transfer restored the missing piece, and the rest was already present.

Key Dialogue (paraphrased):

Kimizuka: “Do you know who you are?”

Siesta: “Codename, Siesta.”

Kimizuka: “Do you know who I am?”

Siesta: “Boy K with a knack for getting into trouble, Kimizuka Kimihiko.”

Kimizuka: “And what am I to you?”

Siesta: “My most important – my dearest partner.”

(Then, crying, clinging to him) “I missed you.”

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Memory Recall: S (Immediately accesses years of memories without confusion or delay.)
Emotional Recognition: A (Accurately identifies Kimizuka’s role and her feelings toward him.)
Identity Integrity: S (Maintains sense of self despite physical and psychological trauma.)

EVENT 3: The Ice Palace – Confronting Ookami (Flashback)

Context: After waking, Siesta joins Kimizuka, Nagisa, and others in the Mizoev Federation (ice continent). In the palace, Ice Doll has gathered the high officials. Siesta fights Ookami (the Enforcer) while Kimizuka searches for Nagisa and Charlotte. This section contains several cognitive feats.

Feat 3.1: Combat Analysis – Reading Ookami’s Fighting Style to Infer His Past

While fighting Ookami, Siesta observes his defensive reflexes and deduces that he is used to protecting someone next to him – implying he had a former partner. She connects this to the previous Enforcer, Amon, and concludes that Ookami was likely his assistant or partner.

Premises:

1. Observation Premise 1: When Siesta fires her musket, Ookami moves his scythe to the right and raises his left arm to protect his vitals.
2. Observation Premise 2: He could have dodged the bullet entirely – his reflexes are fast enough. But he chooses to block and shield.
3. Combat Form Premise: Raising the left arm to protect the chest/head while blocking with a weapon is a defensive posture optimal for protecting someone behind or beside the defender.
4. Timeline Premise: Ookami became Nagisa’s assistant substitute only about a year ago (during Kimizuka’s time as Reloaded’s familiar). Before that, he had another role.
5. Previous Enforcer Premise: The previous Enforcer, Douglas Amon, died. Ookami inherited the scythe.
6. Relationship Premise: Amon and Ookami were known to be close. Ookami’s desire to avenge Amon (against Phantom Thief) was expressed earlier.

Chain of Reasoning:

* Step 1: Engage in combat with Ookami, firing a test shot to gauge his reaction.
* Step 2: Observe his defensive movement – scythe to the right, left arm raised. Note that this is not a pure evasion; it is a protective stance.
* Step 3: Compare to typical solo combat evasions – a solo fighter would duck, roll, or twist away. Raising an arm to shield is instinctive for someone who is used to having a companion behind them.
* Step 4: Recall that Ookami was assigned to protect Nagisa as her assistant substitute. But that assignment is recent.
* Step 5: Infer that this protective reflex must have been developed earlier, with a previous partner.
* Step 6: Connect to the known fact that the previous Enforcer (Amon) was Ookami’s friend. They likely fought together.
* Step 7: Conclude that Ookami was Amon’s partner or assistant before becoming Nagisa’s.
* Step 8: Ask him directly: “Did you ever fight next to someone, even before you became Nagisa’s assistant substitute?”

Conclusion: Ookami’s fighting style reveals a history of partnership, likely with the late Enforcer Amon. This is a subtle piece of combat psychology.

Key Dialogue (paraphrased):

Siesta (after a bullet grazing Ookami’s shoulder): “Good reaction.” … “Hey, can I ask you something? Did you ever fight next to someone, even before you became Nagisa’s assistant substitute? … It looks to me like you’re used to supporting someone next to you.”

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Combat Analysis: A (Reads opponent’s defensive posture to infer past training.)
Psychological Insight: B (Recognizes that protective reflexes indicate a former partner.)
Observational Reasoning: A (Notices the specific blocking pattern - scythe right, left arm up).
Historical Inference: B (Connects to known facts about the previous Enforcer.)

Feat 3.2: Meta‑Reflection on Her Own Narrative Role – “Kimizuka Kimihiko Was My Nucleus from the Beginning”

When Ookami accuses her of having become “ordinary” and no longer the protagonist (because her actions now revolve around Kimizuka rather than him revolving around her), Siesta reflects and then reveals that Kimizuka has always been the center of her story – not the other way around. She redefines the detective‑assistant dynamic and admits (to herself, and indirectly to the reader) that her feelings for him are romantic.

Premises:

1. Ookami’s Accusation Premise: He says: “You are trying to save the world now, in order to affirm Kimizuka Kimihiko wasn’t wrong. Isn’t that the opposite of what you used to do? Your current efforts are all centered around Kimizuka Kimihiko. You used to be the one who dragged him into action.” He concludes: “That’s why you’ve become ordinary. … You’ve fallen out of the protagonist’s throne.”
2. Her Past Behavior Premise: In their three years of travel, she often took the lead, made decisions, and he followed as her assistant.
3. First Meeting Premise: She first met Kimizuka (as “Boy K”) in Japan before the plane incident, when Ice Doll sent her to search for a spy. She was drawn to him immediately.
4. True Motivation Premise: Her decision to make him her assistant was not purely practical – she wanted him by her side.
5. Narrative Truth Premise: Every story has a protagonist. She has been acting as if she were the protagonist, but in reality, her actions have always been in service of her connection to him.

Chain of Reasoning:

* Step 1: Hear Ookami’s accusation. Consider whether it has merit.
* Step 2: Reflect on her own history. She did drag Kimizuka into her cases. But why did she choose him in the first place?
* Step 3: Recall the true origin – not the plane at 10,000 meters, but the earlier meeting in Japan. She was on a government mission, but she noticed him.
* Step 4: Recognize that from that first moment, she was drawn to him. The “Singularity” explanation was a rationalization, a cover for a simpler emotional truth.
* Step 5: Acknowledge that her actions have always been centered around him – even when she seemed to be the leader, she was acting to keep him close.
* Step 6: Conclude that he is her “nucleus” – the core around which her story revolves.
* Step 7: State this truth to Ookami, and then add (with a smile) that it’s “quite fun being at the whim of a boy. The boy I’m crazy about.” – finally admitting the romantic nature of her feelings.
* Step 8: This admission is both a personal revelation and a strategic move – it disarms Ookami’s accusation by redefining the terms of “protagonist.”

Conclusion: Siesta redefines their relationship – he is not just her assistant; she is, in a sense, his follower. This is a profound meta‑cognitive reframing that acknowledges her emotional truth while maintaining her dignity.

Key Dialogue (paraphrased):

Ookami: “You are trying to save the world now, in order to affirm Kimizuka Kimihiko wasn’t wrong. Isn’t that the opposite of what you used to do? Your current efforts are all centered around Kimizuka Kimihiko. … That’s why you’ve become ordinary. … You’ve fallen out of the protagonist’s throne.”

Siesta: “You seem to be a little bit mistaken. … It’s true that I’ve been dragging Kimizuka Kimihiko into my story since that day seven years ago. The detective was the protagonist, and he was the assistant. … But that was just one side of the coin. Just as Kimizuka Kimihiko kept getting caught up in my troubles, I too kept holding onto his hand. … From the very beginning, Kimizuka Kimihiko was my nucleus. It didn’t start in the sky 10,000 meters above. Before that – I had once visited Japan to search for a certain spy at Ice Doll’s behest. The moment I met the boy K, my fate was already decided. … It’s actually quite fun. Being at the whim of a boy. The boy I’m crazy about.”

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Meta‑Cognition: S - Analyzes her own narrative role and relationship dynamics from a third‑person perspective.
Emotional Self‑Awareness: S - Admits (to herself, and then aloud) romantic feelings – a rare vulnerability.
Reframing: S - Reinterprets detective‑assistant dynamic entirely, changing the terms of the accusation.
Strategic Vulnerability: A - Uses emotional honesty to disarm criticism.

EVENT 4: The Ice Palace – Confronting Ice Doll (Flashback)

Context: After dealing with Ookami, Siesta faces Ice Doll, the high‑ranking official who has been the primary government contact for the Tuners. Ice Doll reveals that she is a former Tuner from 400 years ago, stripped of her rank and forced to serve as an immortal hero. She fights with two swords and has a fragment of SYSTEM floating above her head.

Feat 4.1: Deduction That the Mizoev Federation Itself Is the Ark

When Ice Doll mentions the “Ark,” Siesta immediately connects the dots: the floating city on the ice continent is not a normal city but a giant ship. She recalls Stephen’s sketch of a city floating on the sea with a tower‑like building in the middle and realizes it is a blueprint of this place. This deduction reveals the Federation Government’s secret plan: to abandon the dying Earth and escape to “Another Eden.”

Premises:

1. Blueprint Premise: Stephen left a sketch of a city with parks, theaters, and apartment buildings – but the city was floating on the sea, and there was a tower‑like building in the center.
2. Ark Myth Premise: The biblical Noah’s Ark was a ship that saved a select few from a world‑ending flood.
3. Government Plan Premise: The Federation Government, knowing the world is ending (due to Yggdrasil and the damaged SYSTEM), has been preparing an escape plan.
4. Location Premise: They are currently in the Mizoev Federation – an “imaginary country” on an ice continent. The city they saw earlier (before entering the palace) was constructed on what appeared to be a movable platform.
5. Ice Doll’s Statement Premise: Ice Doll says “Charlotte Arisaka Anderson’s father is a high official of the Federation Government. His family’s member would qualify for boarding the Ark.”
6. Charlotte’s Location Premise: Charlotte is missing, and they have reason to believe she is in the Mizoev Federation.

Chain of Reasoning:

* Step 1: Recall Stephen’s sketch from his clinic – a city floating on the sea, with a central tower. At the time, she noted it was unusual but didn’t understand its significance.
* Step 2: Hear Ice Doll use the word “Ark” – a specific term for a ship of salvation.
* Step 3: Observe that the Mizoev Federation is built on ice, but the structures are not anchored to bedrock – they are on a platform.
* Step 4: Connect: the floating city in Stephen’s sketch is the Mizoev Federation.
* Step 5: The central tower is not just a building – it is the ship’s mast or command center.
* Step 6: Infer that the government plans to use this giant city‑ship to escape the Great Cataclysm.
* Step 7: Conclude that Charlotte is being held here because her father (Lot) is an official and wants her on the Ark.
* Step 8: State the conclusion aloud: “That’s right, Charl is here. In this giant Ark called the Mizoev Federation.”

Conclusion: The entire ice country is a functional Noah’s Ark, designed to escape the dying Earth and travel to “Another Eden” – a parallel world.

Key Dialogue (paraphrased):

Ice Doll: “The members of the Federation Government, the key political figures, businessmen, nobilities, and those with special bloodlines. We are in the process of selecting passengers.”

Kimizuka: “…Nobility? Is the Lupwise family included?”

(Later)

Ice Doll: “Charlotte Arisaka Anderson’s father is a high official of the Federation Government. His family’s member would qualify for boarding the Ark.”

Siesta: “That’s right, Charl is here. In this giant Ark called the Mizoev Federation.” (To Kimizuka) “Stephen happened to leave me a blueprint of this country. At first glance, it’s just a normal sketch with parks, theaters, and apartment buildings. But Siesta had noticed. The fact that the city was floating on the sea. And there was a tower‑like building in the middle. Siesta believed the tower was the ship’s mast.”

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Pattern Recognition: S - Connects a sketch of a floating city to the actual ice palace.
Abductive Reasoning: S - Infers the government’s secret plan from sparse clues (sketch, the word “Ark,” Charlotte’s location).
Mythological Knowledge: B - Knows the Noah’s Ark story and its implications.
Spatial Reasoning A Visualizes how a city can function as a ship (tower as mast, floating platform as hull).

Feat 4.2: Deduction About Ice Doll’s True Identity as a Former Tuner (Holy Knight)

After Ice Doll reveals she is a former Tuner from 400 years ago, Siesta asks pointed questions that lead Ice Doll to disclose her original position – Holy Knight. Siesta pieces together that the government stripped immortal heroes of their Tuner ranks to use them as nameless, expendable assets.

Premises:

1. Ice Doll’s Combat Prowess Premise: She fights with two swords, her speed and skill matching Siesta’s. She is clearly not a mere bureaucrat.
2. Aging Anomaly Premise: Ice Doll mentions that she stopped aging due to a drug given by a past Inventor. She has been alive for over 400 years.
3. Tuner System Premise: There are only twelve Tuner positions at any time. These positions are granted by SYSTEM based on will and heroic deeds.
4. Government Manipulation Premise: The nobles who controlled the Federation Government wanted to keep immortal heroes as enforcers but did not want them to have the authority that comes with official Tuner ranks.
5. Historical Practice Premise: The government has a history of demoting or dismissing Tuners who become inconvenient (e.g., Bruno, the Vampire clan).
6. Ice Doll’s Emotional State Premise: She is bitter and cynical, suggesting long‑term mistreatment.

Chain of Reasoning:

* Step 1: Observe Ice Doll’s swordsmanship – it is the skill of a seasoned warrior, honed over centuries, not a politician.
* Step 2: Hear her mention she fought as a Tuner 400 years ago. Note the discrepancy: if she was a Tuner then, why is she now a government official?
* Step 3: Recall that the number of Tuners is fixed at twelve. If she is still alive and still has her abilities, she must have been replaced.
* Step 4: Infer that the government would want to keep such powerful beings but limit their authority – make them servants rather than heroes.
* Step 5: Conclude that they were stripped of their Tuner ranks, becoming nameless operatives working for the government.
* Step 6: Ask Ice Doll to confirm: “Ice Doll, if you were a former Tuner, what was your position? … How long have you been a Tuner?”
* Step 7: Ice Doll refuses to name her position (“There is probably no one left who remembers my position”), but later Siesta identifies it as “Holy Knight” – likely from contextual clues or prior knowledge.

Conclusion: Ice Doll was once the Tuner “Holy Knight,” forced into centuries of anonymous service. The government systematically erased the identities of immortal heroes to control them.

Key Dialogue (paraphrased):

Siesta: “Ice Doll, if you were a former Tuner, what was your position? … And that swordsmanship. I heard that there was something like Master Fencer until a little while ago. How long have you been a Tuner?”

Ice Doll: “I am not Master Fencer, and I have no need to identify myself. There is probably no one left who remembers my position. The last time I stood on the battlefield as a Tuner was four hundred years ago.”

(Later, after the battle, Siesta to Kimizuka): “Holy Knight – Ice Doll.”

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Historical Inference: A - Infers 400‑year history from sparse clues (aging drug, combat skill, cynicism).
Character Reading: A - Recognizes that Ice Doll is not just a politician but a warrior with centuries of experience.
Systemic Analysis: S - Understands the political manipulation of Tuner ranks (stripping titles to control heroes).
Deductive Reasoning: A - Connects fixed number of Tuners to the need to demote immortals.

Feat 4.3: Deduction That Abel Hid the Akashic Records Inside Yggdrasil

During the final battle, as Yggdrasil rampages, Siesta connects the dots: Abel, the Phantom Thief, stole the Akashic Records, but SYSTEM recovered only part. The missing part was hidden inside Seed (the primordial parasite), which later became Yggdrasil. This explains why Yggdrasil became the world destroyer – it is trying to expel the foreign data.

Premises:

1. Theft Premise: Abel stole the Akashic Records (the brain of SYSTEM) during the Great Cataclysm.
2. Recovery Premise: SYSTEM eventually “took back” the Akashic Records from Abel, absorbing his physical body in the process. But SYSTEM still malfunctions – implying the recovery was incomplete.
3. Phantom Thief’s Nature Premise: Abel’s power as the Phantom Thief is to steal things in such a way that the victim does not even realize they are missing. The theft is “invisible.”
4. Seed Connection Premise: Abel collaborated with Seed (the primordial parasite) years ago during the incident with the fake sacred text. He injected his “Code” into Seed.
5. Yggdrasil’s Origin Premise: Seed was the core of Yggdrasil, the great tree that spread across the world. After the Great Cataclysm, Yggdrasil continued to grow and encroach on human territory.
6. Yggdrasil’s Behavior Premise: Yggdrasil is not maliciously destroying the world – it is acting erratically, as if something inside it is trying to get out. The white flowers and orange particles (pollen) are releasing data.
7. Lil’s Warning Premise: Lil (Magical Girl) told Kimizuka that world crises always have a hidden cause, and they should not overlook it.

Chain of Reasoning:

* Step 1: Recall that Abel and Seed met during the sacred text incident (Volume 9). At that time, Abel gave Seed his “Code” – a program that can manipulate reality.
* Step 2: Understand that Abel’s “Code” could have been used to hide stolen data inside Seed’s biological structure.
* Step 3: When SYSTEM tried to recover the Akashic Records from Abel, it only retrieved the part that was still in Abel’s possession. The part hidden inside Seed remained.
* Step 4: Seed became Yggdrasil (the great tree) after the Great Cataclysm. The stolen Akashic Records fragment is therefore still inside Yggdrasil.
* Step 5: Yggdrasil’s rampage is not an attack – it is an attempt to expel the foreign data. The white flowers and orange pollen are the “repair program” trying to return the stolen records to the Earth.
* Step 6: Conclude that Yggdrasil is not the enemy; it is a victim of Abel’s theft, and it has been trying to return what was hidden inside it.
* Step 7: State the conclusion to Kimizuka and Nagisa: “Abel hid the stolen Akashic records inside Yggdrasil. … The Akashic records were actually placed in front of our very eyes.”

Conclusion: Yggdrasil is not a world‑destroying monster but a suffering organism trying to return stolen data. This deduction solves the central mystery of the Great Cataclysm and points to the solution: Nagisa must use her Word Soul to command Yggdrasil to return the Akashic Records.

Key Dialogue (paraphrased):

Kimizuka: “Has Abel not returned everything he stole? In other words, he hid them somewhere SYSTEM couldn’t find?”

Siesta (putting finger to chin, reflecting): “There’s one more thing Phantom Thief had stolen. The sacred text. Abel stole the fake sacred text that Mia and I made at the time to deceive Seed. In other words, the two antagonists met at that time. … Abel hid the stolen Akashic records inside Yggdrasil. … Those who have something stolen by the Phantom Thief will not be even conscious of that fact. Even SYSTEM, the administrator of this world, can still be deceived. The Akashic records were actually placed in front of our very eyes.”

(Later, to Nagisa): “Yggdrasil’s original role is as a medium for memory. This pollen is the repair program that will restore the original world.”

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Long‑term Causal Chain: S - Connects events from four years ago (sacred text incident) to current crisis.
Abductive Reasoning: S - Infers hidden location from Yggdrasil’s anomalous behavior (rampage, white flowers, pollen).
Understanding of Phantom Thief’s Power: S - Knows that victims don’t realize what’s stolen – applies this to SYSTEM itself.
Synthesis of Multiple Data Points: S - Integrates: Abel’s Code, Seed’s nature, Yggdrasil’s growth, SYSTEM malfunction, Lil’s warning.
Scientific Reasoning: B - Uses the concept of “repair program” and “data expulsion” as an analogy.

Feat 4.4: Empathic Reading of Ice Doll’s True Wish – Letting Her Stop Her Own Attack

At the climax of their fight, Ice Doll raises her sword to kill Siesta. Siesta does not dodge or counter – she simply looks into Ice Doll’s eyes. Ice Doll stops the attack herself. Siesta then says, “Is that your will, Ice Doll?” – recognizing that Ice Doll never truly wanted to kill her. This is a masterful use of emotional intelligence and strategic vulnerability.

Premises:

1. Ice Doll’s History Premise: She fought for 400 years as a nameless hero, stripped of her Tuner rank, unable to die, trapped in endless battles.
2. Emotional State Premise: She is exhausted – physically and spiritually. She has seen countless world crises, saved countless people, but never received recognition or rest.
3. Suicidal Subtext Premise: Her support of the Ark Project – abandoning the dying Earth – is a form of escape. She wants to stop fighting forever.
4. The Ark as Surrender Premise: Choosing the Ark means giving up on saving the world. It is an admission that 400 years of heroism were meaningless.
5. True Desire Premise: Deep down, Ice Doll wants to be proven wrong. She wants to see that the eternal fight has meaning – that someone can succeed where she failed.
6. Siesta as a Mirror Premise: Siesta represents what Ice Doll once was: a young, passionate hero who still believes in saving everyone.
7. Projection Premise: Ice Doll projects her own despair onto Siesta, but also her hope. Killing Siesta would mean killing her own younger self.

Chain of Reasoning:

* Step 1: Observe Ice Doll’s hesitation during the fight. She could have killed Siesta earlier but did not.
* Step 2: Recall Ice Doll’s words: “I have no intention of laying a hand on you [the Singularity]” – she is reluctant to harm Kimizuka, but she attacks Siesta freely. Yet even then, her attacks lack killing intent.
* Step 3: Recognize that Ice Doll is not fighting to win – she is fighting because she has no other purpose. She is going through the motions of a hero because she doesn’t know how to stop.
* Step 4: Understand that what Ice Doll truly wants is not to kill Siesta but to see if Siesta will prove her wrong – to show that justice can continue without sacrificing oneself.
* Step 5: Decide on a radical strategy: instead of countering, stand still. Offer no resistance. Make Ice Doll confront her own action.
* Step 6: Look directly into Ice Doll’s eyes – communicate silently: “I trust you. You don’t really want to do this.”
* Step 7: Ice Doll’s sword stops. She throws it away and kneels.
* Step 8: Ask the question: “Is that your will, Ice Doll?” – affirming that Ice Doll made a choice, not Siesta. This gives Ice Doll agency and dignity.
* Step 9: Then make a promise: “We will not stop walking, stop fighting. … There will be companions, those who will remember me and pass on my story.” – offering a new path: not eternal solitude, but shared struggle with successors.

Conclusion: Siesta’s empathy and willingness to trust Ice Doll’s better nature defuses the fight without violence. She recognizes that Ice Doll’s aggression is a cry for help, not a genuine desire to kill.

Key Dialogue (paraphrased):

(Ice Doll raises her sword; Siesta does not move.)

(The blade stops mid‑swing.)

Siesta: “Is that your will, Ice Doll?”

(Ice Doll throws away her sword, kneels.)

Siesta: “Ice Doll. We will not stop walking, stop fighting. … Even if I lose my name, even if I forget my rank, even if the day comes when I lose sight of my purpose. There will be companions, those who will remember me and pass on my story. We will keep running towards the uncertain future.”

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Emotional Intelligence: S - Reads Ice Doll’s internal conflict (400 years of despair masked as aggression) and true desire (to be proven wrong).
Strategic Non‑Violence: S - Chooses vulnerability (standing still) as a tactic to force Ice Doll to confront her own will.
Psychological Insight: S - Understands that Ice Doll is projecting her own younger self onto Siesta.
Empathy: S - Recognizes suffering beneath the mask of a ruthless official.
De‑escalation: A - Ends the fight without further bloodshed.

EVENT 5: The Yggdrasil Battle – Coordinating the Final Solution (Flashback)

Context: After Ice Doll surrenders, the group turns to the rampaging Yggdrasil. Nagisa steps forward and uses her Word Soul to command Yggdrasil to return the Akashic Records. Siesta provides support and analysis.

Feat 5.1: Understanding That Nagisa’s Word Soul Is the Key to Commanding Yggdrasil

Siesta recognizes that Nagisa, who inherited the “seed” that granted her the Word Soul ability, has a unique connection to Yggdrasil (which originated from Seed). Therefore, Nagisa is the only one who can communicate with Yggdrasil and command it to release the stolen Akashic Records.

Premises:

1. Seed Connection Premise: Yggdrasil grew from the primordial Seed, the same entity that parasitized Nagisa and gave her the Word Soul ability.
2. Nagisa’s Unique Ability Premise: Nagisa can use “Word Soul” – the power to command reality through passionate speech. This ability came from the seed inside her.
3. Yggdrasil’s Intelligence Premise: Yggdrasil is not just a plant; it has a form of consciousness derived from Seed. It can respond to commands.
4. Akashic Records Location Premise: The stolen Akashic Records fragment is hidden inside Yggdrasil. Yggdrasil has been trying to expel it but cannot on its own.
5. Nagisa’s Role Premise: As someone who shares biological material with Yggdrasil (via the seed), Nagisa can interface with it.

Chain of Reasoning:

* Step 1: Observe that Yggdrasil is releasing orange particles (pollen) – these are data packets, the “repair program.”
* Step 2: Understand that Yggdrasil is trying to return the stolen records but lacks the will or command to do so fully.
* Step 3: Recall that Nagisa’s Word Soul ability came from a seed that originated from the same source as Yggdrasil.
* Step 4: Infer that Nagisa, therefore, has a biological and metaphysical connection to Yggdrasil.
* Step 5: Conclude that Nagisa can use her Word Soul to command Yggdrasil – essentially, to give it the “push” it needs to release the records.
* Step 6: Communicate this to Kimizuka and Nagisa, setting up the final resolution.

Key Dialogue (paraphrased):

(After Siesta’s deduction about Abel hiding the records in Yggdrasil)

Nagisa (stepping forward): “Speaking of which, I left everything to that girl, without ever saying goodbye.” (She walks toward Yggdrasil, frozen plants crawling up her feet.)

Siesta (to Kimizuka, stopping him from following): “Ah, but I don’t have any feelings for you, in fact, only resentment…” (This is misdirection; she is letting Nagisa act alone.)

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Biological Reasoning: B - Understands that shared origin (Seed) creates a connection between Nagisa and Yggdrasil.
Strategic Delegation: A - Recognizes that Nagisa is the right person for this task, not herself.
Understanding of Word Soul: A - Knows the limits and potential of Nagisa’s ability.

EVENT 6: The Cherry Blossom Epilogue – Philosophical Reflection and Relationship Management

Context: After the Great Cataclysm is resolved and the world returns to normal, the group holds a cherry blossom viewing party. Siesta is present, along with Kimizuka, Nagisa, Charlotte, Saikawa, Mia, Lil, Noel, and Noches. Kimizuka reflects on whether this is truly a happy ending, given that SYSTEM has been restored and world crises will continue.

Feat 6.1: Understanding That Kimizuka Still Feels Unfulfilled – Offering to Wait

When Kimizuka mentions (in a flashback within the epilogue) that he had something important to tell Siesta “when this case is over,” and then in the present he hesitates, Nagisa excuses herself to give them space. Kimizuka, flustered, says it’s not the right time. Siesta immediately accepts this without pressure, saying “It’s okay. I’ll wait for you, no matter how long it takes. You two are the ones who gave me tomorrow.”

Premises:

1. Kimizuka’s Hesitation Premise: He is not ready to say what he wants to say (the reader understands this is likely a confession of romantic feelings).
2. Nagisa’s Consideration Premise: Nagisa leaves to give them privacy, showing she is aware of the tension and is stepping back.
3. Siesta’s Awareness Premise: She knows what Kimizuka wants to say (or can infer it) and understands why he is hesitating.
4. Prior Commitment Premise: She promised to wait for him at the bar before they left for the Mizoev Federation.
5. The Value of Patience Premise: Rushing emotional confessions often ruins them. The right moment will come naturally.
6. Gratitude Premise: She acknowledges that Nagisa and Kimizuka saved her (by waking her up), so she owes them her future.

Chain of Reasoning:

* Step 1: Observe Kimizuka’s awkwardness and Nagisa’s retreat.
* Step 2: Recognize that pushing him would be counterproductive – he needs to find the right moment himself.
* Step 3: Acknowledge that she has already waited over a year in a coma; a little longer is nothing.
* Step 4: Reassure him verbally: “It’s okay. I’ll wait for you, no matter how long it takes.”
* Step 5: Add “You two are the ones who gave me tomorrow” – this reframes her waiting not as a burden but as a gift she is happy to receive.
* Step 6: Do not pressure or tease him further. Accept the situation.

Conclusion: Siesta demonstrates patience, emotional maturity, and gratitude. She prioritizes the relationship over immediate gratification and trusts that the right moment will come.

Key Dialogue (paraphrased):

Kimizuka (thinking about his promise at the bar): “I had said I wanted to tell Siesta something when this case was over. … My bad, Siesta. It seems that it’s not the time yet.”

Siesta: “It’s okay. I’ll wait for you, no matter how long it takes. You two are the ones who gave me tomorrow.”

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Emotional Intelligence: S - Reads Kimizuka’s hesitation and respects it without pressure.
Patience: A - Willing to wait indefinitely for the right moment.
Gratitude Expression: B - Acknowledges that Nagisa and Kimizuka saved her future.
Relationship Wisdom: S - Knows that forcing a confession would ruin it.

Feat 6.2: Final Line – “It’s just love at first sight, stupid Kimi” – Redefining the Entire Series

In the final lines of the volume, Kimizuka reflects on their history: “Siesta. You made a choice that day, remember? You chose the Singularity. She had deduced this constitution of mine would make her job easier. Or perhaps, that was the mission of generations of past Ace Detectives.” Then Siesta, as if overhearing his thoughts (or as part of the narrative), says: “Singularity? What’s that? It’s just love at first sight, stupid Kimi.” She then walks away, leaving him stunned.

Premises (implicit in her statement):

1. Kimizuka’s Logical Explanation Premise: He has always believed (or been told) that Siesta chose him as her assistant because his Singularity nature would help her solve cases.
2. The Rationalization Premise: That explanation was a convenient cover – a way for Siesta to justify her attraction to herself and to others.
3. The True Feeling Premise: The real reason is much simpler and more human: she fell in love with him the first time she saw him.
4. The Meta‑Narrative Premise: The entire series has been built on this foundation – not cosmic destiny, but a personal, irrational, romantic choice.
5. Humor as Delivery Premise: She says it in a teasing, almost dismissive tone (“stupid Kimi”) to avoid sentimentality.

Chain of Reasoning (Siesta’s internal thought process, inferred):

* Step 1: Hear Kimizuka’s internal monologue (or sense his thoughts) about the “Singularity” explanation.
* Step 2: Realize that he is still clinging to that logical framework – he needs to hear the truth.
* Step 3: Decide to finally tell him, after seven years of dancing around it.
* Step 4: Dismiss the “Singularity” concept with feigned ignorance (“Singularity? What’s that?”) – this is playful, not literal.
* Step 5: State the simple, emotional truth: “It’s just love at first sight.”
* Step 6: Add “stupid Kimi” – a term of endearment disguised as an insult, maintaining their bantering dynamic.
* Step 7: Walk away without waiting for a response – she doesn’t need one; she just wanted him to know.

Conclusion: This line redefines the entire series. The grand cosmic machinery of SYSTEM, Tuners, Singularity, and world crises is ultimately secondary to a simple human emotion: love at first sight. Siesta cuts through all metaphysical complexity and reveals her deepest motivation.

Key Dialogue (paraphrased):

Kimizuka (thinking): “Siesta. You made a choice that day, remember? You chose the Singularity. She had deduced this constitution of mine would make her job easier. Or perhaps, that was the mission of generations of past Ace Detectives. She was the one who had found me in the sky and promoted me as her assistant that day, so I wanted us to depend on each other a little bit more.”

Siesta (as if responding to his thoughts): “Singularity? What’s that? It’s just love at first sight, stupid Kimi.” (She chuckles and starts walking away.)

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Emotional Honesty: S - Abandons all rational explanations for a vulnerable, simple confession.
Humor: A - Delivers the line with a teasing tone to avoid sentimentality.
Relationship Wisdom: S - Knows that the real reason for their partnership is emotional, not logical.
Meta‑Narrative Awareness: S - Understands that the story’s foundation is a personal choice, not cosmic destiny.
Timing: A - Chooses the perfect moment to reveal this – after the crisis, during a quiet moment.

Overall Cognitive Tier for Volume 11: S (Genius) – Siesta demonstrates a wide range of cognitive abilities: practical survival, meta‑cognition about memory manipulation, long‑term causal reasoning connecting events across years, emotional intelligence in reading both allies (Nagisa, Kimizuka) and enemies (Ice Doll), strategic non‑violence, and finally a profound emotional honesty that reframes the entire series’ premise. Her deduction that Abel hid the Akashic Records inside Yggdrasil is the volume’s most impressive logical feat, solving the central mystery of the Great Cataclysm. The final line – “It’s just love at first sight” – is not just a romantic confession but a meta‑cognitive dismissal of the entire logical framework that has governed the series, revealing that human emotion is the true engine of the story.

VOLUME 12

I still haven't read it 💯🥲

VOLUME 13

Note on Volume 13's Structure: This volume is a complex narrative that weaves together three distinct temporal threads:

1. The Present (Prologue): After recovering memories via the Holy Relics, Siesta, Kimizuka, and Fuubi explore the control tower and recover the final memories of the Great Cataclysm.
2. The Great Cataclysm Flashback: The events of the final battle against Abel, the confrontation with the Federation Government, and the awakening of Siesta as "Vampire."
3. Another Eden Flashback: Kimizuka is transported to Another Eden and encounters child versions of Siesta and Nagisa (programs created by Alicia), learning the tragic history of that parallel world.

EVENT 1: The Jungle Trek – Survival, Memory Recovery, and Meta‑Cognition (From the Future: A Prologue)

Context: Siesta, Kimizuka, and Fuubi trek through a dense jungle on a remote island to reach an ancient control tower where the Akashic Records (SYSTEM) is located. The jungle is overgrown due to Yggdrasil seeds spreading worldwide. Siesta walks ahead, seemingly unaffected by the difficult terrain, and demonstrates practical survival skills and meta‑cognitive awareness about the world's memory manipulation.

Feat 1: Fire Creation from a Hemp Wick – Practical Survival Reasoning Under Threat

When a snake suddenly lunges at Siesta, she instantly creates a small flame from a hemp wick and uses it to startle the snake away. She does not rely on modern tools (lighter, matches) but uses a primitive fire‑starting method, demonstrating knowledge of both survival techniques and animal behavior. This is not a random skill – it reflects years of field experience traveling through dangerous environments.

Premises:

1. Environmental Premise: They are trekking through a humid jungle; snakes are common in such environments. The jungle is overgrown due to Yggdrasil seeds, making visibility and footing difficult.
2. Threat Premise: A snake bares its sharp fangs and lunges directly at Siesta. The snake is fast and close – drawing a gun or knife would be too slow. The snake's trajectory suggests it is aiming for her leg or torso.
3. Tool Premise: Siesta carries a hemp wick – a dry, flammable plant fiber. She has it prepared (likely pre‑charred or kept dry in a waterproof container). The fact that she carries such a tool indicates she anticipates needing fire in various situations.
4. Ignition Knowledge Premise: Hemp wick can be ignited by friction (rubbing against a rough surface) or by a spark from a flint. Siesta likely has a hidden fire‑starting tool (or the wick was already smoldering). Her speed suggests she is practiced at this.
5. Animal Behavior Premise: Most wild animals, including snakes, are startled by sudden bright light or heat. Fire is an ancient animal deterrent. Snakes are ectothermic and sensitive to infrared heat; a sudden flame mimics a predator's body heat or creates an aversive stimulus.
6. Safety Premise: A small, controlled flame is safer than a gunshot (which could ricochet or cause a larger fire) or a knife (which requires close contact and risks injury).
7. Speed Premise: The snake is attacking now – there is no time for elaborate preparation. The solution must be instantaneous.
8. Psychological Impact Premise: The casual remark "it's just a snake" maintains morale and prevents panic.

Chain of Reasoning:

* Perceive the snake's attack trajectory – it is aiming for her leg or torso. The snake's fangs are visible.
* Assess available responses: gun (too slow, risk of missing, risk of hitting Kimizuka or Fuubi), knife (requires close combat, risk of bite, requires dropping other equipment), shouting (ineffective against an attacking animal), or fire (fast deterrent with no physical contact).
* Recall that snakes are ectothermic and sensitive to infrared heat; a sudden flame creates an aversive stimulus that triggers a flight response.
* Retrieve the hemp wick from her pocket or belt – she keeps it accessible for precisely such situations. This is not an improvised tool but a pre‑prepared one.
* Ignite the wick – using a hidden flint, a pre‑lit ember from earlier, or friction against her metal belt buckle (not shown but implied by her speed). The ignition must happen in under a second.
* Present the flame toward the snake – the sudden burst of light and heat startles the animal, overriding its attack instinct.
* The snake twists its body and flees – confirming the tactic worked.
* Immediately control the flame to prevent spreading fire in the dry jungle. She extinguishes or contains it.
* Casually remarked to Kimizuka that it was "just a snake," minimizing the danger and maintaining calm.

Conclusion: Siesta's choice of a non‑lethal, primitive fire deterrent is optimal for the situation – it is fast, safe (no risk of friendly fire), effective against the specific animal, and requires minimal energy. This demonstrates practical survival knowledge honed over years of field work.

Key Dialogue (paraphrased):

Kimizuka: "Siesta!" (warning of the snake)

Siesta (calmly): "Sorry, can't really give you my blood."

(A small flame rises from her hand; the snake twists and flees)

Siesta (looking exasperated at Kimizuka): "You don't need to shout so loud. It's just a snake."

(Narrator: "In her hand was the burnt end of a hemp wick.")

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Practical Survival: A - Knows how to create fire from natural materials under pressure.
Animal Behavior Knowledge: B - Understands that sudden flame deters snakes.
Reaction Speed (Problem‑solving): A - Chooses the optimal deterrent in under a second.
Risk Assessment: B - Evaluates that fire is safer than weapons in this context.
Tool Preparedness: B - Carries a hemp wick for fire‑starting – shows forethought.
Emotional Regulation: A - Remains calm and minimizes the incident.

Feat 2: Rhetorical Question About What to Take to a Deserted Island – Strategic Self‑Positioning and Relationship Management

When Kimizuka asks what she would take to a deserted island, Siesta first answers "I don't really need anything in particular" – demonstrating self‑sufficiency. Then, after Kimizuka lists practical tools (knife, rope, fishing rod), she rephrases: "That's the case, so I would take you with me. Boredom kills, after all." This is not mere flirtation but a strategic acknowledgment of their partnership, delivered with humor to avoid sentimentality. It also serves to boost Kimizuka's morale during a difficult trek.

Premises:

1. Self‑Sufficiency Premise: Siesta can survive alone in the wilderness – she can create fire, catch fish, build shelter, find water. She needs no external tools. This is a statement of fact, not arrogance.
2. Kimizuka's Answer Premise: He says he would take her ("Knowledge and experience, this girl has it all"), which is a compliment but also practical. He acknowledges her value.
3. Social Dynamic Premise: Their relationship is built on teasing and banter, not overt emotional declarations. Direct sentimentality would be out of character and uncomfortable for both.
4. Psychological Need Premise: Even if she can survive alone, isolation is mentally taxing. A companion alleviates boredom and provides backup. This is a practical acknowledgment of human psychology.
5. Humor as Defense Premise: She uses a joke ("Boredom kills") to avoid saying something overly sentimental, while still affirming that she values his company.
6. Morale Boost Premise: Kimizuka is struggling with the difficult trek. This exchange lightens the mood and reminds him of their partnership.

Chain of Reasoning:

* Hear Kimizuka's question – it is hypothetical but reveals his priorities. He is trying to engage in conversation during a difficult trek.
* Evaluate her own survival capabilities – she truly needs no tools. This is an honest self‑assessment.
* Answer honestly but plainly: "I don't really need anything in particular." This is not arrogance but factual.
* Listen to his follow‑up: "Well, Siesta, I would take you" – he has turned the question back on her, implying he values her more than any tool.
* Recognize that a straightforward "I would take you too" would be too cloying for their dynamic. It would also undermine her self‑sufficient persona.
* Instead, rephrase her answer: "That's the case, so I would take you with me" – accepting his compliment while reframing it as a practical choice.
* Add the humorous justification: "Boredom kills, after all" – which both teases him (implying he is entertainment, not a survival asset) and affirms that she wants his company.
* When he retorts "So I'm just a clown?", she smiles and takes his hand, completing the gesture.

Conclusion: The answer is a masterclass in balancing honesty, humor, and affection. She acknowledges his value while maintaining her self‑sufficient persona.

Key Dialogue (paraphrased):

Kimizuka: "Well, Siesta, I would take you. Knowledge and experience, this girl has it all. Far more useful than taking any tool, she and I are capable of overcoming any difficulty together."

Siesta: "Are you stupid, Kimi?"

Kimizuka: "You're so unfair. … Siesta, what about you? If you could take only one thing with you, what would it be?"

Siesta: "Eh, I don't really need anything in particular."

Kimizuka: "Figures. … That's the case, so I would take you with me."

Siesta: "Boredom kills, after all."

Kimizuka: "So I'm just a clown?"

(She smiles and accepts his hand.)

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Social Intelligence: A - Uses humor to express partnership without sentimentality.
Self‑Assessment: B - Accurately knows her own survival capabilities.
Emotional Communication: A - Conveys affection indirectly through banter.
Strategic Framing: B - Reframes his compliment as a practical choice.
Morale Management: A - Boosts Kimizuka's spirits during a difficult trek.

Feat 3: Recognition of the World's Memory Manipulation – Meta‑Cognition About Thought Control

As they approach the colossal ruin (the control tower), Siesta remarks that they "didn't even question it" – referring to the world's anomalies (Yggdrasil seeds spreading unnaturally, encroaching on human territory). She connects this to Abel stealing the Akashic Records and controlling human thoughts. This demonstrates her ability to step back and question her own perception – a form of meta‑cognition that is rare even among the Tuners.

Premises:

1. Anomaly Premise: Yggdrasil seeds have spread worldwide, causing plants to grow at an unnatural pace, slowly encroaching on human territories. This is not normal ecology – it is accelerating and covering previously barren lands.
2. Human Passivity Premise: Despite this obvious crisis, humanity has not reacted with urgency. The problem has been "accepted" as normal. There is no coordinated global response.
3. Memory Loss Premise: The world's memories of the final battle with Abel are missing. Key terms ("Phantom Thief," "Akashic Records," "Singularity") have been erased from collective consciousness.
4. Controlled Perception Premise: If memories can be erased, other cognitive functions – such as pattern recognition, suspicion, and questioning – might also be suppressed. The ability to question anomalies might be actively disabled.
5. Enemy Capability Premise: Abel (Phantom Thief) has the power to inject "Code" that alters reality, including human perception and memory. His power is not just about memory – it can rewrite how people think.
6. Historical Precedent Premise: Hakutengu (from earlier volumes) mentioned that there are devices scattered around the world that record the past and future, and that these devices can be manipulated. This is part of a larger system of world management.
7. Self‑Reflection Premise: She herself did not question Yggdrasil's spread until now – which is itself evidence of manipulation. If even she, a trained detective, failed to notice, the manipulation must be extensive.

Chain of Reasoning:

* Observe the rapid, unnatural growth of Yggdrasil vegetation – it is not normal ecology; it is accelerating and covering previously barren lands.
* Note that this growth is actively reducing human living space, yet there has been no coordinated global response. Humanity is acting as if this is normal.
* Recall that the world's memory was forcefully "rebooted" after the Great Cataclysm, erasing knowledge of Abel and the Akashic Records.
* Extend this reasoning: if memory can be erased, then other cognitive filters could also be active – perhaps the very tendency to question anomalies has been suppressed.
* Recognize that she herself did not question Yggdrasil's spread until now – which is itself evidence of manipulation. This is a crucial moment of self‑awareness.
* Infer that the perpetrator is the same one who stole the Akashic Records – Abel A. Schönberg. His Code can manipulate not just physical reality but also human cognition.
* Conclude that the world's passivity is not ignorance but engineered perception control – "Perhaps even our very thoughts were being controlled."

Conclusion: The world's failure to react to Yggdrasil is not due to stupidity or apathy but to active manipulation of human cognition by Abel. Siesta's ability to recognize this manipulation in her own thinking is a high‑order meta‑cognitive skill.

Key Dialogue (paraphrased):

Kimizuka: "They seemed to spread to this land as well. … I believe this place was originally a desert. Yet the reason the area is now thick with trees – Yggdrasil. The special seeds carried by the wind from that great tree have rejuvenated and brought life to the wastelands. But now those seeds have been sown across the world, and the plants that continue to grow at an unnatural pace are slowly, but surely, encroaching on human territories."

Siesta (softly): "We didn't even question it. Perhaps even our very thoughts were being controlled. By the thief – who stole Akashic records."

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Meta‑Cognition: S - Recognizes that her own thoughts may have been manipulated – questioning her own perception.
Causal Inference: A - Links Yggdrasil spread to Abel's memory theft.
Pattern Recognition: A - Connects Hakutengu's warning about recording devices to current anomalies.
Epistemological Reasoning: S - Questions the reliability of her own memory and perception at a meta‑level.
Self‑Reflection: S - Recognizes that she herself failed to question the anomalies earlier.

Feat 4: Using the "Key That Opens Any Lock" – Understanding of Narrative Mechanics and the Singularity's Role

At the door in the control tower, after Kimizuka fails to pick the lock and Fuubi threatens to break it down, Siesta produces a key from her pocket – one of the "seven tools of the Ace Detective." She claims it can open any lock. When Kimizuka questions this, she admits the trick but adds a crucial twist: "However, this one key can always open the doors of the stories you're involved in." The door opens with a click. This demonstrates her understanding that their reality follows narrative logic and that Kimizuka, as the Singularity, is the protagonist whose story must progress.

Premises:

1. Lock Premise: The door has a small, complex keyhole. Conventional lock‑picking (wire) fails. The lock is mechanical, not electronic.
2. Fuubi's Solution Premise: Fuubi suggests breaking the door down – a violent, brute‑force approach that could damage the mechanism or trigger traps.
3. Tool Premise: Siesta carries a special key that she has used before. It is part of her "seven tools" as the Ace Detective – a set of items that have symbolic significance.
4. Previous Instances Premise: In their past adventures, certain "plot‑important" doors have opened for them at exactly the right moment, often with minimal effort. This is not coincidence but narrative logic.
5. Narrative Premise: Their world is governed by SYSTEM, which operates like a story program. Events follow a logic of causality that rewards protagonist action. The world is structured like a narrative.
6. Singularity Premise: Kimizuka is the "Singularity" – an existence that unconsciously corrects contradictions and allows the story to reach its intended conclusion. He is the protagonist.
7. Key Symbolism Premise: The key is not a physical master key but a symbolic object that represents "access to the next story beat." It works because the narrative requires it to work.

Chain of Reasoning:

* Observe the failed lock‑picking attempt and Fuubi's impatient suggestion.
* Recall that in previous critical moments, a solution has always appeared when they were truly stuck.
* Recognize that brute force is inelegant and may damage the mechanism – but more importantly, it violates the "rules" of their narrative world.
* Retrieve the special key – not because it is a universal physical tool, but because it is a narrative device that works for the protagonist.
* When Kimizuka accuses her of just being a good lock‑pick, admit the trick playfully – this acknowledges the surface‑level explanation while hinting at the deeper truth.
* Then reveal the deeper truth: "However, this one key can always open the doors of the stories you're involved in." This is a direct statement of narrative logic.
* Insert the key; the door opens instantly – confirming the narrative logic.

Conclusion: Siesta operates on a meta‑level – she knows that the world runs on story logic and that key moments are pre‑destined to open for the protagonist (Kimizuka).

Key Dialogue (paraphrased):

Fuubi (frustrated): "No choice, we shall break it down?"

Kimizuka: "That sounds so wrong on so many levels."

Siesta (murmuring, pulling out a key from her pocket): "Maybe, with this."

Kimizuka: "I always thought it was just a cover for your lock‑picking skills?"

Siesta (cheerful smile): "Well, revealing the trick does take away some of the magic. However, this one key can always open the doors of the stories you're involved in."

(Click – the door swings open.)

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Narrative Intelligence: S - Understands that their reality follows story conventions.
Practical Tool Use: B - Possesses a functional key that works in this context.
Social Deception (Playful): B - Misled Kimizuka about the key's nature for humor.
Problem‑Solving: A - Chooses the elegant solution over brute force.
Meta‑Awareness: S - Understands the narrative logic governing their world.

Feat 5: Touching SYSTEM to Recover Memories – Calculated Risk and Trust in the Singularity

At the climax of the prologue, they reach the cracked, weakly glowing triangular pyramid SYSTEM. Kimizuka reaches out and touches it, recovering the memories of the Great Cataclysm's conclusion. Siesta does not hesitate or object – she trusts that this is the correct course. She also touches it together with him, sharing the experience and the burden.

Premises:

1. Memory Storage Premise: SYSTEM / Akashic Records holds the world's erased memories. The three holy relics were fragments; SYSTEM is the master database containing the complete records.
2. Previous Experience Premise: Kimizuka has touched holy relics before and recovered lost memories. Each touch caused nausea and sensory overload but was ultimately beneficial. The pattern is established.
3. Holy Relics Premise: They have already used three holy relics to recover partial memories. The relics fit into a stone slab, which opened the door to SYSTEM. This is the final step.
4. Singularity Premise: Kimizuka's nature as the Singularity allows him to interface with SYSTEM without being destroyed. He is the only one who can safely access the records.
5. Necessity Premise: To understand the ongoing Great Cataclysm (Yggdrasil spread, world anomalies, memory gaps), they need the full memory of the final battle with Abel.
6. Risk Premise: Touching SYSTEM could have side effects – pain, memory overload, or physical harm. The SYSTEM is damaged and cracked. But the alternative is worse.
7. Partnership Premise: They have always faced dangers together. She will not let him face this alone. Physical touch shares the burden.

Chain of Reasoning:

* Recognize that this is the same SYSTEM they fought over a year ago, now cracked and malfunctioning.
* Recall that Kimizuka's touch on the holy relics restored memories; this larger SYSTEM should have a similar function but on a grander scale.
* Understand that the missing memories are the key to understanding why the world is still in crisis.
* Assess the risk – the SYSTEM is damaged, but still glowing weakly. It is functional enough to use.
* Accept that the risk is necessary. Ignorance is not an option when the Great Cataclysm is ongoing.
* Let Kimizuka take the lead – he is the Singularity, so the interface is safer for him.
* When he hesitates, she does not argue – she simply overlaps her palm with his and touches the relic together.
* After the memory transfer, when he experiences nausea, she rubs his back – physical comfort.

Conclusion: Siesta makes a calculated decision to recover the lost memories, understanding that this is the only way to move forward. Her non‑verbal support demonstrates emotional intelligence and partnership.

Key Dialogue (paraphrased):

Kimizuka (reaching toward SYSTEM): "I have come to take back our story."

(He touches SYSTEM; it lights up brightly. Then: "And then I remembered. The continuation of the fight against Abel—the story conclusion.")

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Risk Assessment: A -Accepts potential danger for necessary information.
Trust in Partner: A -Defers to Kimizuka as the Singularity for the interface.
Strategic Memory Recovery: A - Knows the correct sequence (holy relics then SYSTEM).
Emotional Support: A - Provides non‑verbal comfort without patronizing.
Shared Burden: A - Touches SYSTEM together to share the experience.

EVENT 2: The Clinic Visit – Understanding Noches' Condition and Oliver's Role (Chapter 1: Seeds of Will Sprouting in May)

Context: Siesta, Kimizuka, and Nagisa visit a small clinic to see Noches, who was damaged in the battle at the card‑themed mansion. Oliver, a former child from Danny Bryant's shelter (Sun House), has repaired her physically but cannot restore her consciousness without Stephen's encrypted program.

Feat 6: Rapid Assessment of Oliver's Capabilities and Trustworthiness

When Oliver explains that Noches requires a custom encrypted program from Stephen, Siesta immediately understands the situation and Oliver's role. She recognizes the connection between Oliver and Danny Bryant's legacy, which establishes his trustworthiness. She also understands the technical barrier: Stephen's encryption is unbreakable without Stephen's direct involvement.

Premises:

1. Noches' Condition Premise: Her physical body is restored, but she remains in a "sleep state" because her consciousness program is encrypted. The damage was extensive, but Oliver has repaired the hardware.
2. Stephen's Role Premise: Stephen is the only one who knows the custom encryption for Noches. He is the original creator and the only one with the key.
3. Oliver's Background Premise: Oliver is one of the children from Sun House, the shelter Danny Bryant founded. He was the one who cracked Danny's vault with mental calculations faster than a quantum computer.
4. Danny's Legacy Premise: Danny Bryant was a detective and protector of children. Oliver's connection to Danny makes him a trusted ally.
5. Bruno's Warning Premise: Bruno had sensed the world's anomalies and prepared for the Great Cataclysm. Oliver's presence here is part of that preparation.

Chain of Reasoning:

* Observe Noches' condition – physically restored but unconscious.
* Hear Oliver's explanation – the encryption prevents activation.
* Recognize Oliver as a trusted ally – he is connected to Danny.
* Understand the technical barrier: Stephen's encryption is unbreakable without Stephen's direct involvement.
* Infer that Oliver's role is supportive, not central – he is like her assistant in this context.
* Conclude that they must wait for Stephen to wake up, or find an alternative.

Conclusion: Siesta trusts Oliver and accepts the waiting period, recognizing his role as a support figure.

Key Dialogue (paraphrased):

Oliver: "To fully activate her, we'll need a certain encrypted program. One custom‑designed by her original developer. If only Doctor Stephen were awake. Until then, I'm afraid there's nothing more I can do."

Kimizuka: "You've known Stephen for long?"

Oliver: "About a year now, I'd say. Doctor Stephen seemed to have something on his mind—like he wanted to leave behind everything he'd learned. In that sense, I guess you could call me his apprentice."

Siesta (later, to Kimizuka): "We've been in this state for over a year."

SCD Categories & Tier Ratings:

Trust Assessment: B - Recognizes Oliver as an ally through Danny's connection.
Problem Framing: A - Identifies Stephen as the only solution.
Character Reading: A - Understands Oliver's role as a support figure.
Patience: B - Accepts the waiting period without frustration.

EVENT 3: The Flight – Sharing Earphones and Team Reflection (Chapter 1: That Codename, I Knew It)

Context: After visiting Noches, the three board a flight. Nagisa and Siesta watch movies while Kimizuka's screen is broken. Siesta shares an earbud with him, creating a moment of intimacy and reflecting on their shared past. She also reveals she met with the former Swordmaster (a retired Tuner) to request cooperation.

Feat 7: Social Intelligence – Creating Intimacy Through Shared Experience

When Kimizuka complains of boredom (his screen is broken), Siesta offers her earbud, creating a "classic scene" of sharing earphones. This is not random – she deliberately recreates a moment of youthful intimacy they missed because they left school to travel the world.

Premises:

1. Kimizuka's Boredom Premise: His screen is broken, so he has nothing to watch.
2. Shared History Premise: They never had a normal school life – they were always traveling.
3. Desire for Connection Premise: Siesta wants to give Kimizuka something he missed.
4. Cultural Reference Premise: The "sharing earphones" scene is a classic romance trope.
5. Playful Dynamic Premise: Their relationship is built on teasing; she can offer intimacy while maintaining banter.

Chain of Reasoning:
